# Pyrotechny

<term>Pyrotechny</term> <note>original: "Pyrotechny", derived from the Greek words for "fire" and "art/craft." In the early modern period, this term referred broadly to the art of managing fire, encompassing chemistry, alchemy, metallurgy, and the creation of fireworks or military explosives.</note>

<summary>This is the title page for a treatise on "Pyrotechny," the early modern science and art of working with fire, including applications in chemistry and explosives.</summary>
<keywords>Pyrotechny, chemistry, fire-making, alchemy, early modern science</keywords>

# PYROTECHNY
<note>original: Pyrotechny; from the Greek roots for "fire" and "art," referring here to the practice of chemistry and alchemy.</note>
## Asserted and Illustrated,
<insert>4 shillings</insert> <note>This handwritten insertion likely indicates the original purchase price of the book.</note>
->To be the surest and safest means for<-
# ART’S TRIUMPH
<note>In this context, "Art" refers to the skilled application of alchemical and chemical knowledge.</note>
->OVER<-
# NATURE’S INFIRMITIES.
<note>Refers to physical diseases and the natural weaknesses of the human body.</note>
->BEING<-
A full and free discovery of the medicinal mysteries studiously concealed by all artists <note>Meaning other alchemists or physicians</note>, and only discoverable by fire.
->WITH<-
An *APPENDIX* concerning the nature, preparation, and virtue of several *specific medicaments* <note>Medicines intended to treat particular diseases.</note>, which are noble and substitutes <note>original: succedaneous; meaning acting as a replacement or backup.</note> to the Great *ARCANA* <note>original: Arcana; refers to the "Great Secrets" or the most powerful, universal remedies sought by alchemists.</note>.
## By GEORGE STARKEY,
## Who is a *Philosopher* by FIRE.
->LONDON:<-
Printed by *R. Daniel*, for *Samuel Thomson*, at the *White Horse* in *St. Paul’s* Church-Yard, 1658.

<summary>This is the title page for George Starkey's 1658 treatise on "Pyrotechny," or alchemical chemistry, which he presents as the ultimate method for curing disease. Starkey, a famous colonial American alchemist, claims to reveal medical secrets previously hidden by other practitioners.</summary>
<keywords>George Starkey, Pyrotechny, alchemy, 17th-century medicine, Arcana, chemical philosophy, pharmaceutical history</keywords>

## READER,

Without further addition, for I am unwilling to miscall you <note>original: "miscal thee"</note>, as you are a stranger to me, and I do not know your proper titles or epithets <term>epithets: descriptive names or honorary titles</term>. But be whatever you wish—provided you are not a *satyr* <note>original: "Satyr"; here referring to a harsh, mocking, or malicious critic</note>—and I do not care. I would neither

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the prefatory letter ("An Epistle") from a friend of the author to the reader. The author's friend uses mythological metaphors involving Vulcan, the god of fire, to describe the book's contents—likely related to chemistry, alchemy, or metalworking.</meta>

...have the fire frighten you, nor have you burn your fingers, but have you understand that a due and proper use of that which is a bad master is to make it a good servant; when kept in discreet obedience, it can be profitable and delightful to you in a thousand ways. I appear here like a prologue speaker for a play by **Vulcan** <note>original: "Vulcan's"; the Roman god of fire and metalworking, suggesting the book deals with chemistry or alchemy</note>, whose apprentice I am—a kind of **Junior Cyclops** <note>original: "Junior Cyclops"; in mythology, the Cyclopes were the assistants who worked in Vulcan's forge</note>. My master bids me tell you bluntly—without bowing <term>original: "making a Leg"; a traditional formal bow made by drawing one leg back</term> or flattering your favor—that if you will have patience, he will show you enough entertainment; and (if you have the wit) he will show you how not to regret the twelve pence <note>original: "Twelve-pence"; the purchase price of the book</note> spent here for his sake. 

For I shall anticipate this much: you will find the lame leg cured, and **Vulcan** <note>In mythology, Vulcan was famously depicted as being lame or having a leg deformity</note> not only able to walk but run on both legs. He will run so nimbly that he needs no golden balls to catch his **Hippolyta** <note>The author likely conflates two myths here: Hippolyta was the Queen of the Amazons, but it was Atalanta who was distracted by golden balls during a race</note>, or a net for **Venus** <note>In myth, Vulcan used a finely-wrought net to trap his wife Venus and her lover Mars</note>. Instead, he quickly overtakes the fleeing **Daphne** and does more than **Apollo** could—namely <note>original: "viz."</note>, satisfy his own desires and your wishes. This, Reader, you shall be entertained with in

<meta>This page continues the prefatory letter to the reader. The speaker, acting as a "prologue" or herald, uses theatrical and biblical metaphors to describe the transition of knowledge from a deceased master ("Elijah") to a living successor ("Elisha").</meta>

->To the **Reader**<-

...the sequel, for which I now blow the trumpet. I also display a modest <note>original: "scurvy"; here meaning lowly or insignificant, a self-deprecating way to describe the introduction</note> kind of picture of the strange **water-fireworks** to be seen within. Without any trickery <term>original: "Jugling"; sleight of hand or deceptive magic</term>, I will show you the sea burning and the fish in a doubt whether they are roasted or boiled. Yet, when the cooking is finished, it proves to be the most wholesome dish in the world; and if you prove worthy to taste it, you will confess it is both food and medicine <term>original: "Physick"</term>, pleasant and profitable.

If you ask, "Who first completed these rarities?" I answer: a noble *German artist*. He was one (if I may reverently borrow the comparison) who, like another *Elijah* <note>In the Bible (2 Kings 2), the prophet Elijah was taken to heaven in a chariot of fire</note>, rode here with horses made entirely of fire. At length, having paved his chariot with love and gilded it with light, he drove directly into eternity. He left his mantle and a double portion of his spirit upon an *Elisha* <note>Elisha was Elijah’s student and successor; receiving the "mantle" or cloak symbolized the transfer of authority and knowledge</note> who is now traveling to and fro, encompassing the earth to destroy the works of that Destroyer <note>A reference to the Devil</note> who takes the same journey like a **roaring lion** seeking whom he may devour. And to complete the trium-

<page-num>iv</page-num>
<header>An EPISTLE</header>

...virate <note>original: "triumvirate"; a group of three, here likely referring to a trio of authoritative authors or healers</note>, I may present to you this our *Author*. Thus, you may—like *Nebuchadnezzar* <note>The Babylonian king who cast three men into a furnace for refusing to worship his idol, as told in the Book of Daniel</note>—behold the three Children walking in the fiery furnace. They chose that fire rather than falling down and worshiping the image which Prince *Galen* <note>original: "Gallen"; refers to Claudius Galenus, the ancient Greek physician whose traditional theories the author is likely challenging in favor of chemical medicine</note> has set up and commanded all his subjects to adore and bow their knee to. 

And furthermore (for the comparison is yours, even if it is not perfectly parallel <note>original: "fourfooted"; an idiom meaning a simile or metaphor that matches in every detail</note>), there is a fourth among them who is like the Son of **God**, that True and Divine Wisdom. Therefore, the fire has no power over them; instead, it is their pleasant garden and fresh walkways where they gather the sweetest flowers: Flowers of the *Sun*, the fair *Lunaria* <note>original: "Lunaria"; literally "moon-plant," often used in alchemy to refer to substances or plants with lunar properties</note>, Apples of *Paradise*, and fruit from the Tree of Life. They are, therefore, the declared enemies of Death and his armies of diseases, for the leaves of that tree are for the healing of the nations. 

And do not wonder, *Reader*, that I allude to *Eden*, the Garden of **God**, whose Guardian Angel stands sentinel with a naked, flaming sword; no man returns there except he who passes through that flaming...

<summary>The author uses the biblical story of the fiery furnace to metaphorically champion chemical physicians over traditional followers of Galen, suggesting their "divine wisdom" allows them to find healing remedies even within the "fire" of the laboratory. This page frames chemical medicine as a return to the restorative powers of the Tree of Life in Eden.</summary>

<keywords>Nebuchadnezzar, Galen, medical philosophy, alchemical metaphors, Tree of Life, Eden, divine wisdom, chemical medicine</keywords>

...flaming blade, and he who has passed through this fiery trial has freedom of access to the middle of the garden where (you know) **God** planted his Tree of Life <note>original: *Arborem Vitæ*</note>. But without fire, there is no cleansing <note>original: "Expiation"; in this context, the removal of impurities</note>, because there is no sacrifice, and consequently no service. For it is only given to him who overcomes to eat of the hidden manna <note>A reference to Revelation 2:17, symbolizing spiritual nourishment or hidden wisdom</note>.

But by now, perhaps I and this book are growing too hot for you to hold any longer, and you throw it away. But I pray you <note>original: "prethee"</note>, *Reader*, which is hotter: your ignorant, dark zeal, or that pleasant and sincere divider between the precious and the vile? That is, I ask, which is a consuming and devouring flame of hell, and which kindly heats and warms with blessed and divine love? Answer for yourself.

But wait, I have now climbed so high that I may be quite out of your sight; I might seem as small as nothing to you, and you value me accordingly. Well, I will tell you where I am, even in words that you can understand—if you know anything of *Aristotle*,

<page-num>vi</page-num>
<header>An EPISTLE</header>

Aristotle, you know that the Element of Fire is the highest. Hello there, (*Reader!*) There I am, and I will be bold with your Stagirite <note>original: "Stagirite"; a title for Aristotle, who was born in the Greek city of Stagira</note> and tell that gentleman that he is not speaking the truth—though I won't call him a liar directly, lest his defenders stab me in the back. For this fire burns, and it can roast as many animals as his worship <note>The author is being sarcastic toward Aristotle here</note> lists in his catalog <note>original: "Muster-book"</note> on that subject; it might even provide a feast sufficient to dine his great patron <note>Alexander the Great</note>, if he also had enough wine for that *Royal Drunkard* <note>original: "Royal Fudler"; a reference to Alexander the Great’s reputation for heavy drinking</note>.

This is what the sphere of fire will perform, and besides this, anything that the cooking fire <note>original: "Culinary"</note> does or can do. To speak plainly of these two in the words of Hermes Trismegistus <note>original: "Trismegist"</note>: "*That which is above is as that which is beneath.*" <note>The famous central maxim of Hermeticism from the Emerald Tablet</note> This is a most certain truth, and all for the operation of one and the same thing. So, if there is any difference, it is that the *cooking fire* serves all the purposes we use it for in our houses on Earth, while the *elementary fire* does the same for the *astrologers* in their houses of heaven. Now, therefore, (*Reader*) I advise you to wipe your eyes; I am closer to you than you thought. And

<summary>The author critiques Aristotle's view of the "Element of Fire," arguing that celestial and terrestrial fires are essentially the same. He invokes the Hermetic principle "As above, so below" to explain the connection between the physical and spiritual realms.</summary>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's address to the reader, moving from the physical properties of fire to its spiritual and alchemical significance.</meta>

<page-num>vii</page-num>
<header>to the READER.</header>

To remove your prejudice <note>original: "Prepossession"</note>, I declare to you that I am no *Persian idolater*; fire is not my deity. Yet, everything it speaks is an oracle; there is no ambiguity <note>original: "Amphibology"</note> or double-meaning in its expression. No, it is the most honest being in the world; it cannot tolerate any masks or coverings whatsoever, but pulls them all off to reveal the naked presence of everything. And woe to the hypocrite (who is nothing but outward appearances <note>original: "nothing but Cloaths"</note>) who falls into it! Everything that lies safely in its heart is gold, and you are happy if, by your service, you gain enough favor with it to share in its embrace <note>original: "partake of its Bosom"</note>. 

It has no equal anywhere except for water. *Lady Water* <note>original: "Mrs. Aqua"</note> is its *Eve*, and he is a High Priest of Nature who can join their hands and hearts—who can reconcile the great hostility between them and make them embrace. Their offspring is always wonderful and is named *Wisdom, Length of Days, Lasting Riches, and True and Substantial Honor*. *Fire-Water* <note>original: "Ignis-Aqua"</note> is a marriage from which we may expect more of a universal peace than the Pope can from a marriage between the Prince of Spain <note>original: "Son of Spain"</note> and the

<meta>catchword: Daugh-</meta>

<summary>The author explains the alchemical purity of fire, which reveals the truth by stripping away all deceptive "masks." He describes the "High Priest of Nature" as one who can unite the opposing elements of fire and water to achieve wisdom and lasting wealth.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, fire, water, Ignis-Aqua, High Priest of Nature, truth, symbolism, elemental marriage</keywords>

into your contemplations and leave you:
Consider then the strange nature of that thing which can never be fed too much; see what a symbol of infinity lies in a small spark. Here give your thoughts freedom, and when you retire, begin again to think: where does this same thing called *FIRE* come from, and where does it go? What is it, and what does it do? When you have completed that thought, step forward again like this: Why does the *Almighty Chemist* <note>The author refers to God as the ultimate practitioner of alchemy.</note> promise to end the world’s story <note>original: "Epilogue the World"</note> with *fire*? Why did he once wash it with water, and will not now cleanse it again except with fire? Why are *Fire* and *Water* the parents of all visible beings—bringing them forth and raising them up—and yet at last turn into *cannibals* and eat their own children? These and a thousand similar questions you may look into without being accused <note>original: "Charge"</note> of being overly curious <note>In this context, "curious" implies prying into forbidden or dangerous secrets; the author argues this study is legitimate.</note>.

Attend, therefore, to your Master, and *Listen to the voice of the fire* <note>original: "Audi Ignis vocem"</note>, and do not be childish in your understanding <note>original: "Apprehensions"</note>. Do not be a dull and unintelligent listener <note>original: "Auditor"</note> at the lectures read to you in the *Pyrotechnic School* <note>original: "Schola Pyrotechnicâ"; a reference to the study of alchemy and the chemical properties of fire.</note>. Do not listen to the cry of the common people <note>original: "the Vulgar"</note>; they are like a wild beast that cannot endure the fire, nor come near it. Instead, call this world a desert—a forest of lions, bears, and tigers—and in this sense, it has but one nature: it is entirely an *Africa* <note>In the 17th century, "Africa" was frequently used as a metaphor for a wild, dangerous, and unexplored wilderness.</note>. ->**TO THE**<-

## Honorable, Virtuous,

->**AND MOST**<-

## Accomplished GENTLEMAN,

# ROBERT BOYLE, Esq;

## My very Good FRIEND;

->**ALL**<-

## Temporal and Eternal HAPPINESS be Multiplied and Continued.

---

**SIR,**

SINCE it *was my good fortune, first through the introduction of our mutual friend,* Dr. Robert Child <note>Robert Child (1613–1654) was a prominent physician and agricultural reformer who was part of Robert Boyle's intellectual circle.</note>, *(whose memory I honor, as he was a most learned and talented* <note>original: "ingenuous"—in the 17th century, this often meant high-minded, noble, or intellectually gifted</note> *man), to meet you* <note>original: "to Kiss your Honour's Hand"—a formal expression for being introduced to or paying respects to a person of higher social standing</note>, *your love toward me has always remained so genuine and constant that if I did not acknowledge it to the best of my power, I would worthily deserve to be marked with infamy;*

<meta>catchword: but</meta>

<summary>This page serves as the formal dedication of the work to the celebrated scientist Robert Boyle, with the author acknowledging their mutual friend, Dr. Robert Child, as the person who first introduced them.</summary>
<keywords>Robert Boyle, Robert Child, dedication, friendship, 17th century, gratitude</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Robert Boyle, transitioning from the author's personal gratitude for their friendship to an acknowledgment of Boyle’s immense scientific reputation.</meta>

*But yet, if I were to name your friendship as the sole cause of this dedication, I would be very unjust both to you and to the truth. Even if you were a complete stranger to me, your own merit—more than that of any other person I know in England—would justify this dedication. You are one to whom the God of Nature has been so remarkably favorable as to reveal many of nature's most secret operations, including their causes and workings, which remain unknown to many learned men. To you, therefore, I present these, my <term>pyrotechnical lucubrations</term> <note>original: "Pyrotechnical Lucubrations"—meaning scholarly writings or studies produced by candlelight, specifically concerning "pyrotechny" or chemistry/alchemy involving fire.</note>, just as an ordinary painter might present a sketch for the judgment of <note>Apelles of Kos was a renowned painter of ancient Greece, used here as a symbol of the ultimate expert critic.</note> Apelles. In doing so, I acknowledge your worth and also remove any suspicion of fraud from the things I write; for since they are humbly laid at the feet of such a capable judge of their truth, I only ask for your acceptance based on their actual value and reality. I entreat you, then, to receive these lines kindly—not as I wish they were, but as the simplicity of my understanding could produce. I assure you that, although they are plainly written, they contain real experiments which will be very acceptable both to you and to all who delight in searching for the mysteries of nature. I am confident that these operations will, in future ages, bring praise to the school in which I have been a <term>painful</term> <note>original: "Painful"—in this context, it means "painstaking," diligent, or hardworking.</note> though unworthy student.*

*As for myself, I do not need to make any apology to you, as I am sufficiently known to you; and your kindness and nobility make you very ready to excuse any failings you may discover in me. Now, since I prefer plainness and truth over the decoration of words when writing to such a friend, I have no doubt that it will be more acceptable to you. Let others judge*

<summary>In this portion of the dedication, the author praises Robert Boyle's deep understanding of natural science and chemistry, presenting his work for Boyle's expert judgment while emphasizing practical experiments over flowery language.</summary>

...my rudeness as they please. I would not be so bold as to place this dedication before the first part of this treatise, which is **defensive** and somewhat sharp against the abuses of the **Galenists** <note>The Galenists were medical practitioners who followed the ancient theories of Galen, emphasizing the balance of bodily "humors." They were often fierce rivals of the new "chemical" doctors.</note>. I did this because I did not want to involve Your Honor in any of my quarrels; therefore, I leave that part to stand or fall on its own, according to the weight or weakness of its reasons and arguments. 

The reason for that sharpness was the rudeness of the **Galenical party** in insulting and reviling this noble art in general, and **Helmont** <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644) was a pioneering chemist and physician whose work laid the foundations for modern chemistry.</note> in particular (who is a well-deserved favorite of both Your Honor and myself). I formerly considered him my **chemical evangelist**, but I now believe him—not because I am merely convinced by his arguments and reasons, but because of **experimental confirmation** and **practical, visual demonstration**.

Indeed, I myself have received many rude insults from the common enemies of the worthy art and artists of **chemistry** and **pyrotechny** <note>original: "Pyrotechny"—the science of using fire, which in this context refers to chemical and alchemical laboratory work.</note> for that very reason. On these grounds, some might think I have written so sharply out of revenge. But although it is very true that **indignation produces verse** <note>original: *Parit indignatio versum*—a reference to the Roman poet Juvenal, suggesting that anger or resentment can drive one to write.</note>, I can truly affirm that I was not motivated to take up the **buckler** <note>original: "Buckler"—a small shield; here used metaphorically to mean entering into a defense or a fight.</note> because of a private grudge. Rather, I did so because I saw Truth itself insulted and despised in the persons of its most deserving champions, such as **Helmont** and **Paracelsus** <note>Paracelsus (1493–1541) was a Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of minerals and chemicals in medicine.</note>. 

I feared that if such insults passed without criticism, the common people—who cannot read either **Helmont** or **Paracelsus** because the foreign nature of the languages in which they wrote keeps them locked away from most of our **English nation**—might suffer. Since their lives are closely affected by the subjects these authors discuss, I put pen to paper. I would have otherwise chosen to remain hidden, but I undertook the defense of those whose...

# The Epistle Dedicatory.

*I admit that I am otherwise unworthy to recommend books.*
*Yet if **GOD**, who alone is wise, has chosen the humble*
*and foolish things of the world to shame the lofty*
*and wise, I hope no man of understanding will*

*inclined toward such a spirit of prejudice; rather,*
*being aware of my own lack of merit, I*
*cannot help but justify myself to others who*
*perhaps know me little or not at all. I am*

*more willing to overlook my imperfections*

# PYROTECHNY
## Asserted and Illustrated, etc.

---

### Chapter 1
### THE PREFACE.

Noble and worthy to be written in letters of gold is that saying of Cicero: "All the praise of virtue consists in action" <note>original: *Virtutis laus omnis in actione consistit*</note>. To this, the poet agrees:
> ->"To what purpose do I have fortune if I am not allowed to use it?" <note>original: *Quo mihi fortunam si non concesseris uti?*</note><-

What profit is there in detailed speculations <note>original: "curious Speculations"—in this context, "curious" means intricate, overly academic, or highly detailed theories that lack practical application.</note> that do not lead to real experiments? To what end does theory serve if it cannot be applied to practice?

As this holds true in all cases, it is even more true in *pyrotechny* <term>the art of chemistry, specifically the use of fire to transform substances</term>, of which the whole subject would be vain and useless unless its principles <note>original: "Theorems"</note> were demonstrable through practical work with the fire.

Therefore, just as I have in my previous treatise formally defended the cause of *chemical* medicine against the unjust insults of the *Galenists* <note>Galenists were traditional physicians who followed the ancient Greek teachings of Galen. They often clashed with "chymists" or iatrochemists like Starkey who used distilled minerals and chemicals.</note>, and have proved that their medicines are actually virulent and dangerous—the very charge they falsely level against *chemical* medicines—and have more—

<summary>George Starkey introduces his preface by arguing that scientific theory is worthless without practical experimentation. He frames "pyrotechny" (chemistry) as a discipline that must be proven by action and fire, while continuing his critique of traditional Galenic medicine.</summary>
<keywords>pyrotechny, Cicero, chemistry, alchemy, experimentation, theory vs practice, Galenists, chemical medicine</keywords>

<page-num>2</page-num>

# Pyrotechny Asserted

...moreover convinced them at large of their impotence and insufficiency. Now it is time to bring to the stage a medicinal system <note>original: "Apparatus"</note> that is, and appears to be, both safe and effective.

The only complaint <note>original: "whine"</note> of the Galenists <term>followers of the traditional medical system of Galen, which relied on balancing the four "humors" using plant-based medicines</term> against Helmont <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644), a Flemish chemist and physician who championed the use of chemical remedies over traditional herbal ones</note> that I have ever heard, is and has been that he tears down but does not build up; that he labors to overthrow the established method of medicine but does not introduce a better one.

No honest "Son of Art" <term>a traditional term for an alchemist or a practitioner of the "Hermetic" arts who has gained true insight into nature</term> can fail to admit how far that noble philosopher has proceeded in the discovery of the true medicinal art, and everyone has cause to bless God for it.

But those who complain in this way want to be treated like infants by their nurses—to have their food not only prepared and provided, but also chewed and pre-masticated <note>original: "præmaſticated"; already chewed for them</note> for them. This is an unreasonable thing to expect and a foolish thing to demand.

My advice to such people is that they should sincerely take to heart the great responsibility placed upon those who care for human lives, and consider the great account they must give on the Day of Judgment. Knowing the terror of that day, they might cease from greed, laziness, and self-conceited pride. By acknowledging their ignorance, they should endeavor to attain true skill and knowledge through those ways and means which God has appointed.

For our books and writings are only like prods and spurs <note>original: "Goads and Pricks"</note>; they serve as encouragement to all who are studious and diligent. Each person, for his own part, should endeavor to profit through his own labor and industry, as far as the Almighty shall be pleased to assist him.

Nor do we, as in other arts, write with the intent that our writings may be understood indiscriminately <note>original: "promiscuously"; meaning understood by everyone regardless of their preparation or character</note>...

<summary>Starkey defends the chemist Van Helmont against critics who claim he only destroys old medical traditions without providing new ones, arguing that practitioners must work hard to understand new truths rather than expecting to be "spoon-fed."</summary>
<keywords>Jan Baptist van Helmont, Galenists, Son of Art, medical ethics, pharmaceutical preparation, iatrochemistry</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page completes the sentence started on page 2, finishing the thought that practitioners should strive for knowledge through diligent study.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>

<header>and Illustrated.</header>

...studiously by every reader. This is a characteristic peculiar to this art and is commanded in the Gospel: that pearls should not be cast before swine. For this reason, we write in a way that is not meant to be easily understood.

To what end, then, do we trouble ourselves and others—some arrogant Galenist <term>a follower of the traditional medical system of Galen, which relied on balancing the four "humors"</term> might object—if we do not intend to be understood? He will cite the example of a man who, encountering a book he could not understand, threw it away with this farewell: "What do I care for an author who does not wish to be understood?" <note>original Latin: "Quid mihi cum Autore qui non vult intelligi?"</note>

I answer that theoretical <note>original: "speculative"</note> and practical doctrines are very different. The one informs the judgment regarding the reasoning behind things; the other directs the hand in the physical <note>original: "Mechanical"</note> practice.

Now, as for the theoretical part, our demonstrations are most plain, true, and evident, as they are drawn from and grounded upon the very nature of the things we treat. Therefore, regarding the existence and effectiveness of our medicines, I know of nothing that could be desired that is not already fully and clearly available in the works of Helmont <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644), a pioneer of chemical medicine</note>, Paracelsus <note>The 16th-century Swiss physician who championed the use of minerals and chemicals in medicine</note>, and others.

But regarding the practical part, we should be excused if we write only to be understood by "Sons of Art" <term>a traditional term for practitioners of alchemy and chemical philosophy who have been initiated into its secrets</term>. This understanding is reached not by simple reading, but by serious meditation and unwearied prayer to God, joined with the tireless effort of searching "in the fire" <note>referring to laboratory work and chemical experimentation using heat</note>, which (under God) is the main key to unlocking all our mysteries.

If these things are attended to, our books may then serve profitably as guides <note>original: "Sea-marks"</note> to those who travel through these practices. By pondering our words, a practitioner may know if his operations are true or not; and if they are not, he may see where his error lies and how to correct it.

# CHAPTER II.

## Of the Art of Pyrotechny in General.

This art, called by some *Chemistry* <note>original: *Chemeia*</note>, or the Art of Separation, and by some *Alchemy* <note>original: *Alchemeia*</note>, or *Al-chemeia* <note>original Greek: Αλ-χημεια</note>—that is, the art of separating salts—is by us called *Pyrotechny* <term>the art of using and governing fire for chemical purposes</term>, or the art of governing and using the fire; this is because fire is the principal agent in our work.

All other agents <term>substances or forces that cause a chemical reaction</term> are either specific <note>original: "singular"</note>—serving only for certain tasks, such as spirit of wine <note>ethanol or high-proof alcohol</note>, vinegar, and so on—or universal, serving for the total dissolution of all bodies, such as the liquid *Alkahest* <term>a hypothetical universal solvent sought by alchemists</term>. Yet even these are neither created nor used except by means of fire. Therefore, fire is the most universal agent in our work, even if it is not the most powerful; it is the most widely used, though not of the greatest effectiveness.

For we possess a liquid that will dissolve and destroy what fire cannot; and what remains stable <note>original: "fixt and constant"</note> within the fire will, in our liquid, be dissolved, turned into vapor <note>original: "volatized"</note>, and separated into its basic elements. However, this is our hidden name, or secret <note>original: *Diploma*</note>, which is not to be shared. But our external agent (the fire) is visible and of the most universal use. It is our essential tool <note>original Latin: *sine quo non*</note> by which we take our name, and we rejoice with Helmont <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644), a pioneer of chemical medicine</note> to be called "Philosophers by Fire" <note>original Latin: *per Ignem Phylosaphi*</note> rather than to be "created" [as doctors] without it by the academic schools.

The Art of *Pyrotechny* then (in brief), is nothing other than knowing how, by the help of Vulcan <note>the Roman god of fire, used here as a metaphor for the furnace or heat</note>, to

<meta>catchword: un-</meta>

unlock Nature's secrets, which are contained <note>original: "shut up"</note> within vegetable, animal, and mineral substances <term>concretes: physical matter or compounded substances</term>; and to raise them to the peak of their perfection. This is achieved through hidden and secret agents <term>substances or forces that cause a chemical reaction</term> prepared from nature with the help of fire and the artist’s skill.

Therefore, since our agents themselves owe their very existence <note>original: "formal being"</note> to the preparation of fire, fire—as the more universal agent—gives its name to our art, which is called *Pyrotechny* <note>meaning 'fire-craft'</note> after the fire.

## CHAPTER III.

->*That not everyone who claims to know this art*<-
->*should be considered a true artist.*<-

From what has been said, it can easily be concluded that not everyone who claims a share in this art should be considered its rightful heir. For it is not everyone who can build a fire or a furnace who should be called a *philosopher by fire* <note>original Latin: *Philosophus per Ignem*</note>—that is, an *adept* <term>one who has mastered the secret knowledge of alchemy</term>—but only the person who knows how to work with fire according to the true principles of nature. 

Just as not everyone who can provide themselves with a good pen, a clean inkwell <note>original: "inkhorn"</note>, and fine paper can be judged a good scribe; nor is anyone who can buy a good sword to be counted among expert fencers. Rather, it is only those who can use these tools with skill and dexterity. Right

<meta>catchword: for</meta>

<summary>This page defines Pyrotechny as the art of using fire to perfect natural substances and argues that true mastery requires understanding nature's principles, not just owning tools or equipment.</summary>
<keywords>Pyrotechny, alchemical agents, philosopher by fire, adept, nature's secrets, chemical tools</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter III, emphasizing that physical tools and laboratory success do not necessarily make one a true master of chemical philosophy. The author intends to distinguish true alchemists from mere "mimics."</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
<header>Pyrotechny Asserted</header>

So it is in this case. Suitable furnaces and proper vessels are so necessary to this work that without them, nothing can be done; yet many people may provide themselves with these external instruments who are still as blind as moles and as stupid as asses when it comes to philosophy.

Nor is everyone who can perform many subtle operations with fire an *adept* <note>original Latin: *Adeptus*; one who has mastered the secret knowledge of alchemy</note>. For the works of God are various and wonderful, and as any subject is handled differently, it will produce different effects. Although these effects may seem glorious to the eye, they may actually be trivial in value. Yet their inventors—who, through ignorance, admire their value at a high rate—talk foolishly <note>original: "prate"</note> and make promises about things they do not understand themselves. This leads, in the end, to the disrepute not only of themselves, but of their art.

Therefore, so that true art may not be condemned because of these pretenders—who are not artists, but at best are mimics and apes of the true Sons of Art <term>a traditional term for practitioners of alchemy</term>—it will be useful to describe <note>original: "decipher"</note> some of them and show them in their true colors <note>original: "paint them in their Colors"</note>. In this way, they may be known and regarded for what they are, and not for what they are not, nor ever were.

# CHAPTER 4.

## *Concerning Erroneous Practitioners in Particular.*

And first, in this investigation, I encounter those who, having obtained some philosophical treatise, immediately set their minds on fire. They cannot rest until they begin the practical work, attempting to bring to life what they have conceived in their imaginations.

These individuals mostly aim for the "Golden Fleece" <note>original: *Golden Fleece*; a classical reference used by alchemists to describe the successful creation of the Philosopher’s Stone</note>. They spend all they have and exhaust themselves for this goal. They believe whatever they read and let their imaginations run wild, considering nothing worth their time except the mastery of Hermes <note>original: *Hermes*; referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical father of alchemy</note>. They think everything else is a waste of effort unless it is the Great Elixir <note>The legendary substance thought to transmute base metals and prolong life</note>. They believe no true fortune can befall them unless they happen to stumble upon that fateful <note>original: "unhappy"—likely used here in the archaic sense of "fateful" or "ill-omened"</note> Stone.

To avoid appearing ridiculous, they try to hide their true goal as much as they can. They pretend to be searching for some exceptionally "Noble Medicine." They pursue this with such vigor so that, when it is finished, they might cure all diseases—though they really hope in that time to cure the "diseases" of their own empty wallets <note>original: "Maladies of their Purse"</note>. As for the actual care of the sick, they intend to leave that to others.

At their start, these "philosophers" usually obsess over one specific substance or another. One dotes on rainwater, another on May dew, a third on native sulfur <note>original: "Sulphur vive"</note>, a fourth on mercury <term>☿</term>, a fifth on iron <term>♂</term>, a sixth on tartar <note>A substance deposited during the fermentation of wine, often used in early chemistry</note>, a 

<meta>catchword: seventh</meta>

<summary>This page identifies the first type of "erroneous operator": the greedy amateur who obsessively pursues the Philosopher's Stone to fix their finances while pretending to seek medical cures.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Golden Fleece, Hermes Trismegistus, Philosopher's Stone, Great Elixir, greed, rainwater, May dew, sulfur, mercury, iron, tartar</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "erroneous practitioners" or failed alchemists. It describes how they fruitlessly jump from one material to another in search of the Philosopher's Stone and, failing that, often turn to medical fraud to recoup their losses.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>Pyrotechny Asserted</header>

...seventh on *Gold*, an eighth on the *Nitre* <note>original: *Nitre*; saltpeter or a mineral salt believed to contain the "spirit" of the world</note> of the *Earth*. On these subjects, they imagine strange, *imaginary* operations for themselves. Every time they fail at these, they are always ready to start over again. When they do not find what they are looking for, they treat what they actually *do* find as being worth nothing at all. Thus, they run from subject to subject, working skillfully but toward no real end. Because they fail to hit their intended target—which, given the material they are handling, is perhaps foolish <note>original: "dotage"</note> to even imagine—they never stop to observe or seek what *God* and *Nature* have actually placed in that substance. Instead, as soon as many unsuccessful trials have convinced them that the *Philosopher's Stone* is not there, they immediately set themselves to another search.

At best, the knowledge of these men is only negative: they can tell you that the *Philosopher's Stone* is not there, but they have no idea what is actually contained in those substances, because they never attempted to find out.

Among this kind of "philosophers," there is another sort who are just as foolish as the first, but far worse.

After spending their time and money for a long season on these unprofitable studies, and at last having neither money to live on nor an honest trade to support themselves, they turn to pure deception <note>original: "Imposture"</note>. They promote a substance as a cure for all diseases of men which could never cure any "disease" in metals <note>Alchemists often described base metals as "diseased" and the process of turning them into gold as "curing" them</note>. For example, one of them takes the bones of long-buried men as his universal material. From them, he distills a foul-smelling liquid and refines <term>sublimes</term> a volatile, ammonia-like salt <note>original: "urinous Salt"; likely ammonium carbonate, which has a sharp, pungent odor</note>. This is the same result that deer antlers <note>original: "Harts-horn"</note> or any other bones will produce, especially those that have been rotting for a long time. Yet, he promotes this as a Universal Medicine, and

<summary>This page critiques alchemists who waste their lives pursuing the Philosopher's Stone in the wrong materials and eventually turn to selling fraudulent "universal medicines" made from common substances like old bones.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, gold, nitre, Philosopher's Stone, medical fraud, distillation, harts-horn, chemical operations</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of fraudulent alchemists and "chemical artists" who promote false cures. The previous page ended by describing a person who sells a worthless substance as a universal medicine.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>
<header>and Illustrated.</header>

[He] sells it at the price of gold, attributing to it—out of a vain confidence—whatever the Philosophers have ascribed to their "Great Elixir" <note>The Great Elixir, or Philosopher’s Stone, was believed to cure all diseases and prolong life.</note>.

Another man takes wood soot as his subject. He promises that by the heat of a water bath, he can refine <note>original: "decoct"</note> it into the Great Elixir. Presuming this soot to be the "true subject" of alchemy, he distills from it a water filled with a volatile, ammonia-like salt and a foul-smelling oil. After distilling it two or three times, he calls it his **Fire of Life** <note>original: *Ignis Vitæ*</note>, recommends it against all diseases, and prices it at half the value of gold.

These follies <note>original: "dotages"</note> are so obvious that simply naming them is enough to disprove them. Therefore, I shall leave these "dregs" of chemical artists aside, as they deserve to be marked with a black coal <note>This is a reference to the Latin phrase *nigro carbone notare*, meaning to mark something as bad or unlucky.</note>.

There are others who, having tried the ineffective common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> medicines of the day, turn to mineral remedies and chemical preparations. At first, they take great pleasure in preparing these substances and perhaps find some greater success with them than with ordinary drugs. Immediately, they begin writing about their "discoveries" so they may appear very learned. They will compile a large volume of preparations—some perhaps only taken on hearsay, many only imagined in their own minds, but never actually tested. This is a very great dishonesty. It is very harmful to those who first established the reputation of this art, especially when these writers praise dangerous poisons as "choice medicines." They recommend substances such as **Turpeth** <note>original: *Turbith*; specifically Turpeth Mineral, a harsh, yellow mercury-based chemical used as a purgative</note> and other preparations of **Mercury** <note>original symbol: ☿</note>, along with many preparations of **Antimony** <note>original symbol: ♁</note>. These should not be considered medicines, nor are they remedies fit for an honest man to use.

These types of men have brought into the apothecary shops a great variety of experimental <note>original: "Empyrical"</note> preparations,

<header>Pyrotechny Asserted</header>

...which for the sake of gain are daily more and more adulterated. Because of this, *chemistry* <note>original: "Chymistry"</note> had almost been brought into disgrace, had not some true Sons of Art <note>A traditional term for dedicated and legitimate practitioners of alchemy and chemistry.</note> appeared in every age to pull the mask <note>original: "Vizard"</note> off these pretenders. They show them for what they truly are: empty bubbles making a great show without any reality; clouds without water, unworthy of being considered what they boast themselves to be—for they are anything but true philosophers.

## CHAPTER 5 <note>original: "CAP. V."</note>
### *Of the true Sons of Pyrotechny <note>Pyrotechny: Literally "the art of fire," a term used in this period to describe chemistry or alchemy.</note>.*

Whoever, then, desires to be a true Son of Art must resolve to give himself up wholly to it. The pursuit of this art, second only to the service of God, ought to be his chief and main goal.

He must join prayer to God with serious meditation and diligent industry, for this is the way to attain true knowledge. His goal must be charity toward the sick and weak, which God will bless. But he who searches after these mysteries out of a greedy mind and a desire for vain glory will often fall short of his desires. Therefore, true *medicine* <note>In this context, "medicine" refers to the entire science of healing and the chemical preparation of remedies.</note> is a serious, secret, and sacred art that requires the whole man. Just as it is to be sought for charitable ends, so is it to be employed only with the design to glorify God by doing good.

<summary>The author warns against fraudulent chemists who discredit the field and establishes that a true "Son of Art" must be motivated by piety, charity, and total dedication rather than greed.</summary>

There are therefore several obstacles to true knowledge, which it is good that everyone who will give himself up to the Art <note>The "Art" refers to the practice of alchemy or chemistry.</note> be advised of, so that he may avoid and shun them; and these they are:

First of all, **Neglect of God**: when the artist does not seriously implore God’s blessing, how can he expect to be prosperous in his search after these mysteries of nature? Since every good gift is from above, coming down from the Father of Lights, it is not the reading of books, nor is it painful search in the fire <note>original: "Search in the Fire." This refers to laboratory work involving furnaces and chemical reactions.</note> that can do any good; only the blessing of the Almighty, which must be implored by earnest and daily prayer.

Secondly, **Vicious Living and wicked conversation** <note>In this context, "conversation" refers to one’s general conduct or way of life.</note>: this will make all a man’s endeavors unfruitful. Indeed, this hinders a man from an industrious search; for he who is once infected with vice will never seriously, as he ought, apply his mind to ingenuity <note>original: "Ingenuity." Here meaning creative intelligence or scientific skill.</note>.

Thirdly, **Idleness and Laziness**: when a man would gladly reap a plentiful harvest, but neglects the breaking up of his ground and the sowing of his seed. This is the fault of many, who if they cannot attain a thing by reading, or by a single attempt, are discouraged and give up.

Fourthly, **Pride and Conceitedness**: those who think that all knowledge is already with them, and if for a short time they have labored in this inquiry, they immediately presume themselves to be nothing inferior to Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the Hermetic corpus and the foundations of alchemy are attributed.</note> or Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine.</note>. Of these, the saying of Seneca <note>original: "Senica." Lucius Annaeus Seneca the Younger, Roman Stoic philosopher.</note> may be proven true: *Many might have attained virtue, if they had not thought they had already attained it.* <note>original: "Multi ad virtutem pervenire potuissent, nisi se putassent pervenisse."</note>

Lastly, **Covetousness**: when men are unwilling to venture their money in search of knowledge,

And finding a showy practice accompanied by large gains, without this expense and trouble, they embrace the cheapest and easiest path to profit. They consider money sweet, even if it is extracted from the ruins of families and the destruction of the sick, proving the maxim of an unworthy emperor: "The smell of gain is sweet, whatever the source." <note>original Latin: *Dulcis odor lucri, ex re qualibet.* A quote attributed to the Roman Emperor Vespasian regarding a tax on public toilets.</note>

My advice to all such people is to avoid meddling with these secrets; they are not meant for them. But assuming that you, *friendly reader*, are properly qualified—pious, diligent, humble, and charitably minded—I shall be your guide. I will share as much as one person may communicate to another without breaking the rules of philosophy, which require us to do our best to exclude all unworthy people from this art!

## CHAPTER VI.

### Concerning the Operations of Chemistry.

It is not my purpose or intent to begin with the theory of furnaces, nor is this treatise intended for those who do not know what they are. Instead, assuming you are already skilled in managing fire, I shall proceed to the intended subject. I leave those who do not know the mechanical principles of pyrotechny <note>original: "Pyrotechny." The art of using fire, specifically in chemical or alchemical operations.</note> to find another instructor.

To speak frankly, let me advise every young artist <note>original: "Artist." In this context, a practitioner of the chemical arts.</note> that he must use his own ingenuity <note>original: "Ingenie." Creative intelligence or technical skill.</note> to design the shapes of his furnaces, glassware, and pots to suit his intended work, and he should alter or vary them accordingly.

<meta>catchword: Some</meta>

<summary>This page warns against those who practice medicine solely for profit and introduces Chapter 6, where the author moves from moral qualifications to the practical use of furnaces and chemical equipment.</summary>
<keywords>greed, ethics in medicine, Vespasian, pyrotechny, furnace design, chemistry operations, ingenuity</keywords>

Some men can make their own furnaces, while others order workmen (who are available everywhere) to make them. This makes a practitioner <note>original: "Artist." In this context, someone skilled in the practical application of chemistry or alchemy.</note> neither better nor worse, for just as a fencer uses a sword that a cutler makes, so it may be in this case. However, to give directions regarding the design of furnaces so as to fit them for the intended work—this is absolutely required of a philosopher <note>original: "Philosopher." Meaning a "natural philosopher" or scientist dedicated to understanding the laws of nature.</note>.

Nothing stimulates invention more than a lack of the things one desires. In times of want, an ingenious man often finds shortcuts and manual procedures <note>original: "Encheiria's." From the Greek *encheirēsis*, meaning hand-operations, manipulations, or the practical steps of a chemical process.</note> as a temporary solution which he would never have otherwise considered.

But once instruments are prepared and materials provided, I advise all who undertake these discoveries not to spare any effort, study, or expense, always praying for divine assistance. Because the principles of this art are very hidden, a man should not grow weary even if he often fails. He may greatly improve these very errors to his advantage if he records all his operations and seriously ponders every result. Thus, by making mistakes, errors may be avoided; and to anyone who searches in this way, he will see that by asking, seeking, and knocking, he shall find, receive, and have the secrets and mysteries of nature opened to him.

Let his goal be the most noble medicines, yet let him pursue them in such a way that the lesser discoveries God reveals to him are not neglected. He should avoid imitating that lazy servant who buried his talent <note>original: "Talent." A reference to the biblical Parable of the Talents (Matthew 25), where a servant is punished for hiding a small sum of money rather than investing it for his master's profit.</note> because he only had one. Instead, by employing and improving what God bestows, let him expect a greater increase in his abilities, which God normally grants and gives to just and faithful servants.

# CHAPTER 7.

## *Regarding the different Levels of Medicines that are to be prepared by this Art.*

And so, by the good hand of **GOD**, I have been brought to survey the various medicines, which is indeed only the beginning of my intended task. Here, I ask for the reader's attention, and for my part, I promise candor and truth.

Therefore, medicines that are truly worthy of the name are of two sorts: either specific or universal. That is, they are either precisely applicable to a few diseases or universally powerful in all cases. This division will also allow for a subdivision, which I shall address in its proper place.

The way a desirable medicine achieves its effect is <term>diaphoretically</term> <note>promoting sweat</note> and <term>diuretically</term> <note>promoting urination</note>—by sweat and urine. As for emetics, purgatives, and the like, I leave those to people who approve of them. I would rather agree with Helmont <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644) was a Flemish chemist and physician who famously criticized the harsh purging methods of traditional Galenic medicine.</note>, who concludes thus: "May I be guilty before God if I do not advise that purgatives should be completely avoided" <note>original: "Reus sim coram Deo, nisi suasero, à purgantibus prorsus abstinendum."</note>.

As for the medicines that God has made for many (if not most or all) maladies in their simple form <note>original: "Simplicity." Referring to raw, unprocessed natural materials.</note>, that does not belong in this discussion; for this treatise concerns only those medicines <note>original: "Medicaments."</note> which are to be prepared by the fire.

And in this discovery, it is necessary only to rely

...to rely upon God's blessing and assistance, and to observe the effect of all operations most diligently. This is because there are no rules that can be set down beforehand to direct one in his search; for as *fire* is an <term>irregular agent</term> <note>original: "Heteroclite Agent," meaning something that deviates from standard rules or patterns.</note>, its products cannot be demonstrated by logic alone. By means of fire, *corrosive* substances are *sweetened*, and sweet things are made *corrosive*; *coagulated bodies* are *dissolved*, and *fluid substances* are *congealed*; *fixed things* are made *volatile*, and *volatile things* are *fixed*. 

In a word, the <term>raw substance</term> <note>original: "Concrete." In alchemy, this refers to a composite or solid natural body.</note> provides very little or no light by which to estimate the things it will produce. *Nature* in this work is most free in her operation and wonderful in her effects. Her footsteps are veiled with much obscurity, yet by the good hand of God, they can be found through industry.

## CHAPTER 8.

### *Regarding the Keys of this Art in General.*

The keys of this science are *congealing* and *dissolving*, *vaporizing* and *fixing*; all chemical operations tend toward these ends.

*Separation* and *digestion*, *fermentation* and <term>cohobation</term> <note>The process of repeatedly distilling a liquid from the same solid substance to increase its potency.</note> are all intended for this purpose: to make that fluid and subtle which was previously coarse, or to make that volatile which was previously fixed, and vice versa.

The primary means of all this (on *Nature’s* part) is *ferment*. On the *chemist’s* part, it is the application of appropriate *agents* and *subjects*, along with external heat according to the requirements of the specific task.

By these means, the hidden *power* <note>original: "Vertue"</note> of the *raw substance* is sought and made to appear openly, which is...

...so far beyond the <term>raw substance</term> <note>original: "Concrete." A composite or solid natural body.</note> in power as it was in its crude simplicity—just as the best wine is superior to the raw juice of grapes in excellence.

For although some things have their power restricted to their physical form, depending on the <term>ultimate life</term> <note>original: "Vita Ultima." The final or essential life-force of a substance.</note> of the subject (as is especially apparent in all things that work magnetically), yet truthfully, in the vast catalog of <term>natural ingredients</term> <note>original: "Simples." Herbs or single substances used for medicinal purposes.</note>, how few things are of use without previous preparation or correction? Either their gross physical bulk or their toxic harmfulness acts as a stubborn obstacle to their effective energy. As for this physical impediment, it is removed by dissolution and <term>vaporization</term> <note>original: "Volatization." The process of turning a substance into vapor.</note>. In these two operations, for the most part, all the toxicity of the subject is either removed or suspended.

For fire, as it cleanses that which was foul, so it ripens that which was raw; it <term>digests</term> <note>In alchemy, "digestion" refers to the process of exposing a substance to gentle, continuous heat to mature it.</note> what was poisonous and reverses its harmfulness, making it appear with new qualities. By means of fire, the potency of a substance is exalted and made a thousand times more noble.

But because fire alone is merely the destroyer of the <term>internal life-force</term> <note>original: "seminal Vertue." The inherent power or "seed" within a substance that allows it to act.</note>—and whatever it masters, it not only extinguishes that force but alters it notably into strange, mismatched mixtures in which that excellent <term>constitution</term> <note>original: "Crasis." The specific balanced mixture of qualities that gives a substance its unique character.</note> (of which we boast) cannot be seen—therefore, the <term>alchemists</term> <note>original: "Sons of Art."</note> have invented and prepared means for the preliminary dissolution of solid bodies. By these means, the bodies are opened so that the more noble parts may be severed from the ignoble and inactive ones, and thus their goal is attained.

<summary>This page explains that natural substances often require chemical preparation to remove their "gross corporality" or toxicity. While fire is a powerful tool for refining and "ripening" these materials, the author warns that fire alone can destroy a substance's internal life-force, necessitating specialized alchemical methods to properly "open" and separate noble elements from waste.</summary>

### CHAPTER IX.

## Of the Keys in Particular, and First of the Solvent <term>Alkahest</term> <note>original: "Liquor Alchahest." A hypothetical universal solvent capable of reducing any body to its primary matter.</note>.

I am now brought to the contemplation of a subject of wonder, which is indeed one of the most admirable secrets in nature. It is an <term>immortal entity</term> <note>original: "Immoral Ens." Likely an OCR error for "Immortal Ens," referring to a permanent, indestructible substance or being.</note>, incorruptible, and possessing the power to resolve all the <term>solid substances</term> <note>original: "Concretes." In alchemy, this refers to composite physical bodies or solid matter.</note> of the world into their first liquid matter, destroying their physical form and bringing them to <term>volatility</term> <note>The state of being easily vaporized or converted into spirit.</note>.

Its name was first given by Paracelsus in the German tongue, sounding like "all spirit" (*All-geist*), which may denote its nature. It is a spirit of such a <term>homogeneous</term> <note>original: "Homogeneal." Consisting of parts all of the same kind.</note> substance that it cannot be altered in nature, except only by its <term>equal</term> <note>original: "Compeer." A companion or equal; here, a substance of the same nature.</note>, by which it is altered and loses its power once joined and mixed with it.

I intend to speak very briefly of this here, having handled it sufficiently and clearly elsewhere in a specific treatise; nor shall I in this place repeat what is fully set down there.

Yet, because our intent here is to provide a short system of the whole <term>art of chemistry</term> <note>original: "Art of Pyrotechny." Literally the "art of fire," used by alchemists to describe the chemical transformation of matter through heat.</note>, which cannot be done without treating this solvent—since the most noble ends and eminent preparations are performed by means of it—it is necessary for us not to neglect such a worthy subject, lest our work appear (and quite justly) to be lame and imperfect.

This solvent, then, is nothing other than that fire, of

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the alchemical liquor (the Alkahest) and its transformative properties, comparing its power to that of a common flame.</meta>

<page-num>20</page-num>
<header>Pyrotechny Asserted</header>

By *striking* the *flint* and *steel* together, fire is made visible in its *sparks*. These sparks, when caught in *tinder*, are by an easy art (known to every *kitchen-maid*) multiplied into a *fire* as large as the *maker* of it pleases. At first it is but a *spark*, yet being cherished with *fuel* proper to it, it becomes in a short space so great a *flame* that it would be able to consume whatever is combustible in the whole *world*, if it were put into it.

So likewise, there is but one *liquor* comparable to *fire*, yet far more powerful and vehement than a common *flame*. For those things which will endure common fire without being conquered by it are, by this other *fire* <note>The author is referring to the alchemical solvent or "liquor" discussed in previous pages.</note>, destroyed and altered radically and fundamentally.

When this *fire* is distilled from any *metal* that is soft and imperfect, it leaves them, after the first or second time, in a meltable substance like wax. From this, the *sulfur* or *tincture* <note>In alchemy, the "tincture" is the soul or essential color/property of a substance.</note> is dissolvable in the best *spirit of wine* <note>High-proof alcohol or ethanol.</note>, and from the residue—after being kept for three days in a vaporizing heat—mercury <note>original: "☿"; the symbol for mercury or quicksilver.</note>, *quick and running*, may be separated. The same may be done with harder *metals*—even with perfect *metals*—over a longer time, by more frequent repetitions of pouring the liquid back and redistilling it <term>cohobations</term>.

But if this *fire* is once distilled from *common* mercury, it leaves it solidified and fixed, so that it will endure the <term>Test of Saturn</term> <note>A method of refining gold or silver by melting it with lead (associated with the planet Saturn).</note>. It is left porous like a pumice stone, but heavy like <term>Turbith Minerale</term> <note>A yellow, poisonous form of mercury sulfate.</note>. It becomes brittle and therefore easily ground into powder. When it is then redistilled with water made from the whites of eggs, it causes that distilled water to stink, but the mercury becomes the color of the best coral; for this reason, it is named <term>Arcanum Corallinum</term> <note>original: "Arcanum Corallinum"; Latin for "the Coral Secret," referring to red mercuric oxide.</note>.

If this *fire* is distilled from any *gem* or *stone* that has been finely powdered, it turns into a simple *salt* of equal weight to the *gem* or *stone*. *Pearls* it resolves into...

<summary>The author explains how the alchemical "fire" (a powerful liquor) can break down metals into their base components and transform mercury into a fixed, coral-colored substance.</summary>

...into a milk-like substance, which is their first essence <note>original: "Ens"; in alchemy, the *Ens* is the most basic, concentrated essence or "being" of a substance.</note>. This also applies to "crab's eyes" (as they are commonly called, though they are not actually eyes, but stones found in the head of the crab <note>These are gastroliths, calcium carbonate deposits found in crustaceans like crayfish and crabs, used historically in medicine as an antacid.</note>) and all plant-based "stones," such as peach stones, date stones, or the like.

In short, this liquor dissolves all plants, animals, and minerals into their first essence. In complex substances <note>original: "Concretes"</note> that contain diverse components <note>original: "Heterogeneities"</note>, it reveals and separates those components, making them distinct.

The advice of this ancient philosopher to all who have dedicated themselves to the Art <note>The "Art" refers to alchemy or the early chemical sciences.</note> is that they should strive with all their might to obtain this "fire," if their goal is to create noble medicines that go beyond the ordinary. Yet he concludes that this is such a difficult and high achievement that not every seeker, but only those chosen by God, shall master it. Therefore, he hopes that the "champions" (those who work industriously for this mastery) will not be discouraged by its difficulty. The challenge is made even greater by the strange obscurity of everyone who has written on this subject until now—primarily *Paracelsus* and his great commentator, *Van Helmont*.

I will discuss this hidden mystery in more detail, focusing on its effects and properties (specifically those which are unique to it and cannot be shared by other dissolving liquids). By doing so, the signs and markers by which it may be identified will be revealed. This will serve as an unparalleled guide and directory for any serious student of this Art, and will therefore be a very welcome work.

## Chapter 11.

### *Concerning the Virtue and Efficacy of this Liquor in greater detail.*

In the first place, it is relevant to our intended purpose to distinguish between this liquor and other substances between which there might be thought to be a great affinity. The misunderstanding of this has caused, does cause, and will cause many errors for those who have dedicated themselves to this discovery; by assuming uncertain foundations to work on, they proceed just as ridiculously in the pursuit of their proposed goal.

Among these, the first are those who blindly confuse this liquor with the <term>☿</term> <note>The symbol for Mercury; here referring to "Philosophical Mercury," a prime substance in alchemy.</note> of the philosophers. I know many of this sort who can by no means be convinced otherwise of this notion, though there can be none more absurd if weighed in the balance of sound reason.

For first, these two differ materially and substantially: the one being Mercury truly and properly so-called, and the other being a salt. It is therefore called the *Greater circular salt* <note>original: "Sal circulatus major"</note>, the *highest and most blessed of salts* <note>original: "Salium summus & felicissimus"</note>, and the *Liquor of Salt* <note>original: "Liquor Salis"</note>.

Secondly, they differ formally and essentially. The one is not only metallic but a metal—namely, a "Philosophical" metal. According to the philosopher, *In metals, through metals, metals are perfected* <note>original: "In metallis per metalla proficiuntur metalla"</note>; and according to the joint consent of all masters in that science, all the principles of the *Philosophical Elixir* are homogeneous and co-essential to one another. Therefore, they formally remain each...

<meta>catchword: each</meta>

<summary>The author distinguishes the alchemical "liquor" (Alkahest) from Philosophical Mercury, arguing that the former is essentially a salt rather than a metal. He emphasizes that confusing these two substances is a fundamental error that leads many alchemists astray.</summary>
<keywords>Alkahest, Philosophical Mercury, Sal Circulatus Major, alchemical principles, metallic essence, George Starkey, Helmontian chemistry</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between the liquor Alkahest and the "Mercury of the Philosophers," explaining how the latter functions as a "dry water" that becomes one with the metals it dissolves, unlike the Alkahest.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>
<header>and Illustrated.</header>

...each with the other, and are transmuted into each other's natures, so that the agents become passive and the patients active <note>In alchemy, the "agent" is the active force or solvent, while the "patient" is the substance being acted upon. Here, the author describes them merging completely.</note> during the progress of the Great Elixir.

And therefore the Philosophers' Water is called, "Dry water that does not wet the hands, nor moistens anything except that which is compatible with it in the homogeneity and identity of its matter" <note>original: "Aqua sicca non madefaciens manus, nec quicquam humectans, nisi quod conveniat sibi in materiæ homogeneitate atque identitate."</note>. Nor is there any difference allowed between perfect Gold and the <term>☿</term> <note>The symbol for Mercury; here referring to "Philosophical Mercury," the supposed prime matter of all metals.</note> of the *Magi* <note>The "wise men" or master alchemists of antiquity.</note>, except only this: that one is ripe and perfect, and the other is crude and imperfect. And *Artephius* <note>A famous alchemist, traditionally said to have lived for over a thousand years using the Elixir.</note>, to remove all doubt, says it is the <term>☿</term> of Saturnine <term>♁</term> <note>The symbol for Antimony, often called "Saturnine" because of its lead-like appearance and its role in "devouring" other metals during purification.</note>, in which no metal is dissolved or drowned except Gold; and he affirms this to be the only agent in the world for the Art.

Also, both he and *Trevisan* <note>Bernard Trevisan (1406–1490), a celebrated alchemist who spent his life seeking the Philosopher's Stone.</note> (to put this matter beyond all controversy) conclude and positively determine that for their work, no agent is useful which does not formally remain with the dissolved bodies, so as to become one thing with them—just as the moisture of the ground does with a grain of wheat that is dissolved in it. Therefore, they reject as "sophistications" <note>original: "Sophistications" - deceptive imitations or adulterated substances that do not perform the true work of alchemy.</note> all dissolving liquids that are not permanent with the bodies they dissolve, and which the dissolved bodies cannot re-congeal with themselves. This is so that the philosophical dissolution of the body causes, at the same time, a congelation <note>The process of a liquid thickening or becoming solid.</note> of the dissolving spirit, so that they may be joined together in an inseparable union. For more on this, read the *Secret Book* of Artephius. Also, see Count Trevisan’s book found in the first volume of *Theatrum Chemicum* <note>original: "Theatr. Chem." - The "Chemical Theater," a massive and influential compendium of alchemical writings.</note> and his letter to Thomas of Bologna <note>original: "Thomas of Bononia."</note>, found in the second volume of *Ars Aurifera* <note>original: "Ars Aurifera" (The Gold-making Art) - a famous collection of alchemical treatises first published in 1572.</note>.

Whereas on the contrary, the liquor *Alkahest* is a real water which will wet either the hand or anything else; it will join with any solid substances <note>original: "Concretes" - a term used in early science for any solid, compound mass of matter.</note>

...in the whole world. It does not merely moisten them, but resolves them and remains with them once dissolved. It distills over with them in a fire of the second degree in a sand bath, and yet it never radically mixes with anything. It remains separable from everything it dissolves, just as phlegm <note>In alchemy, "phlegm" refers to a watery, tasteless, and inactive byproduct of distillation.</note> is separable from the Oil <note>original: 🜖</note> of Vitriol <note>Sulfuric acid.</note>.

Similarly, although it dissolves gold, it does not stay with the gold once dissolved—a condition which is absolutely necessary in all "Generations" <note>The creation of the Philosopher's Stone or the transmutation of metals.</note>. Therefore, we shall briefly give the differences between the one and the other, as they appear in Matter, Form, and Operation.

The Philosophers' Mercury <note>original: ☿</note> is an antimonial, saturnine <note>Containing qualities of lead or antimony, which were thought to "cleanse" gold.</note> *Living Silver* <note>original: *Argent Vive*; another name for quicksilver.</note>. It is a middle substance, as clear as pure silver. (*Artephius* <note>The author likely refers to the 12th-century alchemist Artephius.</note>)

The Liquor Alkahest <note>original: Alchabest</note> is a salt of an exquisitely fiery nature, the likes of which is not found elsewhere in the world. It is neither mineral nor metallic, but is "circulated" <note>A process of continuous distillation in a closed vessel.</note> until it becomes a very spirit, which is called Algehest.

The Philosophers' Mercury <note>original: ☿</note> does not wet the hand, nor anything else that is not of its own metallic nature; nor will it join with anything else.

The Liquor Alkahest wets the hand, and everything else in the world besides. It will dissolve each thing according to its kind and reduce it to its primitive matter. It will mix with a substance when dissolved, just as a spirit mixes with a watery phlegm; however, because it is not "radically joined" <note>Fused at a fundamental, chemical level.</note>, it is therefore separable from all things that are dissolved by it.

In the Philosophers' Mercury <note>original: ☿</note>, gold—and gold only—is "drowned." After dissolution, the dissolver and the dissolved are united in an inseparable union, so that from both, one single substance is made.

In the Liquor Alkahest, gold and other metal-

Alkaline substances are dissolved, but it remains with none of them, nor is it altered by any of them.

To conclude, the solution made by the Mercury <term>original: ☿</term> of the philosophers is a process of generation. It does not separate the tincture <note>In alchemy, a "tincture" is the active soul or essence of a substance that carries its transformative properties.</note> from the mercurial <term>original: ☿al</term> part, but instead joins them more firmly to each other, so that both may grow up together into a substance that can be multiplied in its own kind.

But the solution made by the Liquor *Alkahest* works in a destructive way, extinguishing the "seed" in terms of its generative power. It divides the tincture from the mercury <term>original: ☿</term>, so that once they are severed, they can never be united again. This makes the tincture volatile; while this result is wonderful for medicine, it is completely alienated from its metallic nature and disposition.

To wrap up this discourse, then: we conclude that although both the Mercury <term>original: ☿</term> of the philosophers and this liquor are very noble secrets, they are so distinct from each other that one has no dependence on the other. In their matter, form, and operations, they are as different as can almost be imagined.

There are others who believe this liquor to be a mercurial water. (The author of the *Chemical Dictionary* is one of these; his words are: "Alkahest is Mercury excellently prepared against liver obstructions" <note>original: Alchahest, est ☿ optimè præparatus contra hepatis obstructiones</note>.) Some would have it made from vitriol <note>Sulfuric acid.</note>—specifically its spirit—mixed and circulated with the pure spirit of wine <note>High-proof alcohol or ethanol.</note>. Some would have it be a pure spirit of salt <note>Hydrochloric acid.</note>. This is not to mention the fools who would have it be an "Ethereal Spiritual Water" drawn out of the air and impregnated with an "esurine" <note>original: esurine; meaning sharp, acidic, or "hunger-inducing," referring to a corrosive salt.</note> salt. Others believe it to be a spirit of true nitre, which they distinguish from saltpeter. But, I presume,
