...fume, and they do not know where to find it themselves, or how to direct anyone else to find it.

For my part, I will willingly allow and permit everyone to follow their own judgment; nor do I care what men's opinions about it are.

This I know: that the subtleties which are often found in *speculative theory* prove to be nonsense <note>original: "Dotages"</note> in *practice*; my own experience has put this beyond question.

The noble *Helmont* <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644) was a pioneering chemist and physician who wrote extensively about the "Alkahest," a legendary universal solvent.</note> states positively: "*That in the whole world, just as there is but one fire, so there is but this one liquor, nor any other that has its qualities, which the Adepts know and can testify to.*" As a scholar, I believe this; but to speak honestly, I do not know how to prove it to myself, and yet I am certain that I know how to prepare the kind of liquor he describes.

He also affirms it to be of "most tedious preparation" <note>original: "Tediosissimæ præparationis"</note> in Chapter 9 of his book *On the Stone* <note>original: "de Lithiasi," a treatise on kidney and bladder stones</note>. In his chapter titled "*The Resolution of the Duelech*" <note>original: "Duelech resolutum." Helmont used the term "Duelech" to describe a specific type of stony deposit in the body.</note>, where he teaches the preparation of the *Ludus* <note>A type of stone or mineral (often septarian nodules) that Helmont believed could be dissolved into medicine.</note> into *Althobolzoim* <note>A specific chemical remedy or state of matter described by Helmont.</note>, he says: "*This is a most difficult work—not regarding the preparation of the Ludus, for that is done in two hours' time, but regarding the making of the Liquor Alkahest.*" For this, he says, the Adepts have a proof that goes beyond all demonstration.

I must seriously profess that this demonstrable proof is unknown to me. Yet, as I said, I know how to prepare such a liquor that shall do everything this great philosopher attributes to his. Whether mine and his are the same in every respect, I cannot affirm, nor can I deny; yet I hope to prepare the one I know in 50 <term>mercuries</term> <note>original symbol: ☿. In this context, it likely refers to "mercuries" as a unit of time or cycles of distillation; he may mean 50 days.</note>, and I would not be wrong if I said 40.

Yet that which I first prepared was done at

...uncertainty. Therefore, in making it, as I often erred, I presume I always took the longest and farthest way through the woods <note>original: "way to the Wood," an idiom for taking a difficult or indirect path</note>. Besides, expecting many errors, I worked on a large amount of material at once so that if one, two, or three trials should fail, I might have enough material to begin again.

Furthermore, since this was only a discovery, I did not make it my entire business but attended to other things I was familiar with at the same time. Yet, since I achieved my proposed goal, I reflected on my operations. If my calculation is not incorrect, I know many chemical <note>original: "Chymical"</note> preparations more tedious than this one—unless the "tediousness" of the work was accounted for by *Helmont* on a different basis than time. I rather believe that he meant the offensiveness <note>original: "noisomeness"</note> of the substance being worked on in its initial preparations. This may be much more unpleasant for one operator than another, depending on the way he proceeds in his work, the method he follows, or the instruments he uses; for there may be great variety in these, yet all tending toward the same end. But this is only an aside; I shall now come to the intended subject.

This is a heavy <note>original: "ponderous"</note> liquid, being indeed entirely *salt* without any watery *phlegm* <note>original: "Flegm"; in early chemistry, this referred to the watery, tasteless, and medicinal-less part of a substance</note>. It is completely volatile, being entirely a *spirit* without any physical body <note>original: "corporiety"</note> left in it. It has no prominent odor, for most things that give off a significant smell are of a very volatile nature or consist of many different components <note>original: "heterogeneities"</note>. It is not, therefore, volatile in the same way as spirit of wine <note>alcohol</note>, urine, or similar substances, which evaporate <note>original: "fly"</note> with the smallest amount of heat. Instead—like a heavy spirit that yields its phlegm first—when this liquor has dissolved any vegetable substance <note>original: "Vegetable Concrete"</note> and made it volatile, it will allow that substance to be entirely separated by the gentle heat of a *water bath* <note>original: "Balneum Mariæ," a vessel of hot water used to heat containers slowly and evenly</note>.

Thus, it is an immortal essence <note>original: "Ens," a Latin term for an entity or being; here it refers to a fundamental substance</note> whose power is not exhausted <note>original: "exantlated"</note> by repeatedly acting upon solid materials <note>original: "Concretes"</note>. Instead, it retains its unaltered vigor and has the power to dissolve bodies perpetually. It is subject to accident <note>original: "casualty"; meaning it can be lost or destroyed by physical mishap</note> but not to change—except only by its equal—and is therefore rightly esteemed by those who know it as an unparalleled mystery.

***

## CHAPTER 12.

### Of the medicines that can be prepared by this liquid, etc.

From what has been discovered concerning this liquid's wonderful nature, it may easily be imagined what a key this is to medicine <note>original: "Physick"</note> and natural philosophy for those who are masters of it.

To illustrate this, I do not need to emphasize the admirable medicinal power found in metals and mineral bodies, in gems, pearls, and animal or vegetable stones. For this liquid also dissolves plants into their primary liquid matter, separating all their diverse parts <note>original: "Heterogenities"</note> by different colors and distinct layers, one above another. In this dissolution, a small amount of liquid always settles in a distinct place, clearly distinguishable from the rest by its color; in this liquid, the fundamental essence <note>original: "Crasis," a term for the specific constitutional mixture of a substance</note> of the whole herb, tree, or seed resides.

<meta>catchword: In</meta>

<summary>The author describes the universal solvent as an "immortal essence" that can dissolve solid substances without losing its own potency. He argues this liquid is a vital tool for medicine because it can separate a plant or mineral into its constituent parts to isolate its core medicinal essence.</summary>

<keywords>Alkahest, solvent, Ens, Cucurbit, chemistry, distillation, pharmacology, Crasis, medicinal virtues, minerals, plants</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's explanation of how a universal solvent (the Alkahest) interacts with "concretes" (solid substances). It explains that while the solvent breaks down the substance into its fundamental parts, it actually enhances the medicinal power while removing toxins.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>
<header>and Illustrated.</header>

In this process of breaking down the solid substance <note>original: "retrogradation of the Concrete"</note> through dissolution, there is no loss of power <note>original: "Virtue"</note>. Instead, its strength is heightened many times over. The only change is that whatever poisonous quality <note>original: "virulency"</note> existed in the raw substance is completely destroyed by this operation, while all the specific medicinal properties <note>original: "Specifick Virtues"</note> found in the original solid substance are preserved.

I have no doubt that you will highly value these preparations in your mind and wish that you could create the same yourself. To tell the truth, they are excellent and very desirable, but

->*Everyone has their own desire, and no one lives by a single wish alone.* <note>original: "Velle suum cuique est, nec voto vivitur uno." A quote from the Roman satirist Persius.</note><-

If you desire this and are wise, you should also desire the means of achieving it—which is to set about the task with hard work <note>original: "industry"</note>. In this way, you will be able to break down all herbs into their liquid fundamental components <note>original: "Principles"</note> without any leftover sediment. One part of this will be oily and fatty <note>original: "unctious"</note>, especially when using trees, gums, seeds, and many roots. Another part will be watery <note>original: "aqueous"</note>, in which the volatile salt of the solid substance will be detectable by taste. 

You may circulate <note>a chemical process of continuous distillation and condensation in a closed vessel</note> the liquid with its own oil until it becomes an essential salt, which is indeed the primary essence <note>original: "Ens"</note> of the solid substance. However, if you want to complete the work in less time, perform your dissolutions with stronger heat. Distill your liquid along with the dissolved substance over an appropriate fire; in this way, the oily part will be completely transformed into a salty spirit <note>original: "sa-line Spirit"</note>. When distilled using a water bath, this spirit will come over in various colors. 

The vital essence <note>original: "Crasis," referring to the specific temperament or healthy constitution of a substance</note> will separate itself from the watery residue <note>original: "Flegm"</note>. You can distinguish it by its color, taste, and smell, as well as by the time it takes to pass over the head of the still <note>original: "Helm"</note>. Your original dissolving liquid will be left behind at the bottom, just as much in quantity and as effective in power as it was before.

Thus, from hellebore <note>a medicinal plant, though often toxic in its raw state</note>, a noble specific remedy can be made against gout and hypochondriac melancholy...

This solvent can produce remedies for tropical fevers <note>original: "Calentures"</note> and delirium <note>original: "Deliria's"</note> during fevers. From myrrh, aloes, and saffron, it creates an excellent medicine for chronic wasting fevers <note>original: "Antihectical Medicine"</note>, as well as for fainting spells <note>original: "Lypothymy's"</note>, loss of consciousness <note>original: "Deliquia's"</note>, convulsions, paralysis <note>original: "Palsies"</note>, and so on. Otherwise, a faster preparation makes the medicine no less effective for curing diseases, though it will be less powerful for extending life.

Yet, although the blessing of long life may be found in the plant kingdom by means of this solvent—for which reason these substances deserve high regard—there is nothing with medicinal effectiveness comparable to what is found in metallic extracts. These perform cures that are impossible for any plant-based remedy.

I intend to speak only briefly about these metallic extracts, saving a more thorough discovery of them for better times. To be honest, I have traveled through these investigations as the Israelites did toward the Promised Land: through a wilderness of difficulties, hardships, and obstacles. All of these were caused—with God's permission—by the malice of Satan and the envy of irrational...

...into fine atoms, or beaten into thin leaves, and then put into this liquid <note>original: "Liquor"</note> and digested in a well-sealed glass container in a heat source, such as a boiling water bath. It is as sweet to the taste as the best honey, and nature has no more supreme <note>original: "Sovereign"</note> remedy for most (if not all) diseases. 

This is the true *Nepenthe* <note>meaning: a legendary medicine mentioned in Greek mythology that banishes grief and pain</note> of the philosophers, providing certain rest and easing all pains. After sleep, it leaves the patient either significantly improved (in the case of more violent and chronic <note>original: "diuturnal"</note> diseases) or completely recovered from less severe ailments.

I can write about this subject with more firsthand experience—and also about *mercury* <note>original symbol: ☿</note>, *sulfur*, and *iron* <note>original symbol: ♂</note>. Though these materials are of little value in themselves, they possess most transcendent virtues once prepared. When I create this liquid again, I will be able to provide a longer discourse on it, as I am unwilling to merely repeat reports [of things I have not seen myself]...

But it is safer to make ***Magisteries***—that is, to dissolve the **metallic powders** <note>original: "Metaline Calxes"</note> and then draw away the Liquid. If you choose to repeat this three or four times with hard metals, you will be left with the metal or mineral in the form of a sweet salt with a fragrant scent. This is drinkable <note>original: "potable"</note> in any liquid and will yield its essence <note>original: "Tincture"</note> if dissolved in pure **Spirit of Wine** <note>meaning: high-proof alcohol or ethanol</note>.

However, if you have reliable furnaces that will provide heat exactly as you desire, then proceed further. Do not stop at making metals drinkable, but make them volatile as well. Separate then the central **Mercury** <term>☿</term> <note>the "spirit" or volatile principle of the metal</note> from the **essence** <note>original: "Tincture"</note>, which is the Oil or Sulphur. Stabilize <note>original: "fix"</note> this, as is taught concerning the Sulphur of Venus, and so you will have medicines that will achieve whatever can be desired by either the patient or the doctor.

## CHAPTER XIII.

### *Of the Substance from which this Liquid is*
### *made, and the Method of its Preparation.*

This secret is so effective and wonderful that it is of unspeakable use when found; as such, many in the world have attempted to obtain it, and with good reason, since once attained, its power <note>original: "virtue"</note> and utility more than repay the effort and expense spent on it.

But as it is with all things sought in the universe, so it is with this: no effort is profitable unless the search is first made *in the proper material* <note>original: "In debita materia"</note> and, secondly, *through the proper means* <note>original: "Per debita media"</note>.

It is not every new thing, nor every strange thing that can be made, that will prove to be this liquid <note>original: "Liquor"</note> once it is produced. No, truly—let the practitioner <note>original: "Artist"</note> work as he pleases, yet Nature will not break her own known rules to create what the operator expects in his idle fantasy. She produces only that which she is bound to by the Law of the Creator.

Now, from this mastery <note>the process of creating the solvent</note>, we shall first exclude all metals and metallic bodies. First, regarding their "Central Mercury" <note>original symbol: ☿</note>: as it is a peerless substance <note>original: "Creature"</note>, it cannot be mixed with anything in the world. It is a single, indestructible essence <note>original: "*Ens*"</note>. Being a real mercury, it will not "wet" or bond with anything except that which is similar to itself—that is, other mercurial substances. Therefore, it is not the liquid itself, nor can it be mixed with anything else by any art, whether by *sublimation* <note>turning a solid into vapor</note> or *dissolution* <note>dissolving into liquid</note>.

Now, as for the *sulfurs* of metals <note>the flammable or oily principle in alchemy</note>, they cannot be fundamentally separated from their *mercurial center* without the help of this very liquid. Thus, they would be obtained too late to be the raw material for a substance that must be completed before they can even be separated and acquired.

As for mineral combustible *sulfurs* (such as brimstone), they are very sluggish and inactive bodies. Their nature cannot be truly altered; instead, they are made into passive medicines <note>original: "Medicaments"</note>, not active solvents <note>original: "*Menstrues*"</note>. Although these passive medicines are very effective for curing diseases, they lack a dissolving power <note>original: "virtue"</note> in regards to breaking down other substances—unless they are burned, in which case they yield an acid liquid.

We are therefore excused from using mineral and metallic sulfurs and mercuries <note>original symbol: ☿es</note>. Now, as for metallic salts,

They also are useless for our purpose, since all of them (without exception) produce an acid spirit. Therefore, they are the opposite of our liquid <note>original: "Liquor"</note>, which is not acid. If it were acid, it could not remain unchanged while acting, according to the true rule of chemical philosophy: "Every acid spirit, by corroding a body, is itself weakened" <note>original: "*Omnis acidus Spiritus corrodendo corpus ipse fatiscit*"</note>.

Since our liquid is not an acid, but a spirit entirely different from acidity, we exclude saltpeter, vitriol <note>sulfate minerals</note>, rock salt <note>original: "*Sal Gem*"</note>, sea salt, and, in a word, all salts naturally found in or extracted from the earth. All of these (without exception) produce an acid spirit.

Alkalies <note>basic or non-acidic substances</note> may claim the highest status above all those named, and truly they deserve it. Their spirits are not acid, and therefore they are highly esteemed solvents, second only to the "great liquid" itself. We shall discuss these at length later.

Yet even these, though they are most noble spirits, nevertheless exhaust their power when dissolving bodies and are solidified <note>original: "coagulated"</note> upon them into a salt (though they still retain their volatility). We therefore exclude them from being the raw material <note>original: "Subject"</note> for our liquid.

To be brief, then, in what I intend, I shall come to the actual matter and reveal the practical method of preparing the liquid.

[Jan Baptist van] Helmont calls it *Latex* <note>a term Helmont used for the primordial water or a pure solvent</note> in these words: "The Art <note>original: *Religio* (referring here to the 'sacred duty' or strict discipline of alchemy)</note> was amazed when this liquid <note>original: *Latex*</note> was discovered, which..." <note>original: "*Stupefacta est Religio reperto Latice, qui, &c.*"</note>. Because the entire mystery is hidden in those few words, I shall act as a clear interpreter of his enigmatic sense and meaning.

He first says, "Art, through careful investigation, seeks a body whose purity would so harmonize with our own that..." <note>original: "*Ars indagando sollicita est corpori quod tantæ puritatis symphonia colluderet nobiscum ut*"</note>

# Pyrotechny Asserted

...could not be dissipated by a corrupting influence, etc. <note>original Latin: *à corrumpente nequiret dissipari, &c.*</note> which we may thus translate into English: *The masterpiece at which our art is aimed is to find a substance <note>original: "Body"</note> which may interact with us in such a symphony, or harmonious agreement, because of its exquisite purity, that no corrupting principle can find in it any impurities <note>original: "Heterogeneities," meaning disparate or conflicting elements</note> through which to cause a separation of its parts.*

This is the true sense of that paragraph, and indeed is a brief but very full description or definition of the highest *goal* <note>original: "Object"</note> and the unparalleled *masterpiece* of our *art*. For it is our (or the *chemical*) *art* that is concerned with this discovery. Meanwhile, the *logician* focuses on his *categories*, *propositions* <note>original: "Enunciations"</note>, *logical forms* <note>original: "Moods, Figures"</note>, and *demonstrations*, etc.; the *grammarian* focuses on his *criticisms of languages*; and the *astronomer* on the *course* of the *planets* and the *position* of the *fixed stars*. 

But the honest, conscientious *physician* focuses on the recovery of sick persons and the conquering of *diseases* <note>original: "Maladies"</note>. To perform this, he searches for the secret, hidden *spirit* of things. To extract and refine <note>original: "exalt"</note> this spirit, he diligently applies himself to attain the necessary means: namely, this *substance* mentioned in the previously cited paragraph, and our immortal *liquid* <note>original: "Liquor," likely referring to the Alkahest</note>, which is the product of that substance.

This *substance* is neither solely *fixed* <note>term: **fixed** — a substance that remains solid and does not evaporate when heated</note> nor *volatile* <note>term: **volatile** — a substance that easily evaporates or vaporizes at low temperatures</note>, but is one *substance* possessing two distinct *natures* and *essences*. This is clearly evident from the words themselves, which sound like this: *That a substance is sought which may interact with us—or sport, play, or game with us—in the symphony or harmonious sound of such great purity.*

This word *symphony* is merely a borrowed *metaphor* from *musicians*, from whom this *author* borrows often, especially when he discovers and describes these Alka- <note>The catchword refers to the "Alkahest," the legendary universal solvent described by Van Helmont.</note>

<meta>The author continues explaining the nature of the "Body" or primary substance required for alchemical work, expanding on Jan Baptist van Helmont’s musical metaphor of "symphony" and "unison" to describe the harmony of its qualities.</meta>

### and Illustrated.

Alkahestical <note>Refers to the Alkahest, a hypothetical universal solvent sought by alchemists</note> operations—as for instance, when speaking of the *Great Secrets* <note>original: "Grand Arcana's"</note> and their operation—he uses this expression: *That they perform their cures in unison* <note>original Latin: *in Tono Unisono*</note>. This alludes to musical instruments which, when tuned to *unisons*, then sound in the most perfect concord, being in unity. All other concords are but steps approaching that perfection; to this, a "second" <note>An interval in music often considered a discord</note>, which is the most absolute discord, is distinct by contrast.

But as a symphony must always be imagined between at least two notes, so this metaphor denotes a duality of qualities in this substance <note>original: "Body"</note>, which yet must agree together in harmony; this is a sounding-together or symphony.

That the duality is not in the substance itself, but in the diversity of qualities under which this one substance appears, the words are proof enough. Therefore, the ancient philosopher says, *Art is concerned with investigating a single body, not with investigating many bodies* <note>original Latin: *Sollicita est Ars (indagando corpori, non indagandis corporibus;)*</note>. This must have been said because some believe various substances should be used for this mastery—such as those who judge that <term>☿</term> <note>The symbol for Mercury or Quicksilver</note> and tartar <note>Potassium bitartrate, a salt found in wine barrels often used in chemical processes</note>, along with several other substances, should be used for this work. But from this "hotch-potch" <note>A jumbled mixture</note> nothing can be expected but a liquid most certainly weakened much, if not wholly destroyed in its active potency <note>original: "Vertue"</note>, by reason of this irrational mixture.

It is one substance, then (not multiple substances), which Art desires to satisfy its purpose in this matter. It is a substance which, being one in essence and at its root, appears to the sight as if distinguished into a twofold diversity. Yet it is only distinct in its qualities or temperament <note>original: "Complexion"</note>, but agreeing so fundamentally that, when touched by an artist's hand, it may create a musical harmony and melody in an artist's ear.

# Pyrotechny Asserted

Of this substance <note>original: "Body"</note>, which is one in essence or kind, yet two in number or appearance, it may be said what Hermes—in another (but very similar) case—said of the <term>☿</term> <note>The symbol for Mercury or Quicksilver</note> of the philosophers and its companion: *That which is above is like that which is beneath, and that below is like that which is aloft; and all work toward the production or creation of the miracles of one thing.* <note>A famous verse from the *Emerald Tablet* of Hermes Trismegistus, a foundational text of alchemy</note> This is our first discovery concerning the material of this noble liquid (which you should engrave in your mind): that it must be one substance in kind and reality, yet distinct with two faces—that is, superficially and apparently.

Nor is this substance easy to find and obtain, to which these words are a plentiful witness: *Art is solicitous, or careful, or diligently industrious about finding such a substance.* <note>original Latin: *Ars indagando sollicita est corpori*</note> Observe here that the word used for "finding" signifies a discovery made through studious search and inquiry; just as a hound that follows the scent of a footprint is properly said to "find" his game in this way. The term is compounded of the Latin words *inde* <note>meaning "from that place"</note> and *ago* <note>meaning "to act or drive"</note>, and it signifies an incessant acting upon known grounds until the thing proposed to be found is attained. This is our next discovery concerning the material of this noble liquid, a lesson I advise you to imprint in your mind so as not to let it slip.

A third consideration regarding this substance—which is very significant—is that while it is numerically two and must be found with pains, industry, and difficulty, it is also admirable when found. It is enough to puzzle reason and baffle the senses to consider how such a substance should exist within the subject in which it is discovered to lie. Therefore, the profound philosopher...

->and Illustrated.<-

[Philoso]pher adds, "At last, religion is struck with amazement by the discovery of the liquid, etc." <note>original Latin: *Tandem stupefacta est religio reperto latice*</note> It brings the Artist to a religious astonishment to consider what he has found, and forces him to cry out, "O Lord! how wonderful are you in your works, etc."

The thing when found—the discovery when made—may truly be said to be the work of God and not of man. "Who can bring," says Job, "a clean thing out of an unclean, or something pure out of the impure?" <note>A reference to Job 14:4, emphasizing the impossibility of human effort creating perfection from corruption without divine aid.</note> This, God alone must do.

Here are riddles enough to amaze and bewilder <note>original: "amuse"</note> both our reason and our senses: that a substance so loathsome should yield a "Body" so pure; that what in itself is so Proteus-like <note>Proteus was a Greek sea god who could change his shape at will; here it refers to a substance that is highly unstable and constantly changing form.</note> and mutable—more so than anything else—should give an <term>Ens</term> <note>A Latin term for "entity" or "essence," referring to the fundamental, spiritual being of a substance.</note> so unchangeable. Here, a "Chemical Faith" is required to believe this before seeing it; yet after it is seen, it will astonish the mind to contemplate it.

This mystery is not much unlike the miracle of the Creation, where so many rare and admirable forms sprang out of the formless abyss <note>original: *Inform Abiss*</note>. Out of the bosom of the dark, rude Chaos proceeded all that glory and excellence of beauty that later appeared in the matchless Paradise. To our human reason, there is no less a sense of impossibility in this peerless production, where the starting material <note>original: "Subject"</note> is as unlikely as a man could imagine. It is no wonder, then, if Art is so diligent <note>original: "sollicitous"</note> in this inquiry to find such a Body as this (since Art searches for it where it does), which must be so pure and indissolvable an <term>Ens</term>, so efficacious in its activity, and so permanent in its virtue <note>meaning its inherent power or medicinal efficacy</note>.

To collect ourselves, therefore, and to see where we are: we have found the material <note>original: "Subject"</note> in which this <term>Ens</term> lies hidden so invisibly, that

# Pyrotechny Asserted

It almost requires a person of absolute faith <term>Solifidian</term> <note>original: "Solifidian"; usually referring to the theological doctrine of justification by faith alone, here used to mean someone who believes based on faith rather than prior evidence.</note> to believe it exists. However, through hard work <note>original: "Industry"</note> it can be achieved and made visible; once it is, it is so incredibly different from the material in which it was hidden that the artist is filled with wonder <note>original: "Admiration"</note> to contemplate the result.

And yet, if the brevity of this book permitted, I could easily calm this wonder by reflecting on similar, or even less likely, creations—since all life springs forth from the heart of decay <note>original: "bosom of Corruption"</note>. But my intended purpose calls me away as quickly as possible; I have a large enough task to perform which will make this book much longer than I originally planned.

A fourth observation regarding this discovery is that this "Body" (the substance), being pure and singular, rejects mixing with any "ferment" <note>In alchemy, a "ferment" is a substance added to a base material to trigger a transformation, similar to how yeast leavens bread.</note>.

And because a ferment is the "parent of transformation" <note>original Latin: *Parens transmutationis*</note>, and since this substance will not "marry" or join with any other ferment, it is a pointless task—like "trying to wash a black person white" <note>original: "washing of a black Moor," a common archaic idiom for an impossible or futile task</note>—to attempt to change it into something else.

The reason given is that it cannot find "a body more worthy than itself to marry" <note>original Latin: *Dignius se corpus cui nuberet*</note>. The process by which it reaches this dignity and unique excellence is by reduction "to the smallest atoms possible in nature" <note>original Latin: *Ad Atomos minimos in Natura possibiles*</note>.

Thus is this liquid <note>original: *Latex*</note>, which at first seems low and worthless, elevated to a supreme height of purity and perfection. This is easy to say, but not so easily understood, and hardest of all to achieve.

This process is briefly taught by **Paracelsus** <note>A famous 16th-century Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of minerals and chemicals in medicine.</note> where he says in his book, *On the Powers of the Limbs*, in the chapter "On the Liver": The process of the **Alkahest** <note>original: "Al-chabest" (split by page break); the Alkahest was a hypothetical universal solvent sought by alchemists.</note>...

<summary>The author explains that the alchemical substance, once perfected and reduced to its smallest "atoms," becomes a pure entity that resists further transformation or mixture. He cites Paracelsus to support the idea that this "Latex" or liquid reaches a state of supreme purity from a seemingly worthless origin.</summary>

...Alkahest is, "that it be dissolved from its solid state <term>coagulation</term> and then solidified into a changed form, just as the process of solidifying and dissolving teaches, etc." <note>original Latin: "Ut à Coagulatione sua resolvatur, ac deinde Coaguletur in formam transmutatam, sicut processus coagulandi & resolvendi docet. & c."</note>. This short process is the greatest insight <note>original: "Light"</note> that this sharp-minded <note>original: "acute"</note> philosopher gives regarding this mystery; it is no wonder, then, if its teaching has remained so obscure in the world until today.

Nor is Van Helmont’s teaching much clearer; it was their intention to write in such a way as to not be understood. They intended their instructions <note>original: "Precepts"</note> to be only like prods <note>original: "Goads"</note> to young students <term>Artists</term> <note>In this context, "Artist" refers to a practitioner of alchemy or chemistry.</note> to stir them up to a serious investigation <note>original: "Inquiry"</note> into such things, of which they only gave hints. They left the discovery of everything to God alone, who will be the giver of these, His gifts, even until the end of the world.

But I have decided on a much more open honesty <note>original: "apert Candidness"</note>, knowing how profitable this secret would be if it were more commonly known among humanity <note>original: "the Sons of Men"</note>. Therefore, I have risked the criticism <note>original: "Censure"</note> of all present and future alchemists as to reveal these mysteries with much more clarity <note>original: "Openness"</note> than has ever yet been done.

To return, therefore, to our proposed task, which is the explanation of that passage by Van Helmont which, of all his writings, most fully teaches the material and the method of making the Alkahest liquid <note>original: "Liquor Alchabest"</note>. We have already explained part of this in what we have written so far.

To summarize this so we can move forward, this, in brief, is his teaching concerning that liquid: "It is a Body of Salt, appearing in two forms, yet reducible to such a harmony <note>original: "symphony"</note> that it cannot be corrupted in the future. It is found in a fluid <term>Latex</term> <note>In early chemistry, "latex" referred to a liquid or water-like substance, not modern rubber.</note> by careful and diligent search; and considering the substance <note>original: "Subject"</note> in which it lies, it would astonish a man to..."

...contemplate its nature as it is when perfected by <term>Art</term> <note>In this context, "Art" refers to the skilled practice of alchemy or chemistry.</note>. In its original state, it is a subject of contempt, but in its refined <note>original: "Exaltation"</note> state, it is an object of wonder.

For finding afterwards no substance <note>original: "Body"</note> more noble than itself to join with, it cannot be mixed <note>original: "commiscible"</note> with any ferment <note>A substance used to provide a "seed" or catalyst for change in alchemy.</note>, and so it is not capable of being changed <note>original: "Transmutation"</note>.

Now, the added phrase, "But the labor of Wisdom made an anomaly in nature" <note>original Latin: *Sed labor Sophiæ anomalum in natura fecit*</note>, is only a further illustration of what had been said before; for everything said of this liquid <note>original: "Liquor"</note> may very aptly be reduced to four heads:

The first is what the practitioner <note>original: "Artist"</note> desires and is understood in these words: "Art is carefully seeking a substance of such pure harmony that it cannot be destroyed by anything that causes decay" <note>original Latin: *Ars indagando sollicita est corpori, quæ tantæ puritatis Symphoniâ colluderet nobiscum, ut à corrumpente nequiret dissipari.*</note>. This is the sum of what the practitioner would attain, and it is the chief goal of everything that can be sought for by Art.

The second is what Art finds through industry, understood in these words: "And finally, devotion <note>or "conscientiousness"</note> was amazed when a liquid was found which—reduced to the smallest possible atoms in nature—remained 'single' <note>original: *cælebs*, literally "celibate"</note> and rejected all 'marriages' with any ferment. Its transformation was therefore deemed impossible, as it could find no substance worthier than itself to 'marry'" <note>original Latin: *Et tandem stupefacta est Religio, reperto Latice, qui ad minimos reductus Atomos in Natura possibiles, cælebs, omnis fermenti Connubia sperneret. Desperata est ergo ejus transmutatio dignius se Corpus non reperiens cui nuberet.*</note>.

The third reveals the unnatural <note>original: "Anomality"</note> character of this production in these words: "But the Art of Wisdom produced an anomaly in nature, which arose without being mixed with a ferment different from itself" <note>original Latin: *Sed Ars Sophiæ anomalum in Natura produxit, quod absque fermento commiscibili a se diverso surrexit.*</note>.

The fourth contains a short description <note>original: "Adumbration"</note> of the process in these words: "That serpent bit itself, was revived by the venom, and from then on does not know how to die" <note>original Latin: *Serpens iste seipsum momordit à veneno revixit, & mori deinceps nescit.* This refers to the Ouroboros, a symbol of eternal return and the cyclical nature of the alchemical process.</note>.

Thus, this liquid has been revealed to us...

...in its subject matter, which is a liquid <term>Latex</term> <note>In alchemy, a "latex" refers to a primordial or pure fluid substance.</note>; in its indirect <note>original: "mediate"</note> production, which is a body of two natures, between which in the end there must be a harmony <note>original: "Symphony"</note> to cause incorruptibility; and finally in its finished production, which is a substance <note>original: "Subject"</note> incapable of being changed by a catalyst <note>original: "Ferment"</note> and beyond the possibility of transmutation (this is to be understood with due limitation).

The unusual nature <note>original: "Anomality"</note> of this generation would require a full treatise to explain and reveal; for now, I shall describe it only briefly:

First, it is unusual in its operations: it is a principle that acts upon others without being changed itself <note>original: "act without Repassion"</note>. Although this is natural for heavenly bodies, it is unheard of among earthly <note>original: "sublunary"</note> creatures, except for fire <note>original: "Vulcan"</note> and this specific liquid.

Second, it is unusual in its matter. For "the tree is usually known by its fruit," and a substance by what it produces; but here it is otherwise. This product is immortal, most pure, and incorruptible, even though the matter it is made from is the most corruptible, impure, and changeable substance in the world.

Third, it is unusual in its manner of production. It becomes its own catalyst <note>original: "Ferment"</note> so that this strange substance <note>original: "Ens"</note> is produced without adding anything except what is already part of itself.

## CHAPTER XIV.

### *The Conclusion of this Subject, with a Closing Address to the Sons of Pyrotechny.*

Thus have I (*Courteous Readers*) with much sincerity <note>original: "Ingenuity"</note> endeavored to *unmask* for you *Nature* in her physical and medicinal secrets. I have shown you the true way and means of proceeding in these investigations <note>original: "Disquisitions"</note> so as to be successful in them, having taught the necessity of philosophical keys—without which nothing can be done in this <term>Pyrotechny</term> <note>Literally "the art of fire," this was an early term for chemistry, focusing on the use of heat to transform substances.</note>. Regarding the noblest of all these keys, the <term>Alkahest</term> <note>The hypothetical universal solvent of alchemy, capable of dissolving any substance into its primary components.</note> liquor, we would not pass over it in silence; rather, as becomes a *true* Interpreter of Nature, I gave it its due dignity of precedence, as being the noblest

and most eminent of all keys, more universal in its operation than the **Mercury of the Philosophers**, which is but a specific substance, applicable only to its own kind, and that in reference to a generative multiplication of species. In contrast, this liquor acts universally and without limitation on every subject in the whole world. By this means, those noble medicines may be prepared which both Helmont and Paracelsus <note>Jean Baptiste van Helmont (1580–1644) and Paracelsus (1493–1541) were famous physicians and alchemists who championed the use of chemicals in medicine.</note> boast of—and not without cause, since by them may be cured all the infirmities incident to the body of man. It is a very desirable thing to have those medicines at one's command. The glorified sulfur of the **Male Metal** <note>original: "Metallus Masculus"; likely referring to antimony or gold in an alchemical context</note>, which **Paracelsus** called his **Wine of Life** <note>original: "Vinum Vitæ"</note> and **Essence of the Limbs** <note>original: "Membrorum Essentia"</note>; nor is it found in the **Mercury of Life**, the **Lily of Mars** <note>original: "Lili of ♂"; in alchemy, the symbol ♂ represents iron or Mars. The studious reader may find these in the works of both **Paracelsus** and **Helmont**. 

All of these are like so many precious jewels. Once a person has this "Key," he may unlock nature's cabinet and command them at his pleasure. It would only be tantalizing a reader to recommend something he is unlikely to ever attain; and surely this secret is such that barely one in a hundred thousand artists <note>In this era, "artist" usually referred to a skilled alchemist or physician.</note> may or shall master it. No one shall reach it except those whom the **ALMIGHTY**, through more than ordinary grace, shall bring to it. 

I would heartily wish (if it seemed good to the Almighty) that this secret were more commonly known than it is. But I dare not prescribe rules to the **MOST HIGH**, knowing that He alone will be the dispenser of this talent until the world's end. Nor shall any attain it whom He, by a peculiar grace, does not bring to this knowledge; for it is not attained by him who wills it, nor by him who runs for it, but only by the blessing of the **LORD**, who reveals such secrets to those He has chosen. To Him alone be everlasting praise, dominion, and eternal blessing. 

Yet I would advise, along with **Helmont**, that as many as desire to be masters of the more noble <term>Arcana</term> <note>original: "Arcana's"; secret, potent medicinal preparations.</note> in medicine should endeavor with all their might to attain that **Secret Fire**. This fire is so highly esteemed for its almost miraculous virtue and effects, of which it may be said: **"The common people burn with fire, but we burn with water."** <note>original: "Vulgus igne cremat, nos aquâ"; this refers to the alchemical concept of "wet fire," a solvent that dissolves substances through chemical action rather than heat.</note> 

Doubtless, he who is industrious and diligent may, through hard work and prayer, perfect his desires to the glory of God, his own comfort, and the relief of many thousands.

Yet I would not advise any young artist <note>In this period, an "artist" refers to a skilled practitioner of alchemy or chemistry.</note> to make this secret the beginning of his chemical studies, as many do—a fault of which I myself was once guilty. God in His wisdom provides for the necessity and convenience of unworthy mankind by making one thing a key to another, and each subordinate discovery a help toward a higher one.

and the provision of food (both meat and drink), protection from cold by clothes and fire, and relief from diseases by medicines.

Nor has God postponed the cure of the sick until the time one attains the highest *secrets* <note>original: *Arcana*</note>, since there are very few in any age who possess this skill. (Although I believe it is chiefly the fault of our own neglect that so few know what legacies our *Heavenly Father* has left us in his created works). For in simple *raw materials* <note>original: *Concretes*; refers to natural substances in their unprocessed state</note> may be found the perfect cure for most (if not all) diseases, were we but as diligent as we ought to be in the inquiry and search for them.

But because the number of herbs is almost infinite (as far as we know), and their virtues are known to very few—and even those which are known are very specific in their operation and efficacy—practicing medicine using only raw herbs <note>original: "Simples"</note> would be very tedious and difficult. Therefore, there are some raw materials that, with an easy preparation, produce admirable effects. To give an example in one salt: consider saltpeter <note>original: Nitre</note>. When slightly prepared, how many diseases may be cured by it? And by its *acid essence* <note>original: *Spirit*</note>, many more and even more difficult ones.

For truly, although many raw materials <note>original: Concretes</note> possess remarkable virtue just as nature formed them, the difficulty of practicing with them may be seen by this example: Suppose a man who is troubled with *kidney pains* <note>original: *Nephritical Pains*</note> seeks to be cured, or at least eased, by such a method. For several purposes <note>original: "Intentions"; a medical term for the aim of a treatment</note>, various herbs are gathered, such as *Goldenrod* <note>original: *Virga Aurea*</note>, *Brooklime* <note>original: *Becapunga*</note>, *Marshmallow* <note>original: *Althea*</note>, *Mallow* <note>original: *Malva*</note>, and *Carrot seeds* <note>original: *Sem. Dauci*</note>, etc. These are combined into a drink by infusion, and through it ease is found. Yet if a physician should have many such patients, they would require a large stock of these herbs; if these are gathered out of season, they have little or no virtue, and therefore they must be provided at the proper time,

<summary>The author argues that God has provided accessible healing through natural plants and minerals (concretes) rather than just rare alchemical secrets. He notes that while raw herbs are effective, they are difficult to manage in bulk and across seasons, suggesting that chemical preparations like the spirit of saltpeter offer more efficient cures.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of practicing medicine using only raw plants (simples). He argues that relying on herbs is impractical because they are seasonal, lose their medicinal power quickly, and require a massive amount of equipment to transport.</meta>

<page-num>51</page-num>
<header>and Illustrated.</header>

And because these diseases are not limited to the seasons of the year when these herbs are at their peak potency <note>original: "Vertue"</note>, they must therefore be gathered at the right time, dried carefully, and stored safely. Otherwise, their potency may be significantly weakened or entirely lost before they are used. Now, to have a sufficient quantity of all the herbs recommended for every kind of disease to which the frail human body is subject—all gathered in season, dried carefully, and stored safely—is a task that is (I fear) shamefully neglected by the doctors of our age. They entrust all of this care to the pharmacists <note>original: "Apothecaries"</note>, who are so incredibly negligent that it is a shame to consider.

Furthermore, despite this trouble, herbs, seeds, flowers, or roots—no matter how seasonally they were gathered, how maturely they were dried, or how carefully they were kept—still lose their potency naturally over a short time. Once this potency is gone, it cannot be recovered. Many inventions are used to remedy this problem, but to very little purpose.

However, the medical equipment <note>original: *Medicinal Apparatus*</note> required for such a practice is so extensive that a man could more easily move a heavily loaded ship from port to port than move a well-stocked pharmacist's shop from town to town. Consequently, the only remedy left to a physician in this situation (unless he chooses to be tied to one place and never leave it) is to hope to find well-supplied pharmacists wherever he goes. For his own part, he focuses only on the "goose-quill practice" <note>original: "Gooss quill-Practise"; referring to the practice of only writing prescriptions rather than preparing the medicines himself</note>, which is the trade of all our modern doctors. By this method, he may as confidently promise success to himself or his fooled patients as that quack <note>original: "Mountebank"</note> could, who, by copying out thousands of recipes <note>original: "Receits"</note>...

Moreover, who does not see that—despite the large provision God has made for the cure of diseases through natural ingredients <term>simples: individual medicinal plants or substances used on their own</term>—scarcely a certain cure for even one malady in ten has yet been found in the common way of medical practice? Therefore, human necessity has driven men to search for a more accurate preparation of these natural ingredients. The goal is that Nature, being helped by "Art" <note>the "Art" refers to alchemy or chemistry</note> through its administration and cooperation, might allow medicines to be prepared that can achieve what was previously sought in various raw plants without success. These previous failures were caused by either ignorance of the plants' true potency <note>original: "absolute Vertue"</note>, negligence in their gathering or storage, or poor judgment in how they were applied or administered.

Now, this preparation is done in many ways; depending on how they are handled, medicines of various potencies and excellence can be created.

Simple boiling <note>original: "Bare Decoction"</note>, which is the pinnacle of the "Galenical tribe's" therapy <term>Galenical Tribe: followers of Galen, the ancient Greek physician whose theories dominated medicine for centuries; here used dismissively</term>, is a slovenly, ignorant, and blundering foolishness <note>original: "Dotage"</note>. Many sick people have experienced this failure to the loss of their hopes and money—and, quite often, their lives.

...intended the preparation of medicinal plants <term>simples: individual medicinal plants or substances used as ingredients</term>. To these practitioners—as a testimony that they were sent from God to tear down the old, crumbling system of the blind philosophy of the pagans <note>original: "heathens"</note>—were given certain credentials <term>diplomata: formal documents or evidence of authority</term>. These credentials were the miraculous evidence of powerful secret remedies <term>arcanas: powerful, secret medicinal secrets believed to be divinely inspired</term>, which demanded belief in their teachings <note>original: "doctrine"</note>. These teachings were so new in reputation and so completely opposite to the "good old way" (as it was called and believed) of the ancient authorities, that without such commanding proof, the old ways would never have lost the prestige they had gained in the world through long-standing tradition.

By this means, honest and noble-minded people were awakened. All of them, seeing the "nakedness" of their own knowledge, reacted in different ways: some trivial people sought only "fig-leaves" <note>a biblical allusion to Adam and Eve; here meaning superficial excuses or minor fixes</note> to hide their ignorance, while others, being thoroughly convinced that common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> medicines were insufficient, seriously searched for a more secure way.

And reading of the rare and almost miraculous power <note>original: "vertue"</note> of some choice secrets—which so highly adorned this discovery and were therefore so highly praised by the leaders <term>antistites: champions or chief advocates; literally "those who stand at the head"</term> of this movement—their minds were completely set on fire with the desire for them. They rejected and neglected all other things below these "top-secrets," considering them unworthy of their search.

...the common method <note>original: "vulgar Method," referring to traditional Galenic medicine</note>, I am glad for your sakes. However, that you should set aside all practice or investigation into lesser medications <note>original: "inferiour Medicaments"</note>, and only pursue the *Alkahest* <term>Alkahest: a hypothetical universal solvent sought by alchemists, believed to be capable of reducing any body into its primary liquid form</term>, the *Horizontal Gold* <term>Horizontal Gold: a specialized alchemical preparation of gold believed to possess supreme medicinal power</term>, and so on, is such a great injury to discretion. Should a man never attempt a sketch or painting unless he were certainly able to improve on *Apelles'* painting of *Venus* <note>Apelles was a renowned painter of Ancient Greece; his "Venus Anadyomene" was considered his masterpiece</note>, it is unlikely he would ever be a good painter <term>limner: an artist or illustrator</term>. Or should a man refuse all employment in the commonwealth unless he could attain the *supreme authority*? He might, and certainly would, spend all his life unprofitably. Or should one absolutely resolve against all deeds of charity until he were able to relieve all the poor around him, he would be accounted (and rightly so) a rash and stubborn man. Yet he is not unlike the man who postpones his practice in medicine until he can equal *Helmont* or *Paracelsus* <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont and Paracelsus were the most famous figures in iatrochemistry, the study of chemical medicine</note> in his remedies.

For such a man is ungrateful to God, hurtful to himself, and unjust to his neighbor; with his own hands, he does what he can to ruin the foundation of his future hopes and expectations.

## ...and Illustrated.

...if you have <note>The text likely completes a sentence from the previous page: "attain to... [if you] have one talent"</note> one talent <term>Talent: a reference to the Parable of the Talents in the Bible (Matthew 25:14-30), where a servant is punished for burying the money entrusted to him instead of investing it</term>, and you decide to bury it because it is only one, how can you hope to escape the doom and destiny of the unfaithful servant? For this very laziness was made an example for all future generations.

The gifts of God are not our own to use as we please; instead, they are to be used for His glory and for the good of both ourselves and those with whom we associate. By doing so, we will find comfort and can expect an improvement <note>original: "enbettering"</note> of our talents and gifts. Otherwise, if they are set aside in discouragement because they do not please us, they "rust"—that is, they grow worse for lack of use and diminish for lack of improvement.

I consider a man engaged in these discoveries of nature to be like a traveler on a journey. Every short step advances him toward his journey’s end. If he should refuse to take a single step because it is short and brings him only slightly closer to his goal, he will never reach his destination. To resolve to stay in one place until he can reach his goal with a single jump is both foolish to imagine and impossible to perform.

It is exactly the same in this case. The skill or knowledge of an adept in ***magical*** <note>In this context, "magical" refers to the Hermetic or Alchemical arts</note> knowledge is a great distance away from a young beginner, or ***tyro*** <term>tyro: a beginner or novice</term>. Yet the entire ***interval*** between them is filled with a variety of rare discoveries of wonderful ***virtue*** <term>virtue: the inherent power or medicinal effectiveness of a substance</term>. These grow higher and higher in excellence the closer they approach the perfection of ***nature*** that ***art*** <note>original: "Art," referring to the practice of alchemy or chemistry</note> can attain. These intermediate secrets are, as it were, so many steps forward on the journey toward perfection, and they lead directly to it. If these are neglected, it is very rare for anyone to arrive...

...the highest [point], unless some Chemical Angel by inspiration, or a Master by instruction, does that for the Artist which the Angel did for *Habakkuk* when he brought him—without taking a single step—by the hair of his head out of *Judea* into *Babylon* to relieve the imprisoned *Daniel* <note>In the biblical apocrypha (Bel and the Dragon), an angel miraculously transports the prophet Habakkuk to Babylon to bring food to Daniel in the lions' den. The author uses this as a metaphor for receiving knowledge through sudden revelation rather than hard work.</note>; this is not a common occurrence.

The well-traveled, beaten path involves employing whatever talents God bestows on us and expecting an increase of the same. First of all, the analogy between God’s creatures is such that by contemplating those whose causes and operations are more visible and more easily understood, we learn how to organize, examine, work upon, and prepare other things that are more secret. Although this is not infallible regarding any particular recipe <note>original: "Receipt"</note>, it is nonetheless reliable regarding the *Philosophical Doctrine* and instruction we receive from it.

Add to this that the hidden spirit within all things is more noble and effective <note>original: "efficacious"</note> in some than in others. Accordingly, it is more at liberty in some things and more tightly <note>original: "streightly"</note> shut up and sealed in others. Among the solid forms <term>Concretes: physical or tangible substances</term> of the three distinct kingdoms—*Animal, Vegetable*, and *Mineral*—there is such a harmony in nature that one serves as the key to the other by which it may be opened. Both together (through the power of an added ferment <note>original: "supervening ferment," an additive used to trigger a chemical or alchemical change</note>) may be elevated to a power far more noble than they possessed before.

Furthermore, we must remember (otherwise we shall experience it firsthand <note>original: "feel with a witness"</note>) that this discovery of nature is not carried out without cost and expense. A man, besides his necessary costs for food and drink, will be required to provide a house suitable for his purpose, and in it to build

By having them thus represented and personified, the reader may better judge each of them, choosing to imitate the one who may reasonably be most approved, and rejecting the other.

-> Chapter 15 <-

## The Character of an Irrational Searcher after Nature’s Secrets.

And first, I shall begin with the man who enters this field without proper discretion and manages his undertaking without prudence or reason. I spoke very briefly of such a person in the fourth chapter; I shall now describe him more fully and vividly.

<meta>catchword: Such</meta>

<summary>The author warns that the high costs and potential for failure in chemical experiments can lead to social ruin and psychological exhaustion, and introduces a comparison between wise and foolish researchers.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, chemistry, financial risk, Fortune my Foe, ingenuity, methodology, social reputation</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the character sketch of the "irrational searcher" mentioned at the end of the previous page, describing his overconfidence and gullible nature as he begins his study of chemical secrets.</meta>

<header>Pyrotechny Asserted</header>

Such a person is generally gullible and overconfident, and therefore he is seldom anything but deceived. He is, in one way or another, convinced of the insufficiency of *common medicine* <note>original: "Vulgar Medicine"</note>, regarding both its *method* and its *remedies* <note>original: "medicaments"</note>. By some stroke of luck, he happens upon some *treatise* that speaks of a more effective way of *practice*, or he meets with some *artist* <note>a skilled practitioner of chemistry or alchemy</note> or other, from whom—either by argument or *visual proof* <note>original: "Ocular proof"</note>—he becomes convinced of the reality of *noble* and *effective medicines*.

Hence, he is *drawn* to study, and entering the *ranks* <note>original: "List"; referring to the list of contestants in a tournament</note>, he encounters a variety of *authors* writing about *rare secrets*. Being a man with a ready and *uncritical* <note>original: "closing"</note> faith, he concludes that he is already an *artist* and an *adept in potential* <note>original: "Adeptus in potentia"; someone who has the potential to master the Great Work of alchemy</note>. He hardly doubts that in a short time he will be the *master* of whatever he reads, and be able to do whatever he reads or hears has been done before him.

He is for the most part *talkative* <note>original: "garrulous"</note> and *boastful* <note>original: "vainly glorious"</note>. In defiance of whatever is beneath his own hopes, he is often preaching about his *Art*, the true attainment of which he has already promised himself. Therefore, he regards himself as a *master*; though he possesses little *experience*, he has infinite *expectations*.

He overvalues his own *talents* <note>original: "Parts"</note>, and being arrogant in his own *judgment*, he is inclined to laugh at those who seek the *Art* in vain—of which he is already as certain as the man who sold a bear-skin before he had *killed* the bear.

On this account, he sets his sights on either the *Alkahest liquid* <note>original: "Liquor Alcahest"; a hypothetical universal solvent capable of reducing any substance to its primary matter</note>, or the *Philosopher's Stone*, or both. By means of these (once they are *achieved*), he expects *wealth* at will, along with *rare gems* at his pleasure, *malleable glass* <note>a legendary type of glass that could be hammered like metal without breaking</note> for his delight, the *perpetual light* <note>the "ever-burning lamp," a legendary alchemical device</note>, and

and drinkable gold <note>original: "Gold potable"; a legendary alchemical medicine thought to grant longevity</note>, which were the undiscoverable mysteries of the Magi <note>the ancient wise men or alchemists</note>.

The secrets of Van Helmont <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644), a Flemish chemist and physician</note> and Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist</note> shall then be his trivial experiments. But wait; now it is time to see him *in his devotion*. He will grow grey only in the contemplation of eternity. Charity shall be his *beginning and end* <note>original: "Alpha and Omega"</note>; the cure of the poor is the primary goal of his intentions—indeed, his only desire. But as for *covetousness*, *ambition*, *pride*, and *vanity*, against these he speaks out as if they were caught criminals <note>original: "detected Malefactors"</note>.

He is abundantly satisfied and wholly preoccupied with considering what he will do in the future. To this end, his head is seldom without a scheme or two that might bring all this to pass—and a great deal more, which my intended brevity will not allow me to detail <note>original: "particularize"</note>.

Let us therefore proceed (for it is time) to survey him *in his operations*. I shall not teach his *recipes* <note>original: "Receipts"</note> (because they are devoted to everlasting secrecy), though I could, since I know many of the methods by which a thousand rare objectives are, or have been, intended to be achieved.

If it is the Philosopher's Stone that our *philosopher* longs for, he has first of all pictured the thing so exquisitely in his mind <note>original: "in Idea"</note> that a more elaborate drama <note>original: "curious Dramma"</note> cannot be imagined regarding what it must be, what form it takes, and how it is to be used for any of his intended *purposes* <note>original: "devices"</note>.

Then he sets to work aggressively <note>original: "falls foul"</note> upon his physical matter, in which he mostly assumes his conclusion <note>original: "begs his question"</note>: namely, *that such a result will come from that matter so prepared through such operations*. He attacks the practice with fury, constantly expecting or hoping for his proposed desires.

Such a person is generally credulous and overconfident, and therefore he is seldom anything but deceived. He is somehow or other convinced of the inadequacy of common medicine <note>original: "Vulgar Medicine"; the standard Galenic medical practice of the time based on balancing bodily humors</note>, both in its methods and its medications. By some stroke of luck or another, he happens upon some treatise that speaks of a more effective way of practicing medicine, or he meets with some practitioner or another from whom—either by argument or by eyewitness proof—he becomes convinced of the reality of noble and effective medicines.

Because of this, he is *invited* to study; and entering the *fray* <note>original: "List"; a metaphor for entering a tournament or field of combat</note>, he encounters a variety of *authors* writing about rare *secrets*. Being a man of a readily accepting faith, he concludes that he is already a professional *artist* and a *potential master* <note>original: "Adeptus in potentia"; Latin for someone who has the potential to become an "Adept" or one who possesses the secret of the Philosopher's Stone</note>. He has little doubt that in a short time he will be master of whatever he reads, and be able to do whatever he reads or hears has been done before him.

He is for the most part talkative and boastful <note>original: "vainly glorious"</note>. In defiance of anything that falls short of his own hopes, he is often proclaiming *his art*, of which he has already promised himself the true achievement. Therefore, he regards himself as a *master*; though he has little experience, he has infinite expectations.

He overvalues his own abilities, and overestimating his own judgment, he is inclined to laugh at those who seek for the chemical art in vain—of which he is as certain beforehand as the man who sold a bearskin before he had killed the bear.

<meta>This page continues the description of the "irrational searcher" (the aspiring alchemist), moving from his general boastfulness to his specific chemical ambitions and his outward show of religious piety and charity.</meta>

<header>and Illustrated.</header>

...and drinkable gold, which were the undiscoverable mysteries of the Magi <note>original: "Magi"; the ancient wise men or practitioners of secret arts</note>.

The secrets of Helmont and Paracelsus <note>Jean Baptiste van Helmont (1580–1644) and Paracelsus (1493–1541) were pioneering figures in iatrochemistry, or chemical medicine</note> shall then be his trivial experiments. "Horizontal Gold," the first essence <note>original: "Ens"</note> of pearls, the elemental fire of the sulfur of copper <term>♀</term>, and the mercury <term>☿</term> of life—these shall be only his recreational diversions.

But wait—now it is time to observe him in his devotion. He will grow old <note>original: "grow grey"</note> solely in the contemplation of eternity. Charity shall be his beginning and end <note>original: "Alpha and Omega"</note>. Curing the poor is his primary intention—indeed, his only desire. But as for greed, ambition, pride, and vanity, he speaks out against these as if they were caught criminals.

He is completely satisfied and preoccupied with thoughts of what he will do in the future. To this end, his head is rarely without a scheme or two to bring all this to pass and achieve even more, which my intended brevity will not allow me to detail.

Let us therefore proceed (for it is time) to survey him in his laboratory work <note>original: "Operations"</note>. I shall not teach his recipes <note>original: "Receits"</note> (because they are devoted to eternal secrecy), even though I could; I know quite a few of the methods by which a thousand remarkable goals are, or have been, intended to be achieved.

If it is the Philosopher's Stone that our philosopher longs for, he has it first of all so exquisitely pictured in his mind <note>original: "in Idea"</note> that no more elaborate drama could be presented regarding what it must be, what form it takes, or how it is to be used for any of his intended purposes.

Then he sets to work on his material <note>original: "falls foul upon his matter"</note>. In this, he usually assumes the truth of what he is trying to prove <note>original: "begs his question"</note>: namely, that a certain result will come from that specific material through those specific operations. He then practices furiously, still expecting or hoping for his stated desires.

<summary>This page describes the grand ambitions and outward piety of the amateur alchemist, who dreams of mastering legendary chemical secrets and performing charitable acts despite his lack of practical success.</summary>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of the "irrational searcher" or delusional alchemist, focusing on how his obsession with the "Great Work" leads him to neglect practical medicine and live in a state of self-important ignorance.</meta>

<page-num>60</page-num>
<header>Pyrotechny Asserted</header>

Now, because he aims for nothing less than perfect mastery, and presumes himself to be absolutely certain of achieving it, he disdains anything short of the highest attainments. Although his general pretense is the study of medicine, it comes with this condition <note>original: "proviso"</note>: that once the medicines he longs for are perfected, then woe to all diseases that dare to cross his path! Until then, he will not involve himself in any medical practice, considering anything below what he seeks as hardly worth the thoughts spent in despising them.

By this means, he is admittedly ignorant of everything except his proposed ultimate goal. And regarding that goal, he cannot help but be actually quite ignorant, having no knowledge of it except what an uneducated imagination has created for him. For whatever a man does not know, he may well be judged ignorant of—this is easily understood by anyone and can be denied by none. Now, what kind of concept is a man likely to create for himself regarding a thing he has never seen, nor was ever instructed in, except by various books? Many of the authors of these books never saw what they described, nor knew what they were writing about—and many whose books are still in existence <note>original: "extant"</note> may be counted among that number. I shall leave this to the consideration of the impartial reader <note>original: "Indifferent Reader"; in this period, "indifferent" meant unbiased or objective</note>.

In this way, he proves the proverb of the man who, reaching for a star, stumbles over a straw. Deciding to appear as nothing unless he can equal the highest, he lives his whole life in obscurity, care, and anxiety.

Even so, while his hopes are still blooming, he is no small philosopher in his own imagination. In his own perception, there is little difference between himself and Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary figure to whom the foundation of alchemy and "hermetic" philosophy is attributed</note>,

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of the delusional alchemist, detailing how his obsession with the "Great Work" leads to financial ruin and a refusal to acknowledge the mounting costs of his experiments.</meta>

<page-num>61</page-num>
<header>*and Illustrated.*</header>

...except that one was more experienced in practice, while the other was just as sharp in *theory*. He will nourish this high opinion of himself until old age and experience force him to change his judgment.

As long as he can pay for the costs and expenses, he will never stop practicing and will rarely lose heart. He looks only for the *Philosopher's Stone* <note>original: "Stone of the Wise"</note> or the *Universal Solvent* <note>original: "Immortal Dissolver"; likely referring to the "Alkahest" sought by Helmont and Paracelsus</note> of *Helmont* and *Paracelsus*. If his experiments <note>original: "Operations"</note> fail to reach this goal, he considers them worthless—fit only for the dung heap—and turns his attention to a different experiment (perhaps even of a completely different kind).

In this way, he is impoverished every day by the cost of coal, and tools made of glass, ceramic <note>original: "Earth"</note>, iron, and so on, as well as the price of the materials he uses. Adding to this the wages for laborers and the cost of building and modifying various furnaces daily, even a large inheritance <note>original: "fair Estate"</note> will soon be drained.

Yet, by all means, these "philosophers" insist that their operations must be cheap and performed at little cost. However, between one expense and another, these recipes <note>original: "Receipts"</note> end up being ruinously expensive in the end.

Even if it were just the cost of supporting oneself for ten, twelve, or twenty years while earning nothing <note>original: "getting of one Groat"</note> to cover expenses, it would require a significant fortune to manage it.

But when a man starts to think of himself as a *Croesus* <note>a legendary king of Lydia known for his immense wealth</note> in potential, one can see how far this foolish obsession drives him. He believes his future wealth will far exceed his current spending. Anyone who has known such "philosophers" (and who hasn't known at least one?) can see this for themselves. On top of all this is the additional cost of building laboratories and designing furnaces, pots, and glassware.

<page-num>62</page-num>
<header>Pyrotechny Asserted</header>

The variety of materials worked upon, in addition to the continuous waste of coal, adds up to such an expense that it will in a short time sink a sizable estate. This is not even to mention the unquenchable thirst <note>original: "Hydropick Thirst"; a metaphor referring to dropsy (hydrops), a medical condition where a person suffers from extreme thirst despite being swollen with fluid</note> that those who have once entered this arena <note>original: "entred this List"</note> express—all to regain what they have already spent and to improve their fortunes according to what they have envisioned in their own imagination and fantasy. They are carried along so eagerly in this pursuit that nothing but running out of money can stop them.

This is the typical end for these "philosophers" <note>here referring to misguided alchemists</note>, no matter how great their estates or their hopes were at the beginning. Afterward, they lead a life that is useless to the world and miserable to themselves.

Against such people (if it were not a pity to add more misery to the miserable), I could speak out quite satirically. Instead, I shall choose to show both them and others their errors. This can be done most conveniently through the persona of a true <term>Son of Art</term> <note>a term for a legitimate, initiated alchemist who understands the spiritual and physical laws of nature</note>—one who searches for truth using the fire according to the true principles of discernment—as this is the proper and appropriate place for such a discussion.
