## CHAPTER XVI.

### *The character of one who searches nature’s secrets in a way that yields profit, and who practices <term>Pyrotechny</term> <note>original: "Pyrotechny"; the chemical art of working with fire</note> so as to become a "philosopher through fire" <note>original: "Per Ignem Philosophus"</note>.*

Having viewed the foolish intermeddler with nature’s secrets and followed him to his end—namely, poverty and want, which lead him to live uncomfortably for himself and uselessly for mankind—the reason for this (as we have shown at length) is an obstinate obsession with one or two grand <term>Secrets</term> <note>original: "Arcana’s"; from the Latin 'arcanum,' meaning a profound mystery or specialized alchemical knowledge</note>. Until he attains these, he postpones all his intentions toward medicine, as he scorns to appear without those great secrets. We shall now come to describe and represent the type of student of nature’s book who, in all probability and with God’s blessing, will have his searches crowned with success.

Such a person is devoted to study from his childhood. From the first years of his maturity, his mind is busy and his thoughts are earnest about how he may live in service to God and mankind, according to the talents with which he is entrusted from above.

Worldly advancements <note>original: "Temporal Preferments"; refers to high-ranking appointments or social promotions</note> are the least of his thoughts, as they are dangerous baits for both soul and body, often risking both. Thinking of the "gentleman's life" of pleasure is a burden to him; the lawyer’s "art of legal dispute" <note>original: "Art of Contention"</note> is like a purgatory for him to embrace; and to live as an idle theorist <note>original: "Speculator"; one who contemplates without practical application</note> is both hateful and tedious.

He knows that this life is but a race, in which we ought to run toward eternity as if toward a goal <note>original: "Gole"</note>, and therefore

<summary>This page introduces the character of the ideal natural philosopher, contrasting him with the failed alchemist by highlighting his lifelong devotion to study, his desire to serve God and humanity, and his disdain for worldly status.</summary>
<keywords>ideal philosopher, Pyrotechny, alchemical character, Arcanum, service to God, vocation, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the ideal natural philosopher. Having rejected worldly legal and social status, the philosopher focuses on his spiritual duty and the practice of medicine as the highest earthly calling.</meta>

<page-num>64</page-num>
<header>Pyrotechny Asserted</header>

The highest employment, he believes, is to glorify God himself and encourage others to do the same.

But because a person who is capable of these high spiritual duties is rarely found, he is aware of his own limitations. He does not dare to interfere in such matters further than what concerns his own salvation. Being unwilling to share in the sins of others, yet absolutely resolved by no means to live a life of idleness, he considers medicine to be the most desirable and highest achievement—second only to glorifying God and being useful in advancing piety in himself and others.

Truly, anyone who seriously and soberly weighs these matters as they should must reach the same conclusion based on undeniable reasons. After all, who cannot conclude from experience along with the poet who advised us to pray for **"a sound mind in a sound body"**? <note>original Latin: "Ut sit mens sana in corpore sano?"</note> It is evident that while we inhabit these "cottages of clay" <note>A common historical metaphor for the human body, emphasizing its fragility and temporary nature</note>, physical illnesses are (second only to sins) the primary disturbers of the mind. They disable the soul in all its functions and make our life more burdensome than death. Because of this, many people—like the biblical figure **Job**—wish for death but cannot find it, hoping that in the grave they might finally be free from all worldly sorrows, miseries, and illnesses.

Although these sufferings are most justly and righteously inflicted upon us because of our sins, they were nonetheless the object of **Christ’s** mercy and compassion. He went about doing good and curing all kinds of diseases among the people. The Apostles, who were tasked with spreading the Gospel throughout the world after Christ, confirmed their message through miracles—not for the conquest or subduing of kingdoms, but for the cure of diseases and ailments. In imitation of them, this <term>Son of Art</term> <note>original: "Son of Art"; a traditional term for a dedicated practitioner of alchemy or medicine who has mastered the "art" of nature</note> is earnest at the throne of grace in pray-

<summary>This page argues that medicine is the highest vocational calling after divine service because physical health is essential for the soul's function. The author connects the work of the physician (the "Son of Art") to the healing ministry of Christ and the Apostles.</summary>

...ple, and is a seeker and diligent in the search for nature’s secrets in the fire <note>In this context, "fire" refers to pyrotechny or chemistry—the use of heat to extract medicinal essences</note>, so that he may (through the blessing of the Most High) by seeking, knocking, and asking, find, receive, and attain medicinal secrets for the restoring of the defects of poor, afflicted mankind—of which he himself is a member, and therefore subject to the same miseries and infirmities.

To this end, he takes advice from those who went before him, according to the Apostle's rule: *Prove all things, but hold fast only to what is good.* <note>1 Thessalonians 5:21</note> On this account, he consults *Galen*, *Hippocrates*, *Avicenna*, *Rhazes*, *Mesue*, *Fernelius*, *Sennertus*, etc.—consulting everyone, both ancient and modern—with a sincere aim and intent to better his judgment and ripen his skill for medical practice.

He does not (as many do) merely for the sake of following the crowd, rail against and denounce the old way received in the schools; instead, he puts it to the test, according to the academic promises, to fulfill (with sincere intentions) his patients' expectations.

But alas! Upon testing, he finds the whole *art*, as it is academically taught, to be but an image of that stable which was cleansed by Hercules <note>A reference to the Augean Stables, which Hercules cleaned as one of his twelve labors; the author suggests academic medicine is a mess of filth that needs a monumental purging</note>—a disorganized medley, partly false, partly ridiculous, and generally desperate and dangerous.

I grant, indeed, that nature has produced almost infinite *simples* <note>term: medicinal herbs or plants used individually rather than in a complex mixture</note> of rare and excellent virtues which, with proper preparation and application, would cure most (if not all) diseases.

Yet, one must consider the specific timing of the season in which they grow and possess their virtue, their restriction to this or that particular soil—according to the proverb, *“Not every land produces all things”* <note>original Latin: Non omnis fert omnia tellus</note>—the exactness required, and the care <note>original: "curiosity," meaning meticulous skill or attention to detail</note> needed in gathering, drying, and keeping them, as well as the necessary observation in their proper...

...detecting for, and application to diseased persons, and lastly their singularity in operation—each of the **simples** <note>original: "Simples"; individual medicinal herbs or minerals used as ingredients in a medicine</note> being sufficient for any case. In these considerations, it must be granted that he who would deserve the name of a **physician** must have a broader knowledge than just that of **simples**, lest he often be accused by his practice of an inadequate and insufficient method of **medicine**.

Add to this the new **catalog of diseases** which daily appears on the stage, many of which are accounted by our **Methodists** <note>In this 17th-century context, "Methodists" refers to physicians who strictly followed the traditional "Method" of Galen and Hippocrates taught in universities, not the later religious movement</note> as incurable. These are added to a large scroll of the same sort which has long since been compiled by them, and all because they lacked distinguishing medicines. A **Son of Art** <note>A term for a practitioner of "Pyrotechny" or chemical medicine who follows the secrets of nature through laboratory work</note> is well-stocked with such medicines, which serve as evidence of his being created by, and sent from, God rather than from the academic schools.

Considering this, a true **Son of Art**, finding the insufficiency of the commonly professed method of **medicine**, addresses himself to other means and ways of providing himself with such medicines which God has made for the comfort and benefit of man.

And hearing of medicines commended by **Sons of Art** (those who were curious to search and study **nature** to find them for their virtue and effectiveness against such diseases which appear incurable to the old **Methodists**, and are confessed by them to be so), he then commits himself to the search for the same. He does this especially because, besides their verbal testimony, he has the evidence of reason on one hand, and the undeniable conviction of proof and experiment on the other, to confirm them to him.

What concerns proof and experiment, the cures of **Paracelsus**, **Quercetan** <note>Joseph Duchesne (c. 1544–1609), a French physician and chemist who advocated for the use of chemical medicines</note>, **Suchten** <note>Alexander von Suchten (c. 1520–1575), an alchemist and physician known for his work on the secrets of antimony</note>, and **Helmont** <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644), a pioneering chemist and physician who bridged the gap between alchemy and modern chemistry</note>, and before them of **Basil Valentine** <note>Basilius Valentinus, a legendary 15th-century alchemist whose writings were influential in the development of chemical medicine</note>, etc., are beyond all...

...denial and question; and although that might be a sufficient conviction to a rational man, yet moreover, there is so clear a light of reason for the authorizing of the *chemical* <note>original: "Pyrotechnical"; referring to the use of fire in laboratory work to create medicines</note> way of *medicine*, as does sufficiently convince the *Sons of Art* <note>A term for practitioners of alchemy and chemical medicine</note>, and enables them to stop the mouths of *critics* <note>original: "Gainsayers"</note>.

Of this I have spoken largely enough in my first chapters of this treatise, nor do I, nor should I, bring the same in again, but that I intend this chapter as a short summary <note>original: "Comprisal"</note> of what I wrote before.

To proceed therefore, a true *searcher of nature*, having by practical observation proved the insufficiency of the *old* (though commonly adored) way of *medicine*, resolves (with *God's assistance*) to spare no *labor*, nor *study*, nor *expense*, in pursuit of such *secrets* in nature (of which he is convinced by reason and confirmed by the testimony of *practitioners* <note>original: "Artists"</note>). These secrets enable the *possessor* of them to help those to whom it shall please God they be administered.

On these grounds he goes on with a mind resolute and fixed, not wavering nor inconstant to his *principles*, but with resolution prosecuting his attempts once they are undertaken.

And because he goes about the discovery of secret and hidden *mysteries*, he provides himself accordingly, resolved not to be discouraged, though he happens to fail again and again, nor to give over his search, although for a long time he falls short of his desired expectation.

He acknowledges and admires the greater *arcana* <note>original: "Arcana"; secret, powerful medicines or "mysteries" of nature believed to have miraculous healing properties</note>, and could, if *God* saw it good, wish himself a *master* of the highest *medicines*; yet he does not despise the least knowledge that is true, so that whatsoever it is possible for him to do the least real good, he does it.

He ascribes the glory of his *knowledge* to God. Acknowledging that he has *received* it from Him, he dare not bury any *talent* <note>A reference to the biblical Parable of the Talents (Matthew 25:14–30), implying that one must use and grow the gifts God provides for the common good</note>, but instead applies every improvement God grants him to his *labors*. He strives to do more and more good each day, and thus, with the help and assistance of the *Almighty*, he increases in knowledge day by day.

And truly, when I contemplate the series of nature’s *secrets* <note>original: "Arcana's"; hidden or mysterious knowledge</note>, I adore the wisdom of the Almighty. In them, He seems to point out the proper method for a person's study. A practitioner <note>original: "Artist"; a chemist or physician skilled in the medical arts</note> cannot miss this path if he considers first the needs of his *neighbor* and, second, his own convenience; in His *wisdom*, God has suited *nature* exactly to both of these ends.

Our *neighbor's* needs may instruct us if we consider the variety of *diseases* to which poor mankind is subject. Not all of them are equally difficult to cure; yet, even the least difficult can be as cruel <note>original: "truculent"</note> as any other if they are not helped by medical *art*.

To give a few examples: first, the *fever*. How common it is every year, and how distressing—even dangerous and mortal—to *mankind*. Yet, it can certainly be helped by many preparations that are easily made anywhere, at any time, and in a short duration. Our *agues* <note>A term for a fever marked by paroxysms of chills, fever, and sweating</note> (as we call them) are of this kind; they were all known to the *Latins* and *Greeks* under a single name <note>likely the Greek "pyretos" or Latin "febris"</note>.

It is true that the greater *secrets* <note>original: "Arcana's"</note> cure all these *diseases* along with others, both acute and chronic, in a short time. In almost any fever—except only the *hectic* <note>A persistent, wasting fever often associated with tuberculosis</note>, which is a true *wasting disease* <note>original: "Tabes"; a medical term for a gradual loss of strength or body mass</note>—these secrets seldom require more than one dose. Many of these *secrets* possess wonderful power <note>original: "vertue"</note>, but they are rarely granted to any practitioner,

He suggests that while powerful chemical secrets ("Arcana") can cure most diseases quickly, they are rare and require great wisdom to obtain.</summary>

Yet, in the absence of these, there are many fever-reducers <note>original: "Febrifuges"</note> on which a careful physician may rely and risk his reputation; they will never stain the honor of those who use them.

These specific substitute medicines <note>original: "succedaneous Medicines"</note> are usually the crowning achievement of the efforts of many to whom the greater secrets <note>original: "Arcana"</note> are—in God’s wisdom—temporarily denied. Nor are these secrets so few in number, so difficult to prepare, or so time-consuming. They were designed, as if by God, to be easily and quickly provided for the remedy of those diseases that allow no delay <note>original: "accept of no Truce"</note>. I could name many of these, but I am saving that revelation for another, more appropriate time and place.

Besides these, many other diseases are cured by simple medicines. Though these illnesses may not be fatal, they are still burdensome to the person suffering and make life uncomfortable—such as scurvy <note>original: "Scorbute"</note>, hysterical fits <note>Historically, these were a variety of symptoms—including anxiety and physical distress—once thought to be caused by disturbances in the womb</note>, jaundice, convulsions, colic <note>original: "Cholical Fits"</note>, kidney pains <note>original: "Nephritical Pains"</note>, and so on. By God’s blessings, these are, have been, and can certainly be cured by specific medicines that serve as substitutes for the great secrets <note>original: "Arcana"</note>.

Indeed, even those diseases that so boldly defy physicians, such as the **gout** and **epilepsy**, have their milder symptoms <note>original: "Mitiora Symptomata"</note>, which are easily cured by specific remedies.

Considering these things, it is an inexcusable madness for anyone pursuing these discoveries to resolve to avoid all medical practice until he has become a master of the greater secrets <note>original: "Majorum Arcanorum Adeptus"</note>.

For since there are so many diseases that are so painful—even cruel <note>original: "truculent"</note>—the cure of which does not require the **greater secrets**, how cruel is he to mankind, and in—

How ungrateful <note>original: "Grateful"; likely a printer's error for "Ungrateful" given the context of neglect</note> to God and harmful to Nature is he who, while pretending to study Nature, neglects the search for those **medicines** <note>original: "Medicaments"</note> which possess such great virtue and efficacy. This is especially true when so many poor creatures in every hole and corner stand in need of help. This need can be abundantly supplied by **substitute medicines** <term>succedaneous medicines: effective remedies used as alternatives to the most powerful, "universal" alchemical secrets</term> for those **Great Alcahestical Secrets** <note>original: "Grand Alcahestical Arcana"; refers to remedies derived from the Alcahest, a hypothetical universal solvent</note>. By using these, a careful physician may cure all diseases incident to the human body—though not all with a single medicine. Many **sudden illnesses** <note>original: "Acute Diseases"</note> may be cured with just one, and all **long-term illnesses** <note>original: "Chronical Diseases"</note> may be cured by one, two, or three varied medicines, as the symptoms <note>original: "Indications"</note> may require.

I know it will be objected against me from the writings of **Helmont** <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644), a foundational chemist and physician</note>, who in the ninth chapter of his book *On the Stone* <note>original: "De Lithiasi"</note> writes: *"No one shall cure leprosy who has not obtained the Liquor Alcahest,"* etc. To this, I cannot answer from personal experience, as I have never had a leper as a patient. Yet, with all respect to that great philosopher, I must ask permission to be of a different mind. My difference in opinion is not without a supporting experiment <note>original: "collateral Experiment"</note> to confirm my view, of which I shall take the time to speak more extensively elsewhere.

I shall conclude this chapter quickly, having briefly touched upon the **practitioner's convenience** <note>original: "Artist's convenience"</note>, which (as I said before) should be like his **guiding rule** <note>original: "Line and Plummet"</note> in his research, along with the necessity of his neighbors, which I have already discussed.

At this point, it is not unreasonable to remember what kind of men usually devote themselves seriously to the study of the **ingenious arts** <note>The "chemical arts" or scientific research</note>. They are, at best, of **middle status** <note>original: "mediocris"</note>, and for the most part **men of no fortune** <note>original: "Nullius fortunae homines"</note>. Meanwhile, those of large fortunes occupy their minds with pleasure and luxury,

So it is that according to the proverb, *only the poor practice philosophy* <note>original: *Soli Philosophantur pauperes*</note>, which is a true proverb suited to our wretched age. Philosophy was formerly considered an entertainment for a prince, and those who were philosophers were considered companions fit only for monarchs and great personages. Now, however, the art must take what it can get, being admired only by the lower or, at best, the middle class of men (in terms of wealth). For their efforts, these men are considered mad by the common people and seen as deserving of a life in poverty and disgrace for dedicating themselves to such a "foolish" science.

By such "sons" <note>followers or students of the art</note> it is held in high esteem; yet according to the poet, *virtue is praised and then left out in the cold* <note>original: *Virtus laudatur & alget*; a quote from the Roman satirist Juvenal meaning that people offer verbal praise for goodness but do nothing to support it financially</note>. These favorites of philosophy, no matter how much they love her, cannot afford to clothe her in the attire she deserves to make her appear beautiful.

Consequently, whoever courts Nature needs to court her as if for an inheritance—to gain a sufficient income <term>competency: a sufficient income or means to live comfortably</term> by which he may both live well and continue in her service without distraction.

To this end, he is willing to undergo reasonable expense until he may gain some intimate knowledge of her. He then expects from her a reward <note>original: "pension"</note> proportional to his service, enabling him to continue his work rather than being forced to seek another "mistress" for a livelihood—be it war, seeking favor at court <note>original: "courtship"</note>, or the like.

And this indeed is the kindness of that nymph <note>Nature personified</note>: she suffers none of her servants to remain long un-

But to be master of her greatest secrets is the crown that few attain, and those only such who by an especial grace are elected to it.

Now, for anyone to resolve that unless he may be the commander and disposer of Nature's most rare and seldom-bestowed secrets, he will refuse such as are offered him—it is so high an indiscretion <note>meaning: an extreme lack of judgment</note> that it cannot be pleaded for.

Because the search into Nature and her secrets is not to be performed without cost and charge—which, as soon as a man is master of any (though inferior) secret, that secret will defray—for him to refuse this because it is not the top secret of Art and Nature is no less madness than for a man to resolve to go naked until he is able to go clad in the richest satin.

But those who are sober sons of Nature <note>original: "sober Sons of Nature"; refers to serious and disciplined students of natural philosophy and alchemy</note> know and are sensible that each discovery of Nature requires cost and charge to bring it about. Therefore, weighing the necessities of mankind, they are sensible that many secrets of Nature—of an inferior or middle rank compared to the highest—are very noble and efficacious, and so are not to be slighted without incurring the censure of folly and madness.

These, therefore, he accepts from God thankfully as pledges of future blessings to be bestowed; or as *Joshua* received the *bunch of grapes* <note>Referring to Numbers 13:23, where a large cluster of grapes was brought back from Canaan as proof of the land's fertility</note> as an earnest <term>earnest: a partial payment or sample given in advance as a guarantee of more to come</term> of the *Promised Land*; or as a metallist <term>metallist: a person skilled in the study or extraction of metals</term> accepts a marcasite <note>original: "Marchasite"; a mineral (iron pyrite) often found near precious metal ores, serving as a sign of a nearby mine</note> as an index of a mine not far from discovery.

Some perhaps, who seek ways to criticize <note>original: "carp"</note> and object, may throw back at me <note>original: "cast in my dish"</note> what I wrote in my first part (which was a defense of my position)—namely, *that in Nature there were so many profitable secrets that a man should not be compelled to practice medicine out of the necessity of supporting himself.*

It is very true that Nature is rich and abundantly stored with a variety of choice secrets, and many of those are very profitable <note>original: "lucriferous"</note>. She is such a bountiful lady that no one has ever yet pursued her seriously and consistently in vain.

But yet, the ways by which she rewards her servants are worth considering. She does not usually repay their pains and diligence with minted money (as some great personages, when in danger of their lives, have thrown gold coins <note>original: "Ceckeens and Pistolets"; Zecchins and Pistolets were types of gold currency</note> among their followers to distract them). No, truly; her reward consists of secrets, which are of use and benefit to those by whom they are made...

Now these secrets are of various kinds, which the diligent searcher of nature (unless strangely led by an unfortunate destiny) cannot fail to encounter, or at least discover one or another of them. I could mention many of this sort, but it is unnecessary to do so here. By this art, the Bow-dye <note>A famous scarlet dye developed at Bow, London, in the 17th century using tin and cochineal</note> was discovered. By this art, the whitening of yellow pearls and the coloring of diamonds have been found out by others, in addition to many specific processes <note>original: "gradations"</note> and mineral extractions known to many, the products of which are real, true, and not counterfeit <note>original: "sophisticate"</note> metals.

But the curing of the sick is an employment that engages the consciences of all those to whom God has given the ability, as it is of a more excellent nature—indeed, a work of mercy. It is not beneath the dignity of a prince, as God demonstrated for a long time through our kings in the cure of that disease commonly known and called by the name of the "King's Evil" <note>Scrofula, a form of tuberculosis affecting the lymph nodes, which was traditionally believed to be cured by the "Royal Touch" of French and English monarchs</note>.

However, it must be granted and cannot be denied that he who devotes himself to these studies must have an adequate income <note>original: "competent maintenance"</note>. Otherwise, no wise man would or could excuse his philosophy from being called foolishness. Although he might obtain this income in several ways, he simply admires the goodness of God, who has provided so many ways in nature for those who are as studious as they ought to be. But (as every wise man is known by his choices when he has the liberty of various options), he chooses that means of support through which he may do the most good and lead a life most beneficial to many. Such is the life of practicing the art of medicine, and thus the objection is answered.

## Chapter 1: An introductory historical narrative of my first introduction to and early progress in chemical philosophy.

In the year of our Lord 1644, I first began the study of **chemical philosophy** <note>original: "Chymical Philosophy"; the seventeenth-century term for the combined study of alchemy and chemistry</note>. How I was first prompted to begin this study would be a story both tedious and useless to tell here; I must only say and acknowledge this: such solid reasons invited me, and such great encouragement confirmed my path, that from that time until this present year of 1658, I have never regretted the time I spent on it—even though the labor I have since undertaken was difficult to endure and would be exhausting to describe.

My aims and intentions when I first began may have been the same as others who are lured to this **Art** by curiosity or other motivations. However, my progress (in terms of industry and diligence) was such as would befit a devoted **Son of Hermes** <term>Son of Hermes</term> <note>original: "Son to Hermes"; a traditional name for a dedicated alchemist, referring to the legendary founder of the craft, Hermes Trismegistus</note>. And my aims (as for the...

<meta>catchword: gene- (likely "general")</meta>

<summary>The author begins the third part of his work by detailing his personal history with alchemy, noting that he began his studies in 1644 and has remained dedicated to the "Art" for fourteen years.</summary>
<keywords>George Starkey, chemical philosophy, alchemy history, 1644, 1658, Son of Hermes, medicinal secrets</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s narrative of his early studies, picking up from the word "general" on the previous page to describe his primary focus on medicine and his rejection of traditional university philosophy.</meta>

and Illustrated. <page-num>77</page-num>

general), was only *medicine*, or at least that principally.

I had not progressed far, nor spent much time in these studies, before I had acquired a reasonable number of *authors* who write about this learning. I began first to read them, then to admire them, and lastly to desire to imitate them.

This way of learning was easily entertained and more readily accepted by me, as I was someone whose affections were disengaged from *school philosophy* <note>original: "School Philosophy"; referring to the traditional Aristotelian logic and physics taught in 17th-century universities</note>, as it required a senseless faith to believe it, being indeed at its best nothing but rotten.

Although I was only a youth, I perceived this easily (as I explained more fully in my first *Apology*, as well as in my treatise written in *English* <note>original: "Latine"; the author is referencing his work *De Liquore Alchabest* (On the Alkahest Liquor)</note> titled *On the Alkahest Liquor*). On that account, I considered the time I spent attaining that university *philosophy* to be wasted; for that reason, I readily embraced the *philosophy* <note>referring to Chemical Philosophy or Alchemy</note> which gave its sons and students better hopes.

I considered this kind of *philosophy* to be such a path, and therefore devoted myself wholly to it. Yet, the necessity of attending to academic studies—for fear of offending my friends and tutors—compelled me to spend a great deal of precious time learning the *philosophy* which I heartily despised, being convinced of its futility.

And having (on serious and sober grounds) resolved to pursue the study and employment of *medicine*, I first tried the works of *Galen*, *Fernelius*, and *Sennertus* <note>Jean Fernel and Daniel Sennert were famous medical authors; Sennert in particular was known for trying to harmonize traditional Galenic medicine with new chemical ideas</note>, along with others, to see what I could find in them. Then, to these I added the *chemical writers*, hoping that by the *theory* of the one and the *practice* of the other, I might find a secure way of curing diseases. And, to deal honestly, the opportunity of practice offer—

[Continu]ing itself each day, gave me the opportunity to find that the promises of many proved vain by their results—the most absolute conviction of all—though cursed by the poet:

->I hope that he lacks success,<-
->Whoever thinks that deeds should be judged by their outcome.<-
<note>original: "Careat successibus opto / Quisquis ab eventu, facta notanda putet"; a quote from Ovid's *Heroides*</note>

This daily disappointment led me to inquire after other writers and authors, and at last, I obtained the works of all, or at least the most eminent of them. I studied these seriously, resolving within myself that God had not given such rare secrets to ***Paracelsus*** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine</note> simply for him to tantalize all future generations with them; rather, I believed that the same secrets might be attained through industry. I thought reading would contribute to this, but I was mistaken.

It is the truth that the books of learned men are of excellent use to those who join practical work with reading; but to those who only read them, they are useless. There is scarcely one recipe plainly set down by any solid author who was a real **adept** <term>adept (original: "Adeptus"): a person who has mastered the secrets of alchemy, specifically the creation of the Philosopher's Stone or universal medicine</term>, that was not—and will not be found to be—either trivial or false.

And to give every man his due, I must thankfully acknowledge that I have reaped more real benefit (regarding solid learning) from ***Helmont’s*** <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644), a pioneering chemist and physician who followed the teachings of Paracelsus but emphasized experimental observation</note> writings than from any that I have read or encountered, whether ancient or modern. In the pursuit of his discoveries, I have spent these past fourteen years; nor have I had the least cause for regret since I first undertook them.

He may truly be called ***Paracelsus's*** great interpreter, to whose writings the world is more indebted than the ungratefulness of this age will suffer...

<summary>The author describes his transition from theoretical reading to practical experimentation, ultimately finding his greatest inspiration in the works of Jan Baptista van Helmont. He argues that chemical secrets cannot be learned through books alone but require "practical operation."</summary>

<page-num>79</page-num>

...to be acknowledged. However, future ages will acknowledge his worth, when many superficial scholars <note>original: "Sciolists"</note> will be buried in oblivion.

It was first through his prompting and encouragement that I began the search for the immortal dissolving liquid, called by **Paracelsus** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a pioneering Swiss physician and alchemist</note> his ***Liquor Alkahest*** <term>Liquor Alkahest: a hypothetical universal solvent sought by alchemists, believed to be capable of reducing any body into its primary liquid form</term>. I have spoken sufficiently about this in my second treatise, as the structure of the discourse required and led me.

This liquid (to be honest <note>original: "ingenious," used here in the sense of being frank or ingenuous</note>) occupied a great deal of my time, because I believed that nothing would remain difficult in medicine once it was attained. Indeed, it is a noble substance <term>substance (original: "Ens"): a philosophical term for an essence or a thing that exists in itself</term>, worthy of the time and study of all who desire to be physicians.

But I remembered and trusted **Helmont’s** restraining conclusion: *Knowledge is given only to chosen men, through long experience of years and labors, who are provided with sufficient health and money, and who do not make themselves unworthy by the burden of their sins.* <note>original Latin: "Scientia datur non nisi electis viris, per longam annorum & laborum experientiam, sufficienti sanitate, & pecuniâ instructis, nec peccatorum gravamine, indignitatem meritis."</note> In English, it means this: this science or skill is only given to those whom God chooses, through the experience of many years spent in labor, where those who search for it are sufficiently provided with both money and health, and do not make themselves unworthy of it through a sinful lifestyle <note>original: "Conversation"</note>.

So, unless a man has land to live off (and those who do are rarely supporters or followers of **philosophy** <note>In this context, "philosophy" refers to "natural philosophy" or alchemy—the study of nature's secrets</note>), he must provide himself with some profitable <note>original: "lucriferous"</note> experiments in the meantime to pay his expenses and help him live. Otherwise, his philosophy will nearly starve itself and the philosopher before he can reach that "gray-headed" experience which, through much pain, is the teacher of secrets.

<meta>This page continues George Starkey’s reflection on his search for the "Liquor Alkahest" (a universal solvent). He cautions the reader against obsessive study at the expense of practical stability and introduces the next section on "Specifics"—medicines for specific ailments.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>
<header>Pyrotechny Asserted</header>

To my comfort, I can say that my studies and efforts regarding the great *Liquor* <note>The "Liquor Alkahest," a hypothetical universal solvent</note> were not unsuccessful. Yet I do, and always shall, discourage the zeal that drove me to search for it while neglecting almost everything else. Had I not done that, but instead pursued each thing gradually—securing the ground I had already gained to support me on all occasions before trying to win more (which is the only right way)—I would have fared better. I recommend this approach to others so they may learn from my experience.

This is not unlike the advice of that old *philosopher* <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644)</note>, who said, "*First, obtain a dissolver that is immortal and homogeneous, etc.*" anyone can read his counsel in his *Treatise of Fevers*. But he says, "*If you cannot achieve that hidden fire, at least learn how to make the Salt of Tartar volatile, so that you may use it to create your dissolutions.*" <term>Salt of Tartar Volatile: potassium carbonate processed to be vaporizable; it was considered a potent chemical tool for dissolving substances</term> I leave the *praise* <note>original: "Encomium"</note> of this substance to anyone who is able and willing <note>original: "list"</note> to read it in the author’s own works.

## CHAPTER 2.
### Concerning *SPECIFICS*.
<term>Specifics: medicines intended to treat a particular organ or disease, as opposed to "Universal" medicines that treat the whole body</term>

In the previous book (the second one), I followed the division of *Pyrotechny* <term>Pyrotechny: the art of using fire in chemical and alchemical processes</term> according to the categories of *medicines* it contains—either *Universal* or *Specific*. In pursuing that division, we arrived at the distinction of the "*Keys*" used in this *Art*. These keys are used to unlock, prepare, and perfect *substances* <note>original: "Bodies"</note> for the purpose of creating *Medicine*.

<summary>Starkey reflects on the personal cost of his obsessive search for the Alkahest and advises chemists to work methodically. He begins a new chapter defining "Specific" medicines and the chemical "Keys" used to create them.</summary>
<keywords>George Starkey, Liquor Alkahest, Salt of Tartar, Van Helmont, Pyrotechny, Specifics, alchemical keys, medical philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues George Starkey’s discussion of alchemical medicines. Having previously discussed the "Alkahest" (the universal solvent), he now moves on to "subordinate" or secondary medicines, specifically focusing on the power of volatile alkalies.</meta>

<page-num>81</page-num>
<header>and Illustrated.</header>

This method led us to discuss the liquor *Alkahest* <note>original: *Alchahest*; a hypothetical universal solvent</note>, the great universal dissolver of all bodies, which works without the least loss of its own power or any decrease in its weight.

I shall now turn to the other inferior "keys" and medicines of a lower rank than those prepared by that art and the previously mentioned liquor.

The liquid subordinate to the *Great Dissolver*, then, is the *Spirit of Volatile Alkalies*. I will speak only briefly of it here, reserving a more full and clear explanation for an entire treatise on that subject titled *On the Mysteries of Alkalies* <note>original: *De Mysteriis Alcalium*</note>, which I intend shall soon see the light of day.

Alkalies are bodies of excellent virtue, according to *Helmont* <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont, a pioneer of iatrochemistry</note>, who says of them:

> *That fixed alkalies <term>Fixed Alkalies: solid alkaline substances, like potassium carbonate, that do not easily vaporize</term>, when made volatile <term>Volatile: the state of being easily evaporated or turned into vapor</term>, equal the virtue of the Great Arcana <note>Great Arcana: the most powerful and "secret" alchemical remedies</note>. For, being endowed with an incisive or resolving power, they penetrate even to the threshold <note>original: *Limen*</note> of the fourth digestion <term>Fourth Digestion: in older medical theory, the final stage of nutrition where blood is transformed into solid body tissue</term> and dissolve whatever unnatural coagulation they find in the veins. In short, their spirit is of such an exquisitely penetrating nature that where they cannot reach, nothing else in the world will be found to reach.*

This is a noble praise, and he is not in the least bit wrong. Therefore, I shall discuss this subject as fully as I can without detracting from the other treatise on this specific subject that I just mentioned, which I intend to publish soon.

I shall leave the details of how they are produced, as well as the philosophical theories regarding their stability <note>original: "Fixity"</note> and the possibility of making them volatile, untouched here, as they are best suited for an entire philosophical treatise on the matter.

<page-num>82</page-num>
<header>Pyrotechny Asserted</header>

Alkalies, then, through the craft of art and the help of nature, can be made volatile; from them, excellent medicines <note>original: *Medicaments*</note> can be prepared—specifically, those required to cure any disease.

The excellent power <note>original: *Vertue*</note> and use of alkalies are evident from how they can be applied to both mineral and vegetable sulfurs.

Using them, sulfur can be extracted from any common mineral or inferior metal. For example, even lead, through the mediation of fixed salts <term>Fixed Salts: solid alkaline substances that do not evaporate when heated</term>, will allow its elements of sulfur and mercury <note>original: ☿</note> to be dissolved and will become a liquid quicksilver <note>original: *Argent Vive*</note>. The sulfurous and saline parts are absorbed into the alkalies, through which they can also be made volatile by art.

Indeed, by simply boiling it in a strong lye <note>original: *Lixivium*</note> of tartar, the sulfur of antimony <note>original: ♁</note> can be obtained, separated from the mercury or metallic antimony <term>Regulus: the pure metallic part of a mineral that settles at the bottom during smelting</term>, just as it is achieved through melting, which leaves it more open and dissolved.

This is why, if antimony <note>original: ♁</note> is melted with an alkali of tartar and salt, the salts (which absorb the sulfur) will dissolve either in water or by themselves in a moist place. The sulfur of the antimony then flows down and is contained invisibly within the lye of the salts. Because this solution will stain the hands of anyone who touches it a golden color (due to the invisible sulfur within it), and because adding an acid will cause it to precipitate into a red form (accompanied by an intolerable stench), beginners <note>original: *Tyrocynists*</note> call it the "Golden Diaphoretic Sulfur of Antimony" <note>original: *Sulphur Antimonii Auratum Diaphoreticum*; a sweat-inducing preparation of antimony</note>. Though these novices use it as a trivial toy, it can be raised to a most admirable power.

To achieve this, let these impregnated salts...

<summary>Starkey explains how alkalies can be used to extract sulfur from minerals and metals, such as lead and antimony, to create potent medicines. He distinguishes his advanced methods from the "trivial toys" or common chemical preparations used by inexperienced practitioners.</summary>

<meta>This page continues George Starkey’s explanation of how to manipulate salts and minerals to extract their medicinal essences, specifically focusing on the creation of a "Panacea of Antimony."</meta>

<page-num>83</page-num>
<header>and Illustrated.</header>

If the salts impregnated with *sulfur* are dissolved until they are red like *blood*, and separated from all their *earthy dregs* <note>original: *Terrene Feces*</note>, then by art reduce the whole mass of salts, along with the *sulfur*, to a volatile state. In this process, there will be a very unsavory stench, which will pass away on its own, and from your *red lye* <note>original: *Red Lixivium*</note> you shall have a sweet mass, without odor, as white as snow.

This snow is a *universal remedy* <note>original: *Panacea*</note> of antimony <note>original: ♁</note>, which purges the body reliably without causing vomiting or nausea—even in the weakest patients—and without causing cramping <note>original: *griping*</note>. It is a cure for many diseases, including those that are chronic.

But to proceed to a further exaltation of its power: Take this snow and, according to the proper art of distillation, process it further. Specifically, mix it with dried clay <note>original: *Potter’s Earth*</note> and, by increasing degrees of fire, distill it until everything has come over (which is achieved by <term>cohobation</term> <note>the process of pouring a distilled liquid back onto its residue and re-distilling it to increase its potency</note>). This leaves behind only a worthless, black, tasteless earth <note>original: *damned black insipid Earth*; refers to the *caput mortuum* or chemical residue</note>. The *spirit* produced will be tinted and fragrant, resembling a drinkable liquid of gold in color. It will have a deep tincture, of which five or six drops administered daily will not fail to help, even in the most desperate cases imaginable.

In the same way, if *residue of vitriol* <note>original: *Colchotar of Vitriol*; the iron oxide left after distilling sulfuric acid</note> is perfectly washed of its salt and dried, and then boiled with an equal part of liquified *salt of tartar* <note>potassium carbonate</note>, and then both are melted in a crucible and poured out, you shall find that the *residue* will allow almost all of its *sulfur* to be absorbed into the *lye* <note>original: *Lixivium*</note>. Then, through the processes of chemical "killing" and renewal <note>original: *Mortification and Regeneration*</note>, bring this to a volatile state and distill it (as was described for the *sulfur* of antimony <note>original: ♁</note>). You shall obtain a liquor of a yellowish-green tint that is quite fragrant.

In this liquor, dissolve quicksilver <note>original: *Argent Vive*</note>, and you shall find that in this dissolution the quicksilver is embraced by the sulfur (contained in the liquor) and so "fixed" <note>rendered stable and non-volatile in fire</note> that, in the fire, both will yield a real metal. However, if it is sweetened <note>original: *dulcified*; processed to remove acidity or corrosiveness</note> with spirit of wine without being reduced to a metal, it becomes a true substitute <note>original: *succedaneous*</note> medicine for *Helmont's Horizontal Gold*. That medicine is made by means of the sulfur of vitriol of copper <note>original: *Vitriol of Venus*</note> brought into an oil by the *Alkahest* <note>original: *Alchahest*; a hypothetical universal solvent</note> liquor.

The same method may be used for the sulfurs of lead <note>original: ♄</note> and tin <note>original: ♃</note>, and even for the sulfur of antimony <note>original: *Metallus Masculus*</note>. These operations are not easily learned from either Helmont or Paracelsus, although Paracelsus gives clear light to this very thing in many places, but especially where he says: "There are, moreover, essences of wine reduced to ashes which dissolve gold, etc.; if they are processed in a circle, they reduce gold, etc." <note>original: *Sunt præterea essentiæ vini cineratæ, quæ aurum ſolvunt, &c. ſi in circulum dentur, aurum reducunt, &c.*</note> By this, he means the Salt of Tartar, which is derived from wine and is considered by him the best part of the wine, as it has more of its essence than any other part. This is "cinerated," or brought to ashes (as is done to extract the salt from it), and afterward "circulated"—that is, volatilized, for it cannot be circulated until that time—and then it reduces gold, etc.

Nor is Helmont obscure regarding this particular point when he says: "If the spirit of volatile Salt of Tartar dissolves either silver <note>original: ☽</note>, copper <note>original: ♀</note>, hartshorn <note>original: *Cornucervi*</note>, crabs-eyes <note>small calcium stones found in the stomachs of crayfish, used as an antacid</note>, or any other simple substance, it will cure not only fever but most (if not all) chronic diseases." Now, mercury <note>original: ☿</note> that is corroded by any liquor and not fixed is an unsafe medicine; it is condemned by this philosopher in many places as unfit for an honest man to use. This liquor, then, by dissolving the mercury, gives it a stability <note>original: *Fixation*</note> sufficient to make it a very noble medicine; but when it is united with a volatile sulfur

(as I taught before) it then gives it a metallic fixity <note>the state of being stable and non-volatile in fire</note>, in the same way—though at a lower level of excellence—as the fixation of it in *Helmont’s Horizontal Gold*, which is created and stabilized by the *Alkahest* <note>original: *Liquor Alchahest*; a hypothetical universal solvent</note> liquor.

There are then three ways to operate upon this volatilized alkali <note>original: *Alcali*</note> to apply it to metallic bodies.

First, the alkali is volatilized—that is, regenerated through a process of "death and life"—and transformed into a totally volatile salt (which has great power on its own). When distilled according to the rules of the art, this produces that noble liquor of which *Helmont* and *Paracelsus* give such high praise <note>original: *Encomiums*</note>, stating that wherever this spirit cannot reach, no other spirit will.

This spirit is volatile and salty (not acidic), and therefore harder to obtain for mere pretenders <note>original: *Putationers*</note> and superficial scholars <note>original: *Sciolist*</note>. It dissolves all solid masses <note>original: *Concretes*</note>, but once they are dissolved, it thickens <note>original: *coagulated*</note> upon them into a volatile salt. When this salt is then sublimed <note>heated until it turns to gas and then solidifies again</note> from the dissolved powder <note>original: *Calx*</note>, it draws out the sulfur <note>the active, medicinal principle</note> of imperfect metals along with itself. In perfect metals, it does the same through frequent circulation.

Secondly: If this alkali, regenerated into a volatile salt, is mixed with the powder <note>original: *Calx*</note> of either iron <term>♂</term>, tin <term>♃</term>, lead <term>♄</term>, or antimony <note>original: *Metallus Masculus*</note>, or with the metallic heart <note>original: *Regulus*</note> of iron <term>♂</term>, and distilled with them, it makes them volatile. Each time the distilled spirit is poured back onto the residue <note>original: *Caput Mortuum*; literally "dead head"</note>, it thickens upon it.

Proceed by repeated distillation <note>original: *Cohobation*</note> until your sign appears, which every natural philosopher should watch for diligently. Then solidify your spirit—which contains the hidden metallic sulfur—using concentrated <note>original: *dephlegmed*; with the water removed</note> spirit of wine,

...medicine, extract the metallic tincture <note>original: *Metalline Tincture*; the concentrated essence or medicinal soul of a metal</note> from the salt. You may then consider yourself the master of a *balsamic medicine* <note>original: *Medicine Balsamical*; a healing, restorative remedy</note>, which you can never value or esteem enough.

The whole secret, then, is to know how to make *alkalis* volatile. This is a secret that will never be known by a lazy person or a conceited *pretender* <note>original: *Purationer*, likely a variant of *putationer*, meaning a mere theorizer or someone who imagines they know more than they do</note>. It is one of Nature's secret keys—to fix <note>to make a substance stable in fire</note> and to volatilize—which she performs incessantly every day across all her three kingdoms <note>the animal, vegetable, and mineral kingdoms</note>.

Whether you lay many tons or only a tiny amount of these fixed salts in any field, in a few months, all of it would be transmuted into a *volatile salt*. This is why *calx* and *ashes* enrich the soil for growing corn. And yet, the "philosophers" of our day have not learned to imitate Nature in her most ordinary operations.

<meta>This page continues George Starkey’s critique of the medical establishment of his day, moving from the technical details of his alchemical "balsamic medicine" to a sharp polemic against traditional physicians who refuse to adopt new, more effective methods.</meta>

The truth is, those who should pay attention to these things are idle and arrogant; they cannot bear the labor of research, and they pride themselves as if they already possessed all knowledge. Meanwhile, they are ready to harass and persecute anyone who will not follow the old paths with them.

However, Truth must and will prevail, and those who have for a long time despised it shall find themselves, in the end, justly despised.

What I write, I write from a treasury of experience, and I am certain that my book will be a welcome jewel to many, even if it is a thorn in the side and a sting to the very heart of others. Let them fret and vent their anger <note>original: "break their spleen"</note>; they can never prevail against what I have written here.

Are not our leaders of *Zoan* <note>A biblical reference to Isaiah 19:11, where the "princes of Zoan" are described as foolish advisors to Pharaoh</note> fools? Yes, indeed, while like primitive *Indians* <note>A common 17th-century comparison to Indigenous Americans, whom Europeans perceived as trading valuable resources for cheap trinkets</note> they adore glass, beads, copper bracelets, and trivial mirrors, all while despising gold and pearls.

They magnify and praise their method of medicine in defiance of any other way—a method that would make *Democritus* laugh and *Heraclitus* cry <note>Two ancient Greek philosophers; Democritus was known as the "laughing philosopher" and Heraclitus as the "weeping philosopher" because of their differing views on the folly of humanity</note> to see men (reputed to be wise) surrendered to such open folly. Meanwhile, poor afflicted souls languish daily and hourly, cheated of both their money and their lives by those who, while pretending to care for and cure them, can never do more than use a *clyster-pipe* <term>an enema syringe</term> or an *urinal* <term>a vessel used to inspect urine for medical diagnosis</term>. To these, they add the butcheries of *bloodletting* <note>original: *phlebotomy*</note>, *scarification* <term>the practice of making small incisions in the skin</term>, *blistering* <note>original: *vesication*</note>, and *purging* <note>original: *purgation*; the use of strong laxatives</note>, along with the foolishness of *barley broths*, *juleps*, and *cordials*. Every *confectioner* is better stocked with these than the *pharmacist* <note>original: *apothecary*</note>, as they usually make candies and preserves with better sugar. Oh, the times! Oh, the customs! <note>original: *Proh Tempora! Oh Mores!*; a famous Latin exclamation by the Roman orator Cicero expressing despair at the state of society</note>

<meta>This page continues George Starkey’s critique of the medical establishment. After condemning the arrogance of traditional physicians, he shifts focus toward the few who are willing to learn and begins to introduce the alchemical secrets of preparing mineral-based medicines.</meta>

<page-num>88</page-num>

Yet certainly there is a remnant (although only a small one) who sincerely care for and seek after "the better part," choosing it as Mary did; and it shall never be taken from them. <note>A reference to the biblical story in Luke 10:42, where Mary of Bethany chooses to listen to spiritual teaching rather than focus on domestic chores.</note> To them, as a friend and brother, I intend and direct these lines, and they will listen to and embrace my advice.

And as for the rest, this is all that I (at present) have to say to them: let them beware, lest by being too stubbornly attached to their established method, they neglect and overlook the opportunity to be better instructed. Although they may perhaps feel it is beneath them to be taught by me, this pride will only bring greater confusion and condemnation upon them later, when they see the list of all those they have either killed or allowed to die through their prideful spirit and refusal to be taught.

But to return to our purpose, from which we have digressed a little—namely, the mystery of preparing medicines—anyone who takes responsibility for people's lives and wishes to carry out that duty conscientiously <note>original: "conscionably"</note> must pay close attention.

Therefore, let him learn to strip mineral sulfurs <note>In 17th-century alchemy, "sulfur" was considered one of the three fundamental principles of matter, representing the soul or the combustible, active part of a substance.</note> of their foreign <note>original: "peregrine"</note> and malignant toxicity <note>original: "virulency"</note>. In these minerals, once prepared and corrected, he shall find medicines that will master all diseases equally, just as the noble Van Helmont <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644) was a famous chemist and physician whose work laid the foundations for modern chemistry and who Starkey greatly admired.</note> has written so excellently about them.

On this subject, I intend to be a bit more detailed and clear, for in truth, it is the very thing that ennobles and honors a physician; therefore, *studious reader*, I ask for your serious attention here.

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues George Starkey’s discussion on the preparation of medicines, specifically focusing on the three kingdoms of nature and why mineral-based remedies require chemical transformation to be effective and safe.</meta>

<page-num>89</page-num>
<header>and Illustrated.</header>

The kingdoms in which Nature works here on Earth are generally divided into three: the *Animal*, the *Vegetable*, and the *Mineral*.

In all of these, she has provided medicines for the infirmities of human life. The highest of these in the *Animal Kingdom* are found in *urine* and *blood*. The highest in the *Vegetable Kingdom* are found in the fixed salts and those which have been volatilized—either by essential *oils* or by other methods, as the artist’s experience shall guide him. In the *Mineral Kingdom*, the medicinal part consists of the *sulfurs* and the salts. These are, as I might say, the "couches of medicinal gifts" <note>original Latin: *Dotum medicinalium tori*. This suggests that the sulfurs and salts are the foundations or containers where the healing powers reside.</note>. The <term>mercuries</term> <note>original: ☿es (the symbol for Mercury). In alchemy, "Mercury" refers to one of the three prime principles of matter, representing the spirit or volatile essence.</note> of metals are closed, uniform substances that will not interact with us; they are like totally estranged essences, tightly locked up, and they do not work "except by means of their sulfurs" <note>original Latin: *Nisi Sulphurum intuitu*</note>—that is, with respect and reference to their sulfur components.

On this subject, and on the superior status of mineral and metallic medicines over those from animals or vegetables, *Van Helmont* <note>Jean Baptiste van Helmont (1580–1644) was a famous Flemish chemist and physician whose theories on "iatrochemistry" (chemical medicine) were highly influential during this period.</note> has written extensively. I will not repeat what he has already stated, as that would only burden the reader rather than educate them.

But *sulfurs* in their natural state are either locked away too tightly to be opened by the *archeus* <note>The "archeus" was a concept in Paracelsian and Helmontian medicine referring to the internal "vital spirit" or governing energy that directed the body's functions, particularly digestion.</note> of our stomach, and so they do not provide the desired help or yield their true power if taken in their raw form. Furthermore, many of them are toxic and harmful in their crude state. Therefore, they require "opening" through chemical preparation, so that their hidden virtues may be revealed and their inherent toxicity may be corrected.

<summary>Starkey explains that while medicines exist in the animal, vegetable, and mineral kingdoms, mineral-based "sulfurs" are the most potent but require chemical "opening" to unlock their virtues and remove their natural toxicity.</summary>

<page-num>90</page-num>

# Pyrotechny Asserted

The greatest and most formal method of doing this is by using the liquor *Alkahest* <note>The *Alkahest* was a hypothetical "universal solvent" sought by alchemists, capable of reducing any body to its primary liquid form.</note>. The secondary method—which follows it in effectiveness—is by using volatile *alkalies* <note>Original: *Alcalies*. In this context, these are alkaline salts that can be vaporized by heat.</note>. I have already given some brief touches on this by way of an *Essay* in the first part of this chapter. I shall now further illustrate and expand upon what I began there, and so bring this chapter to a conclusion.

In operations involving metals, this liquor <note>Referring to the volatile alkaline solvent.</note> may very well take the place of the *Great Solvent* <note>Another name for the Alkahest.</note>. In its absence, it may serve a *Son of Art* <note>A traditional term for a skilled practitioner or initiate of alchemy.</note> to perform his dissolutions of most (or all) *concretes* <note>Solid, composite substances or minerals.</note>, and the vaporization of the <term>sulfurs</term> <note>In alchemy, "sulfur" represents the soul or flammable, medicinal essence of a substance, not just the chemical element.</note> of inferior metals and minerals.

As for the supreme metals—namely *Gold* and *Silver*, and their equal in consistency, *Mercury* <note>original: ☿</note>—I would be doing an injustice to the truth if I did not confess that in the preparation of these, this *substitute key* <note>original: *succedaneous Key*</note> falls far short of the dignity of the liquor *Alkahest*. And yet, its effects on these bodies are such that it deserves the praise of being called a noble *dissolvent*, rather than a vulgar <term>corrosive</term> <note>A "dissolvent" was thought to gently pull a substance apart into its natural components, while a "corrosive" (like strong acid) was seen as violently destroying the substance's internal structure.</note>.

For if *Gold* or *Silver* is dissolved in this, the solvent itself—by acting on them in a dissolving manner—is coagulated into a volatile *salt*. When the <term>phlegm</term> <note>The watery, inactive byproduct of a chemical reaction.</note> (produced by this coagulation and by the liquor spending its strength in dissolving the bodies) is evaporated, it will crystallize in a cool place.

If you <term>sublime</term> <note>To heat a solid until it turns into vapor and then collect it as it becomes solid again.</note> this volatile *salt* three or four times from the <term>calx</term> <note>The powder or residue left after a metal has been dissolved or roasted.</note> of the dissolved *Gold*, you shall find that, besides being endowed with the virtues of Gold <note>original: ☉ (the symbol for the Sun/Gold)</note>, it will carry up with it a volatile <term>tincture</term> <note>The medicinal color or essence of the metal.</note> of the Gold <note>original: ☉</note>, leaving the residue very pale.

Indeed, by a method not difficult for an artist expert in *Pyrotechny* <note>The art of using fire for chemical and alchemical processes.</note>, the elements of the *Gold* will be

<meta>catchword: dif-</meta>

<summary>The author explains how volatile alkalies can serve as a substitute for the universal solvent "Alkahest" to dissolve metals and extract their medicinal essences (sulfurs), specifically describing the process of extracting a "tincture" from gold.</summary>
<keywords>Alkahest, volatile alkalies, Son of Art, Gold, Silver, Mercury, dissolution, sulfur, sublimation, tincture, Pyrotechny</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of chemical solvents, specifically comparing the volatile alkaline "spirit" to the universal solvent Alkahest in the extraction of medicinal "sulfurs" from Gold, Silver, and Mercury.</meta>

<page-num>91</page-num>
<header>and Illustrated.</header>

...dissolved and made separable from one another, just as in the process using the *Alkahest* <note>The *Alkahest* was a hypothetical "universal solvent" sought by alchemists, capable of reducing any body to its primary liquid form.</note>. However, there is a significant difference: this liquor loses its strength with each dissolution, becoming solidified <note>original: "coagulated"</note> whenever it is put to work. By casting off a <term>phlegm</term> <note>original: "Flegm"; in early chemistry, this refers to a watery, tasteless, or inactive byproduct.</note>, its quantity decreases with every operation, a loss that the liquor *Alkahest* does not suffer.

Thus, regarding the refined <note>original: "glorified"</note> sweet *sulfur* <note>In alchemy, "sulfur" refers to the internal essence, soul, or medicinal property of a metal.</note> of Gold <note>original: ☉</note> and Silver <note>original: ☽</note>, anyone who can control these volatile alkalies may obtain those essences through their *spirit* without needing the liquor *Alkahest*. Although the *Alkahest* works faster, more easily, and without losing its own power, this *spirit* is much quicker and easier to acquire. Anyone who knows the secret of making it may make as much of it as they desire.

But concerning Mercury <note>original: ☿</note>, which by this art can be prepared for incredible uses (such as in medicine): first, the *sulfur* of Antimony <note>original: ♁</note>, or of the vitriol <note>A sulfate or crystalline substance derived from a metal.</note> of Mercury, or of the "male metal" <note>original: *metallus masculus*</note>—which is *Augurellus’s* "sulfur glaure" <note>Referring to Giovanni Aurelio Augurello, an Italian alchemical poet; "sulfur glaure" refers to a specific refined sulfur mentioned in his work.</note> according to *Van Helmont* <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644), a pioneering Flemish chemist and physician.</note>—must be turned into vapor <note>original: "volatized"</note> using the *spirit* of volatile salt of tartar. These two should then form a single liquor. In this liquor, let <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: *Argent Vive*</note> be dissolved. Remove the phlegm by evaporation until it is dry, then add more of the spirit. Repeat this process of dissolving <note>original: "resolve"</note> until the Mercury no longer solidifies any of the spirit, but the spirit remains as strong as when it was first added. 

In this way, the Mercury in these solutions is embraced by the spiritualized sulfur within the spirit. They are joined so closely that they will not separate except under conditions as difficult as those required to separate the Mercury of a metallic body from its own sulfur. ## CHAPTER 3.
### Concerning the Volatile Salts of Herbs and Their Virtue.

Having completed our exploration of *alkalies* <term>alkalies</term> <note>In this context, basic salts often derived from the ashes of plants or from minerals, used as solvents.</note> so far as to give a short view of their virtue in relation to *mineral solutions*, let us now proceed to discover their use and serviceability when applied to *vegetables* <note>In 17th-century usage, "vegetables" refers to the entire plant kingdom.</note>: specifically their preparation, correction, purification, and the enhancement <note>original: "Exaltation"</note> of their potency.

For *plants* possess a most admirable and excellent efficacy. Although they are secondary to *minerals*, they are so noble in virtue that Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals in medicine.</note> boasts—and not in vain—of several most excellent cures performable by a single herb, if it is duly prepared. He uses *wormwood* <note>original: "Wormwood"</note> as an instance, affirming that he knows perfectly how to cure many (even the most desperate) diseases with it.

Oh, the care and love of the Almighty for poor mortal man! But shame upon the pride and arrogance of our lordly *doctors* <note>A critique of the university-trained physicians of the era who often rejected chemical medicine.</note> who, like Tantalus <note>In Greek mythology, Tantalus was punished with eternal hunger and thirst; water and fruit remained just out of his reach.</note>, are parched with thirst in the middle of a river and tormented with hunger despite having so many beautiful apples ready to brush against their noses. But let not their pride and sloth seduce any "son of art" <note>A term for a dedicated student or practitioner of alchemy and chemistry.</note> to choose their wretched, lazy, and unconscionable life. This lifestyle makes them a laughingstock <note>original: "by-word"</note> to every common laborer <note>original: "Porter"</note>, yet they are not moved by this at all as long as they can get money—even if it results in the ruin of families and lives.

Regarding this preparation of herbs, the noble Van Helmont <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644), a foundational chemist and physician.</note> speaks in his *Pharmacy Shop* <note>original Latin: *Pharmacopolium*</note> and his *Dispensary* <note>original Latin: *Dispensatorium*</note>...

He explains how they should prepare *simples* <term>simples</term> <note>Individual medicinal herbs or plants used as a single ingredient.</note> that possess great power—not by weakening them <note>original: "castrating"</note>, nor by cluttering them with other ingredients by grinding everything together into a random mixture. Instead, he advises adding a *ferment* <term>ferment</term> <note>A substance used to induce a chemical change or "ripening" in the medicine, similar to yeast in bread-making.</note> to extract the hidden power: by neutralizing their harmful qualities <note>original: "virulency"</note>, by swapping one quality for another, or by combining the right ingredients to awaken new properties that did not exist in the plant's raw state.

If this advice were well-heeded and followed, it would quickly destroy those slaughterhouses <note>original: "Shambles of Butchery"</note> known as *apothecaries' shops*. These shops have been more fatal to humanity than the sword ever was. Because of them, multitudes have died—either from the absolute toxicity <note>original: "virulency"</note> of their so-called "medicines" (which are actually real poisons, and are only called "remedies" sarcastically <note>original: "ironically"</note>), or for want of the genuine help that cannot be found in their shops.

Correcting these shops is impossible. They have been established for so long that they seem to have gained a permanent legal right <note>original: "Prescription"</note> over nature for their future existence. I will not, therefore, attempt the impossible <note>original: "wash a Black-moor," an archaic idiom for a futile task</note> by trying to reform them, but will instead teach the honest and hardworking how to make better preparations.

First, then, let the industrious *chemist* <note>original: "Artist"</note> know that by using the *fixed salt* <term>fixt Salt</term> <note>A stable salt obtained from the ashes of a plant after burning.</note> of any *herb*, any *volatile oil* may be transformed along with the *alkali* into a *volatile essential salt*. Along the way, it dissolves everything it finds stubbornly sticking to the *vessels* through which it passes. Since it is *healing* <note>original: "Balsamical"</note> due to the *oils* which are turned into salt within it, it reaches as far into the body as any *medicine* of any power possibly can.

Moreover, because it is made of *plant-based* <note>original: "Vegetal"</note> and not *mineral* components, it works its way even into the building blocks of our body and reaches the "fountain of animal life"—a feat denied to any *apothecary's drugs*.

For whatever reaches the *balsam of life* <term>Balsam of Life</term> <note>In Paracelsian medicine, a vital internal fluid or spirit that preserves the body from decay.</note> must be a *salt*, since *blood* (the seat of life) is saline. The *urine*—which is waste separated from the *blood*—is likewise saline, as is our *sweat*, and even the very *tears* of the eyes. Nothing can be admitted beyond the limits of the first digestion unless it is of this saline nature. Therefore, all *herbs* and *vegetables* in the *stomach* are either digested or they are not. If they are digested, they lose what they were, being turned into *chyle* <term>chyle</term> <note>A milky fluid containing proteins and fats that is produced in the small intestine during digestion.</note>. They become a completely new substance through this total transformation <note>original: "transmutation"</note>. By this process, if they were *medicinal* before, they are stripped of all that power before they can even be admitted to...

...the second digestion. And even if they may retain a few qualities of the *Great Requirement* <note>original: "Magnum oportet"; likely referring to a powerful or necessary remedy</note>, yet these are too feeble to uproot a disease settled in any vessel of the second digestion, much less of the third <note>Early modern medicine divided the body's processing of food and nutrients into three stages: the stomach (1st), the liver and veins (2nd), and the tissues/organs (3rd).</note>.

But if what is taken in is not digested, it is then cast out in the stool <note>original: "draught"</note>. This happens if, because of its stickiness <note>original: "gummousness"</note> or indigestibility, it will not allow itself to be softened <note>original: "macerated"</note> by the ferment of the stomach. It may even be rejected both ways if the toxin has a sticky and highly fermenting power. These substances never cure except by accident, as I fully revealed in my book *Nature's Explication, etc.*, and therefore I shall not repeat it here.

Therefore, the intended cure claimed by these methods is useless. The idle promises that syrups can reach and heal the **Lungs** are absurd, considering that the **Liver**, which is much nearer, cannot be reached by either syrups or herbal drinks <note>original: "Decoctions"</note>, regardless of what the **Galenists** <note>Followers of the traditional medical system of Galen, which relied heavily on herbal mixtures and the "four humors."</note> persuade their deluded patients to believe.

But ***Salts***, being of a different nature, do not break down in a digestible way by the ferment of the stomach. Instead, retaining their potency, they pass on to the **mesenteric** and **mesaraic veins** <note>Veins that carry nutrients from the digestive tract to the liver.</note>. In their passage, they dissolve whatever unnatural blockages they find, and so become **cleansing** <note>original: "abstersive"</note>, **diuretic** (promoting urine), and **diaphoretic** (promoting sweat).

This is evident in **sea salt**. After passing through the digestion of the stomach and the **duodenum**, it is received into the mesaraic veins. It travels along with the semi-digested blood or *clotted blood* <note>original: "cruor"</note> until it reachers the urinary separation. There, it remains fundamentally the same as it was when it was first taken in, and from there it is separated out in its entire substance, form, and potency.

But **alkalies** <note>original: "Alcalies"; basic substances like lye or potash</note> in the stomach are neutralized <note>original: "satiated"</note> in their **alkaline** <note>original: "lixiviate"; relating to the properties of lye</note> qualities by the acidity of the stomach. These then pass on to the "urinary digestion," where they take on the qualities of urine, but they increase the amount of **fixed salt** <note>Salts that do not evaporate when heated.</note> in the urine, making it different from how it was when first ingested.

However, if they are first made **volatile** <note>Capable of evaporating or being absorbed easily into the bloodstream.</note> through an inseparable union with **essential oils** until both become a single salt, this substance then passes through all the body's stages of digestion where any blockage <note>original: "coagulation"</note> might have formed. This prepared salt dissolves and expels these blockages, partly through urine and partly through sweat. Because it is essential and volatile, it can reach parts of the body where plain alkalies, in their own isolated nature, could find no entry.

To provide a clear visual demonstration of what has been said regarding the vomit-inducing and laxative qualities found in some plants—to prove that these qualities stem from a toxic principle—I will give examples of a few obvious preparations.

Take black or white **Hellebore** <note>A toxic plant used historically as a powerful purgative.</note>, or the juice of **wild cucumbers** <note>Known as the "squirting cucumber," it was famous for its violent laxative effects.</note>, or any of the most harshly aggressive plants. If they are prepared with any **fixed alkaline salt**, they lose both their vomit-inducing and laxative qualities. Instead, they become **diuretic** <note>promoting urination</note> and **diaphoretic** <note>inducing sweat</note>. In fact, after this preparation, a person can take a double or triple dose without any distress, whereas even half that amount would have been deadly before.

I would like to ask any **Galenist** <note>A traditional physician who followed the ancient teachings of Galen, often opposing chemical medicine.</note> whether or not the medicinal value <note>original: "virtue"</note> of such **simples** <term>Simples</term> <note>Individual medicinal herbs or plants used as ingredients in medicine.</note> lies in their ability to cause vomiting or purging. If it does, then what happened to that power after this simple preparation? There is no escaping this demon-
