As for the demonstration, the facts of a simple experiment will convince them; it is the underlying reason that I now demand from them. Perhaps they will think to respond with their old objection <note>original: "cavil"</note>: namely, that **chemistry** <note>original: "Chymistry"</note>, by torturing substances with **fire**, exhausts <note>original: "exantlate"</note> the noble virtues of **simples** <term>simples</term> <note>medicinal herbs or plants used on their own rather than in a mixture</note> through long preparation. They claim it imposes on the credulous **common people** <note>original: "Vulgar"</note> by offering a weakened <note>original: "castration"</note> version of these herbs instead of enhancing their power.

I admit that boiling <note>original: "decoctions"</note> and long digestion alter things significantly. For example, the **cassava** root (well known in the **West Indies**) is a deadly poison to humans or animals when raw, as many have discovered to the cost of killing their **pigs** and **poultry**. However, when it is thoroughly baked, it makes a very pleasant and wholesome bread eaten by many thousands. Similarly, raw **asarum** roots <note>also known as wild ginger</note>—whether in powder or infusion—cause very violent vomiting; yet, after boiling in water for only half an hour, they become an excellent **diuretic** and an approved remedy for slow, lingering fevers.

Experience also shows that in what is commonly called the **Extract of Rhubarb**, an ounce of the extract will not purge the bowels as effectively as a single dram given in powder form. If anyone attributes the reason for this to the **vanishing of the herb's power in the fire**, I shall disprove that claim with an undeniable proof involving the **fire**. 

Suppose an infusion of **rhubarb** is made in a **retort** <term>retort</term> <note>a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling substances</note>, to which a **receiver** is attached so that not a drop of moisture can evaporate out of the retort without being caught in the **recipient**. Let fresh liquid be poured on and then decanted until the **tincture** <note>the colored medicinal solution</note> stops forming and the remaining pulp becomes as tasteless as the **dust of a rotten fence post** and has just as little effect. Then, let the moisture be distilled off until it reaches the consistency of a **rob** <term>rob</term> <note>a thickened fruit juice or syrup, reduced by boiling</note>. This must be done with such a gentle heat that there is not the slightest danger of an **empyreuma** <term>empyreuma</term> <note>a burnt smell or taste produced when organic matter is heated too much</note>. Of this **rob** or...

Take an **extract**, giving a proportional amount to what would be pulled from two drams <note>original: "drachms"</note> of **rhubarb** <note>original: "Rhabarb"</note>, and dissolve it in its proper amount of the water distilled from that herb. Even so, these two drams will not cause nearly as much purging as a mere half-dram that was never extracted, but only ground into powder and taken raw <note>original: "crude"</note>.

From this, it is evident that without losing even a single grain of the substance, the laxative <note>original: "purgative"</note> quality can be significantly reduced simply by boiling it over a fire, even without creating a **burnt taste** <term>empyreuma</term> <note>original: "Empyrheum"; the scorched smell or taste resulting from overheating organic matter</note> during the process. Indeed, if the water that distills off is **redistilled** <term>cohobated</term> <note>original: "cohobated"; the process of pouring a distilled liquid back onto its residue and distilling it again</note> repeatedly—by returning it to the vessel when half has distilled over—the **vomit-inducing** <note>original: "Emetick"</note> and **laxative** <note>original: "Cathartick"</note> qualities can be completely neutralized in a short time. However, by adding a liquid made from an **alkaline salt** <note>original: "Alcalizate Salt"</note>, this can be achieved in just half an hour through boiling.

Now, where has this medicinal power gone? Has it vanished? If one answers that it is the nature of fire **to create new products** <note>original: "Nova producta facere"</note>, I will dismiss that answer as easily as it was offered. We can achieve the same effect without using any cooking fire at all.

Therefore, take any **plant matter** <note>original: "Vegetal"</note> that has been finely powdered and **sifted** <note>original: "searced"</note>. Mix it with an **alkali**—for example, **salt of tartar** <term>salt of tartar</term> <note>potassium carbonate, a common chemical used by early modern scientists</note>. Add enough white wine (or any other liquid) to give it the consistency of **dough** <note>original: "Dow"</note> or a **poultice** <note>original: "Pultis"</note>. Let it sit so that the alkaline salt can penetrate to the center of the powder. As it dries, moisten it again, or keep it compressed and covered in a **small glass jar** <note>original: "Gally-glass"</note> so that it does not dry out. Within six weeks at most, the vomit-inducing or laxative quality will be completely gone, yet without any more loss of taste, color, or smell than if it had simply been kept damp—

...moistened with clean water, nor even that much, for such a moistening would have brought about a fermentation, which is prevented by the **alkali** <note>original: "Alcali"</note>. Thus, the **specific** qualities remain in this final operation (as shown by the taste and the smell, which are enhanced rather than ruined), but the vomit-inducing and laxative qualities are destroyed. Consequently, it is rightly concluded that these harsh effects are not part of the herb's true medicinal power <note>original: "vertue of the Simples"</note> but are separate from its substance and specific qualities, which remain intact even when the former are lost.

And here, honest reader, observe the flaws <note>original: "rottenness"</note> of the **Galenic** system <note>original: "structure"; referring to the traditional medical teachings of Galen</note>. Those who follow it look only at the vomit-inducing or laxative "venom" in herbs of excellent medicinal power. This "venom" might well be compared to the flaming sword in the hand of the Cherub who guards the path to the Tree of Life. Thus, this poisonous appearance often hides the most noble and admirable qualities in many medicinal herbs <term>simples</term> <note>original: "Simples"; plants used as medicine in their natural state</note>. Because of this poisonous exterior, they cannot gain entry into the more private chambers of nature. A strict watch is kept over these chambers, such that the **Archeus** <term>Archeus</term> <note>the internal "spirit" or vital force that governs the body's functions in Paracelsian medicine</note> will be enraged, the stomach will suffer convulsions (and all the nerves by a secondary reaction <term>deuteropathia</term> <note>original: "Deuteropathia"; a condition where one part of the body suffers in sympathy with another part that is primarily affected</note>), and the whole "little world" of the human body <term>microcosm</term> <note>original: "Microcosm"</note> will be thrown into a state of chaos <note>original: "hurly-burly"</note> rather than admit the "venom" of **hellebore** to enter the **mesaraic** <note>original: "Mesaraick"; relating to the veins of the intestines</note> or **mesenteric veins**.

Instead, the body rejects it all with loathing and detestation. But the excellent healthy constitution <term>crasis</term> <note>original: "crasis"; the specific temperament or healthy balance of qualities in a substance</note> of which **hellebore** can deservedly boast is not to be obtained—indeed, it is scarcely even noticed—during this violent and disturbing reaction to the herb in its raw state <note>original: "crudity"</note>. If any of its beneficial effects on the spleen <note>original: "Splenetick"</note> or the head <note>original: "Cephalick"</note> happen to appear amidst this turmoil, it is only as the **sun** shows a glimmer of its beams through the dark veil of a thick black cloud or a misty fog.

<page-num>102</page-num>
<header>Pyrotechny Asserted</header>

But once this *veil* is removed, the true, noble, and specific virtues of the plant appear. This applies to any other harsh *plant* <note>original: "Vegetable"</note> which the *Galenists*—due to their vague and obscure methods—cannot bear to look at directly. However, these virtues are revealed and taught to us through the discipline of fire, which is our highly praised *chemistry* <note>original: "Pyrotechny"; literally the "art of fire"</note>.

By degrees, fire applied through boiling in liquid <note>original: "humid decoction"</note> erases the mark of poison within *plants*, according to the very true maxim: *Every plant poison is softened by boiling, and through long cooking, it vanishes* <note>original: "Omne Vegetabile venenum coquendo mitescit, diutina vero coctione evanescit"</note>. Fire does this not by creating something new (as happens when *solid substances* <note>original: "Concretes"</note> are distilled), but by ripening the rawness to which the poison is attached. This is according to the true maxim: *Every poison is bound to the final life-force of the solid substance* <note>original: "Omne venenum, vitæ concreti ultimæ alligatur"</note>. 

Even *arsenic* itself loses its poison if it is "fixed" by *saltpeter* <term>saltpeter</term> <note>potassium nitrate, used here to stabilize volatile substances</note>—meaning it is forced to endure the boiling heat of fire. Otherwise, it "flies" <note>evaporates or turns to vapor</note>; it will not withstand the trial of fire. In arsenic, the poison is "material"—meaning it is physical and corrosive. In *plants*, however, the poison is "ideal," fermentative, and spiritual. This spiritual poison hates boiling, and it especially hates the stable purity of an *alkaline salt* <note>original: "Alcalizate Salt"</note>. Fire stamps its character and impression upon this salt so thoroughly that it may well be called the *son of fire* <note>original: "Ignis filius"</note>, which I have elsewhere named the *tail of Vulcan* <note>original: "Cauda Vulcani"; referring to the leftover essence or power of the fire, Vulcan being the Roman god of fire</note>.

Thus, even our *grain*, our *meat*, *fish*, *beer*, and everything else are harmful if they are not well-baked or boiled. Although eating them might provide some nutrition, it is poor nutrition. For evidence, I point to young women who eat raw oatmeal or children who eat raw fruit. In the same way, the *medicinal* power in *plants* is, at best, weighed down by an unwholesome rawness, of which it is as rash to

It is as difficult to make a medicine without boiling <note>original: "Decoction"</note> as it is to eat young cucumbers <note>original: "Cucumers"</note> without pickling them first. Furthermore, many plants have a poison attached to their rawness <note>original: "crudity"</note>, which provokes the *internal life force* <term>Archeus</term> <note>in Paracelsian medicine, the "Archeus" is the vital spirit or "internal chemist" that governs the body's functions</note> to rage and fury. Consequently, the disease is perhaps disturbed, and a faint glimmer <note>original: "light Irradiation"</note> of the herb's <note>original: "simple"</note> beneficial power appears through the dark cloud of physical distress <note>original: "Anxiety"</note>. 

When the patient suffers *vomiting* and *diarrhea* <note>original: "Loosness of the Belly"</note>, the foolish doctor notices and cries out, "*O, what an excellent medicine!*" He does not consider that the medicine arrived as an enemy that Nature refused to admit. Instead, Nature shut the doors of her parlors and closets against it and, gathering her forces, tried to throw it out of the first room (the stomach) or at least kick it out the back door (the bowels). 

Even so, the medicine left a tiny spark of its hidden virtue behind. Imagine what it could have achieved if it had been stripped of its hostile harmfulness <note>original: "Malignity"</note>! If that were done, Nature would have been comfortable with it and led it from room to room and closet to closet, all the way to the entrance of the "privy chamber"—the most private room, where no one is allowed access except the Prince or his closest friends. Such a welcome guest is far superior to a "reconciled enemy"; these guests are what we call *aromatic balsamic essences*.

In short, boiling in liquid <note>original: "Humid Decoction"</note> or any "digestion" using a non-burning heat may ripen raw qualities <note>original: "Crudities"</note>, but it does not change the essential nature <note>original: "Species"</note> of the substance. However, if the heat is raised above a ripening <note>original: "pepantick"</note> or fermenting heat, it becomes a decomposing <note>original: "putrefactive"</note> heat (wherever the substance is capable of it). This heat is the source of total transformation <note>original: "Transmutation"</note>. This is seen in meat or moist herbs kept in a fever-like heat <note>original: "febrile Heat"</note>, such as the heat found inside a horse's belly or in horse dung, which is similar to the heat of a person in a feverish state <note>original: "feverish temper"</note>. This heat triggers a fermentation that causes a total transformation, just as a separating or burning heat causes the death of

### Pyrotechny Asserted

...the *Compound*, and by consequence a new *Product* which is the **Son of Fire** <note>original: "Filius Ignis"</note>. But the inherent vital power <note>original: "seminal Virtue"</note> of the solid substance <note>original: "Concrete"</note> is not totally extinguished except by open burning. In a closed heat used for separation, the parts are worked upon in a confused way, partly retaining the **middle life** <note>original: "Vita media"; a transitional state of existence for a substance</note> of the former solid, but being significantly altered from their former **specific forms** <note>the unique essential nature that makes a thing what it is</note> through the active impression of **Vulcan** <note>the Roman god of fire and metalworking, used here to represent the intense heat of the alchemist’s furnace</note>, whose character they receive.

Whereas in a moist heat, the **nature** <note>original: "Species"</note> is unaltered, even though the raw impurities <note>original: "Crudities"</note> are taken away by <term>Decoction</term> <note>the process of boiling a substance down to extract its essence or medicinal properties</note>; yet this may happen without the loss of a single grain of substance, as the essential properties of the solid matter remain regardless. For example, beef, pork, mutton, fish, or fowl are not changed by boiling (except from raw to cooked); the specific identity still remains. Only the color, taste, smell, etc., which belonged to the rawness, are changed into others that follow the boiling process, yet they stay within the bounds of the same species. Even if the boiling were continued until a **jelly** <note>original: "Gelatina"</note> or a **concentrated broth** <note>original: "Jus Consummatum"</note> is produced, these jellies or broths keep their inherent and distinct properties. Thus, chicken broth, veal broth, or mutton broth are clearly distinguishable from one another. They are not radically changed except through the help of a ferment (which is not found at temperatures higher than a feverish or **digestive heat** <note>original: "Pepantick Heat"</note>) or by a burning degree of fire. Such intense fire is the **artificial death of the compound** <note>original: "Mors Artificialis Compositi"; "artificial" here refers to the chemist's art or skill</note> and would prove to be the **extinguisher of the seeds** <note>original: "Seminum Extinctor"; the destruction of the substance’s essential life-force or reproductive power</note> if it were allowed to act openly with a free flame or burning.

To apply what has been said to our purpose: I would ask the arrogant <term>Galenist</term> <note>a follower of the traditional medical system of Galen, which relied on balancing "humors" rather than the chemical approach advocated by the author</note>, what he can say—

<page-num>105</page-num>

...to support the logic of this argument: everyone in **Europe** knows that **wheat** is a wholesome grain, yet it does not lack its **harmful raw impurities** <note>original: "Malignant Crudities"</note> until it is baked. Any baker can tell you this, and every housewife can learn it for herself. The same applies to meat, fish, and everything else. Even though some tender herb leaves are used and considered healthy when raw, this applies only to healthy stomachs. For those with weak constitutions or frail health <note>original: "crazy Healths"</note>, even these leaves and delicious fruits require boiling, baking, or stewing. This proves that they are more wholesome when prepared in this way.

Only the **Galenist** <note>a follower of the ancient Greek physician Galen, who favored herbal mixtures and generally opposed the "chemical" medicines produced by the followers of Paracelsus</note> is content to use his medicines in their raw state—such as **elaterium** <note>a powerful purgative made from the juice of the squirting cucumber</note>, **jalap** <note>original: "Zalap"; a cathartic drug derived from the roots of a Mexican climbing plant</note>, **mechoacan** <note>a root used as a laxative, named after a province in Mexico</note>, **bryony** <note>original: "Briony"; a plant with toxic, purgative properties often used in traditional medicine</note>, and others. Besides being raw, these also contain a harmful **toxin** <note>original: "Venome"</note> that cannot be removed without prior preparation.

Additionally, all **solid plant matter** <note>original: "Vegetable Concretes"</note>—or at least most of it—has its **essential temperament** <note>original: "Crasis"</note> or **healing power** <note>original: "Virtue"</note> enclosed in a gummy, sticky substance, like a nut in its shell. In herbs or grains created for human nourishment, this substance is what the **digestive system** <note>original: "Digestive Faculty"</note> works upon. If this substance is removed through fermentation, wholesome drinks can still be made from them, yet they lose their former **nutritional value** <note>original: "Nutritive Faculty"</note> as food. This is evident in wine and beer; while they were still barley and grapes, they were suitable for food and nourishment.

But as soon as that sticky, viscous nature was made volatile by fermentation and thus formally transformed into a new substance, what was once food became a wholesome drink. It then had the power to refresh and cheer the **spirits** <note>the "vital spirits" or subtle fluids believed to circulate through the body and govern mood and energy</note> if taken moderately, or to dull and stupify them if used in excess. These effects were not present in the original grain or grapes.

From this, it is clear that when **chemistry** <note>original: "Art"</note>, by the introduction of a **catalyst** <note>original: "Ferment"</note>, has made a **plant substance** <note>original: "Vegetal Concrete"</note> volatile and fundamentally altered its **thickness** <note>original: "Viscosity"</note>, its **spirit** (which was produced from that gummy substance) is no longer subject to the stomach's digestion. Instead, it is merely separated there and spiritually gains access to the heart and the fine arterial **fibers**. These are the channels that convey spirits from one vital part to another; their effect is warming, reviving, refreshing, and cheering, which they do more powerfully the more strong <note>original: "generous"</note> and spiritual the **liquid** is.

For whatever is digested in the stomach is first made into **chyle** <note>the milky fluid produced from food in the small intestine</note>, or an acidic **cream** <note>original: "Cremor"</note>. Afterward, this is transformed by the **catalyst** <note>original: "Ferment"</note> of the liver into a blood-based salt. Thus, the **blood** is salty; it is not then altered but only refined and sent to the heart. There, it is infused with a **spirit of life**, which is **Helmont’s** <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644), a Flemish physician and chemist who pioneered the study of gases and digestion</note> **vital breath** <note>original: "Aura Vitalis"</note>. Then the **liver blood** <note>original: "Cruor Hepaticus"; refers to venous blood</note> becomes **arterial blood** <note>original: "sanguis Arterialis"</note>, which is the conveyer of the vital spirits to the entire body. It moistens each part with a vital breath or **dew**, by which the spirits spent and impaired by the various **functions** of the body are repaired. This is the ultimate goal intended by Nature in her natural craving for food and drink.

For Nature does not, in its thirst, desire beer or wine as such, but as moisture to resupply the depleted **fluids** <note>original: "Latex"</note>. However, **wise chemistry** <note>original: "Provident Art"</note> has married **water** to a **spirit** that is familiar to Nature, so that at once the **thirst** may be quenched and the spirits cheered. But I discuss this more fully in my treatise titled *The Method and Mystery of Curing Diseases*, which I intend to publish very shortly and to which I refer the reader.

*First*, that all **plant compounds** <note>original: "Vegetal Concretes"</note> have a **gummy, sticky substance** <note>original: "gummous viscous Substance"</note>. In plants created for nourishment, this is the substance upon which the **catalyst** <note>original: "Ferment"</note> of the stomach acts, and from which it extracts **chyle** <note>the milky fluid produced from food in the small intestine</note>. This is apparent in all broths, extracts of grain or herbs, or the juice of fruits, which (once the watery part has evaporated) leave a **thick syrup** <note>original: "Rob"</note> or **extract** that is sticky and thick, with the consistency of tar. Although it is not **oily** <note>original: "pinguous"</note>, it is sticky and gummy.

*Secondly*, if this gummy substance is **vaporized** <note>original: "volatized"</note> by a catalyst, it produces an **alcoholic spirit** <note>original: "Vinous Spirit"</note>. This is essentially different from what it was before and is no longer a proper object for the stomach's catalyst to work on. Therefore, it is no longer nutritious as food, though it is refreshing as a spirit.

*Thirdly*, not all plants are intended for food. Some are resinous or woody, or otherwise of a nature too tough for the stomach’s catalyst to handle; these are rejected and may disrupt digestion but will never satisfy the appetite. Others have a harmful exterior, and so the stomach recoils from them.

*Fourthly*, whatever is digested is received into the **vital economy** <note>original: "Oeconomiam Vitalem"; the internal system and management of the body's life-sustaining processes</note>. If it brings a **foreign** <note>original: "peregrine"</note> quality into this system, it immediately becomes hostile and creates bad blood. It is fortunate if the harm is **purged** <note>original: "expiated"</note> through boils, scabs, and so on.

*Fifthly*, being rejected either by vomiting (if the hostility is more obvious), or by **bowel movement** <note>original: "Siege"</note> (if it is not so **toxic** <note>original: "virulent"</note>), it is conveyed as an enemy to the place of waste, where, when it comes to receive...

I say this to their shame, with hopes (if possible) to reform them.

The second deficiency in their medicines is the gummy, earthy residue <note>original: "gummous Terrestriety"</note> that accompanies all *plant-based substances* <note>original: "Vegetals"</note>, which they take no care to soften and overcome before the stomach is burdened with them.

I previously showed this gumminess by visual demonstration in all those fake preparations commonly called *extracts*, which is most evident in the *resin of jalap* <note>original: "Resina Zalappæ"</note>, *scammony* <note>original: "Scammonii," a resinous juice obtained from the root of Convolvulus scammonia, used as a purgative.</note>, etc. I do not argue about what the extraction is made with—whether with water, or with a distilled "phlegm" or "dew of vitriol" <note>Vitriol refers to sulfuric acid or sulfates; distilled "phlegm" or "dew" refers to the watery parts produced during distillation.</note>, or any other distilled water, or with *spirit of wine* <note>alcohol/ethanol</note> (though that is the best of all those mentioned)—yet none of these separate the gummy, viscous parts from the pure, subtle, and salty parts. Thus, the stomach either finds a burden without benefit, or at least the benefit is so weighed down...

...is so weighed down with its burden, that the *Archeus* <note>In the medical theories of Paracelsus and van Helmont, the Archeus is the "internal chemist" or vital force that governs the body's functions.</note> does not reap the proper benefit of the medicine.

For as I mentioned before, the stomach will not let any gummy or viscous substance pass through the *pylorus* <note>The opening from the stomach into the small intestine.</note> and *duodenum* into the *mesaraics* <note>original: "Mesaraicks"; the mesenteric veins that carry nutrients from the intestines to the liver.</note>, nor will the *Archeus* of the second digestion suffer such abuse. Instead, the substance must be either concocted into chyle <note>The milky fluid of digested food.</note> and so received into the *economy of vital principles*—and consequently no longer be a *medicine*—or, if it is either too stubborn or too harmful to undergo that stomach digestion <note>original: "stomatical Maceration"</note>, it is cast out among the *excrements*. In this case, the *crasis* <note>original: "Crasis"; the specific medicinal essence or "temperament" of a substance.</note> (like the meat of a nut) never shows its effects, being clogged and made ineffectual by the gumminess which, like a shell, keeps its goodness from diffusing itself.

For the appetite of the stomach craves nothing but food and drink. Consequently, whatever it finds too thick for drink and unfit for food, it rejects as waste without further examination.

Most absurd, then, is the practice of many doctors who prescribe medicinal herbs in cock or chicken broth, or in jelly made from knuckles of veal. They do not consider that food and medicine are two different and distinct things; and while they think to trick nature with medicated broths, they pervert the digestion and prove the proverb true in their patients, causing them *to live medically—that is, to live miserably* <note>original: "Medicè, id est, miserè vivere."</note>.

For when the *Archeus* finds broths or jellies in the stomach—which are the usual and proper objects for its enzymes <note>original: "Ferment"</note> to work upon for the sake of nutrition—it begins to attempt digestion. But upon finding the deception <note>original: "Fucus"; a Latin term for a disguise, pretense, or cosmetic wash.</note>, it rejects everything, in effect crying out with the sons of the prophets, *"O doctor! there is death in the broth!"* <note>original: "O medice! mors est in jusculo!"; a parody of 2 Kings 4:40, where the prophets cry "O man of God, there is death in the pot!"</note> and be-

Being often so deluded, and its ferment <note>In the medical theory of the time, a "ferment" was a digestive juice or vital catalyst produced by the body's internal spirit.</note> exhausted in vain, the stomach becomes more wary afterward, rejecting all foods for the sake of those medicines which have so often deceived it. And if any of this chyle <note>The milky fluid of digested food.</note>, which contains an unnatural foreign quality, reaches the second digestion <note>The stage of digestion occurring after the stomach, usually associated with the liver and the creation of blood.</note>, consider what fermentations, excesses, obstructions, and disturbances it produces. Few who have undergone a *Galenical method* <note>The traditional medical system based on the teachings of Galen (2nd century AD), which used herbal mixtures to balance the body's "humors."</note> (for some *chronic disease*) can fail to tell the cost; they can read this lesson in themselves with "it is proven" <note>original: *Probatum est*</note> written upon their own bodies.

Lastly, we accuse and condemn the venomous malignancy of many *simples* <note>Raw, unrefined medicinal plants or herbs.</note>, which doctors most ignorantly call *medicines*, such as *scammony*, *elaterium* <note>A potent purgative derived from the squirting cucumber.</note>, *aaron* <note>The plant Arum maculatum, or cuckoopint.</note>, *asarum* <note>Wild ginger.</note>, *colocynth* <note>original: *Colocynthida*; a bitter, powerful laxative fruit.</note>, and many others which it would be tedious to list.

I easily grant and admit that under the mask of *toxicity* <note>original: *Virulency*</note>, the most noble *virtues* <note>The specific healing or "spiritual" powers of a substance.</note> are for the most part hidden (although it does not necessarily follow that this is always the case). Yet the *medicinal virtue* consists neither in the *emetic* <note>inducing vomiting</note> nor the *laxative power* of a simple plant, which it performs *as a poison* <note>original: *Qua venenum*</note>. Instead, the *specific excellence* is much more secretly hidden, and is not to be commanded except by a true and *philosophical preparation* <note>The chemical or alchemical process of refining a substance to remove its "dross" or toxins.</note>. Let fools admire those qualities as medicinal which are reduced by *boiling* <note>original: *Decoction*</note>, and by complete boiling are wholly taken away; we know that they are but so many toxins, and are side-effects of the *rawness* <note>original: *Crudity*</note> and *gummy stickiness* of such *poisonous substances* <note>original: *Virulent Concretes*</note>, of which no traces are to be found in the first *essence* <note>original: *Ens*; the fundamental, pure being or "seed" of a substance.</note>.

I yield, moreover, that there are many *simples* not intended by Nature for food that possess excellent virtue, such as those that are *sweat-inducing* <note>original: *Diaphoretick*</note>, like *carduus* <note>Blessed thistle.</note>, *chamomile flowers*, *rosemary*, *sage*, *wormwood*, and so on.

I shall conclude this chapter with a serious exhortation both to those who practice and those who stand in need of the art of medicine: *If the blind lead the blind, it is hazardous lest both fall into the ditch.*

I have heard (with blushing) the vain promises of many doctors—and those men of no mean reputation—how they will discourse on *obstructions*, *inflammations*, and *unnatural heat* of the *liver*. They promise to wash the liver (as a laundress washes foul linen) with their *cleansing agents* <note>original: *Abstersives*</note> and clear away blockages <note>original: deoppilate</note> with their *opening medicines* <note>original: *Apperitive Medicines*; drugs used to open the ducts or pores of the body</note>. Alas, these never reach further than the ordinary passage of waste <note>The digestive tract.</note>, unless they are those which have a *volatile alkali* <note>original: *Volatile Alcaly*</note>. These do indeed often do much good if duly applied, but much less than they otherwise would if they were rightly prepared. This preparation the following chapter will more largely discover.

## A *Concluding Appendix* concerning several Noble *Specific Remedies*, preparable by *Chemical Art* <note>original: *Pyrotechny*; literally "fire-art," referring to alchemical or chemical laboratory work.</note>, and *Substitutes* <note>original: *Succedaneous*</note> to the *Great Secrets* <note>original: *Grand Arcana*; universal or highly potent alchemical medicines.</note>.

**IN** the previous chapter (Kind Reader), I spoke generally about the application of alkalies to plants <note>original: *Vegetables*</note> for their refinement <note>original: Correction</note>, ripening, and preparation. Through this process, they become excellent tools in the hands of a careful physician for achieving (with God’s blessing) the cure of all kinds of diseases, although not every specific case of every disease.

We did this more generally in the previous chapter and gave an account of the raw <note>original: crude</note> imperfections that accompany all plants; of the gummy, earthy quality <note>original: *gummous Terrestriety*</note> that is mixed into all common infusions, extractions, or decoctions of milder medicinal herbs <note>original: *Vegetal Simples*; individual plants used for healing</note>; and of the malignant poison of other more intense herbs. These qualities render their use as remedies against diseases at best weak <note>original: Lame</note> and imperfect, and often dangerous and desperate.

We showed how no physical substance may be admitted to the second, and consequently to the third, stage of digestion until it is broken down <note>original: macerated</note> by the ferment of the first stage. Whatever is digested in this way becomes nutritional fluid <note>original: *Chyle*; the milky fluid produced in the small intestines during digestion</note> and is no longer a medicine. If any foreign <note>original: exotick</note> qualities make it unfit for nourishment, the *Archeus* <note>The internal "chemist" or vital force believed to govern the body's functions and distinguish between nutrients and toxins.</note>—who is God’s representative <note>original: Vicegerent</note> and quickly senses it—rejects it into the waste <note>original: Draught; the digestive waste stream</note>, where when it

I also made it clear how the stomach has no appetite for anything except to convert it into nourishment. This *nutritive substance* <note>original: *Nutritive Object*</note> is enclosed within the gummy matter of the plant. If the stomach digests it, it creates chyle <note>the milky fluid formed during digestion</note>, which is food, not *medicine*. If the plant material is unsuitable for digestion, the stomach rejects it, ignoring the *medicinal secrets* contained within, of which the stomach has no awareness.

I now come to the true and *philosophical preparation* <note>referring to chemical or alchemical processing</note> of real and true *medicines*, whose *nature* I shall briefly describe to satisfy the noble-minded reader.

First, then—in contrast to what I criticized in the *medicines of the Methodists* <note>referring to a school of medical thought that relied on traditional, often simpler, herbal remedies</note>—their raw imperfections are removed either by fermentation or by adding something with a fermenting power. ...is absolutely required for the proper preparation of medicines <note>original: Medicaments</note>. Secondly, fire is necessary because, although it does not formally transform a substance when boiling in liquid <note>original: in a humid Decoction</note>, it significantly alters the *qualities* of solid materials <note>original: Concretes</note>. This is especially true for substances that are fragrant <note>original: odorous</note> and whose essential constitution <note>original: *Crasis*</note> consists of a light essential oil <note>original: *Sulphur*</note>, such as *cinnamon*, *mace*, *nutmegs*, and so on.

In the preparation of these, if boiling is ever used, let it be done so that the fragrant and essential parts are preserved. In this way, when they are "re-married" and more deeply united with their own more solid <note>original: fixed</note> substances, they may both become a single *elixir* together.

Secondly, a proper preparation distinguishes between that which is gummy <note>original: Gummous</note> and that which is purely salty <note>original: Saline</note>. This is achieved either by separating them or by soaking the sticky, earthy matter <note>original: viscous *Terrestriety*</note> and, through a "secret digestion" <note>a controlled chemical heating process</note>, changing it into either a spiritual oil <note>original: *Sulphur*</note> or a dissolvable <note>original: liquable</note> *salt*. 

This is because salts and oils are merely the "beds of the seeds" <note>original: *Seminum tori*; the matrices or vessels that contain the life-force</note>—disguises in which the essential constitution <note>original: *Crasis*</note> of solid materials is masked. These forms can be transformed into one another in succession. 

For example, if the juice of grapes is boiled, the watery parts evaporate <note>original: exhaling</note> and the residue becomes a thick syrup <note>original: *Rob*</note>, which is gummy and sticky. Through fermentation, this is made volatile and becomes a spiritual oil <note>original: *Sulphur*</note>, or "burning spirit" <note>alcohol</note>. When this spirit is treated with refined ammonia <note>original: rectified *Spirit of Urine*</note>, it is entirely turned into a *volatile salt*. 

No example can be clearer or more convincing than this to show that these principles—or rather, several forms of the same substance—are interchangeable. An earthy or gummy stickiness is turned into a volatile spirit that is completely flammable, and this spirit is then turned into a real, pure *salt* that is not flammable. On the other hand, the transformation <note>original: Transmutation</note> of salt into oil is most evident in the distillation...

This is evident with *oil of turpentine* <note>original: Oyl of Terebinth</note>, *mace*, *nutmeg*, etc., as the *urine* will clearly smell of them for some hours after they are consumed.

Only the *alkalinity* <note>original: Alcalizateness</note> of the *salt* is neutralized <note>original: satiated</note> by the acidity of the stomach. Such is the difference between being carried along with digestible matter from one stage of digestion to another as a friendly stranger, and being formally absorbed into the substance of what is being digested. The former is the treatment given to a truly noble medicine <note>original: Medicament</note>; the latter is the treatment of food intended for nutrition <note>original: Nutriment</note>.

Many things worthy of knowing could be discovered concerning this subject, but the shortness of this present task will not permit me to dwell on it at length here. ...as to taste the virtue and efficacy of the *Great Circulate* <note>original: *Circulatum majus*; in Helmontian alchemy, this refers to a "superior" universal solvent believed to reduce substances to their first matter without destroying their medicinal essence</note>, which is the indivisible, great dissolving liquid.

> *My advice is (he says) not to weaken <note>original: castrate</note> those medicinal plants <note>original: Simples</note> which possess excellent and great virtues, but through Art to make them better and to advance them. This is done by suspending their toxicity <note>original: virulency</note> and by extracting their hidden qualities; by changing offensive qualities into others, which is achieved by introducing a ferment, or by adding some noble, powerful medium through which this may be effected.*

To illustrate this, let me remind the reader of what I discussed more fully before: that both the raw state <note>original: crudity</note> and the venom are gradually worn away by boiling <note>original: decoction</note> until, in the end, they become wholly extinct. This is much like how the ripples <note>original: Circles</note> in water made by a falling stone gradually vanish and at last disappear entirely.

However, we did not advise this boiling as the best method of preparation. This is partly because it leaves the gummy thickness <note>original: gummous viscosity</note> unconquered (which cannot be mastered except by a ferment that makes it volatile, or else separated by a proper chemical agent of sufficient strength for that purpose). But it is especially because *Fire* gradually weakens the specific constitution <note>original: *Crasis*; the unique internal balance or temperament of a substance</note> of whatever plant-based solid <note>original: Vegetable Concrete</note> is boiled by it. 

Therefore, the most desirable and truly philosophical way to perform this is by adding an *Agent* that is both penetrating and ferment-like. In this way, through a secret circulation and without any noticeable heat <note>original: sensible heat</note> that might alter it, the agent may perform this task triumphantly and perfectly—whereas mere boiling <note>original: decoction</note> does so lamely and imperfectly.

Such a means is to be sought for diligently, and high—

<summary>The author argues that medicinal plants should be enhanced through chemical "Art" rather than weakened, favoring the use of a "Great Circulate" solvent or ferments over simple boiling, which can destroy a plant's vital essence.</summary>

highly to be prized when found: such a medium <note>original: mean</note> is to be found in the world <note>original: Commonwealth</note> of *Alkalies*. Next to the *Grand Dissolvent* <note>original: the *Alkahest* or universal solvent in alchemy</note>, Nature has not provided a more excellent subject that can more accurately perform what the Artist seeks—if it falls into the hands of a man of intellect <note>original: mental Man</note>, and not of a bungling *sciolist* <note>term: sciolist; a person who pretends to be knowledgeable but has only a superficial understanding of a subject</note>.

I gave a hint just now, in the previous chapter, that *Alkalies* would simultaneously ripen raw materials <note>original: Crudities</note>, separate gummy textures <note>original: Gummousness</note>, and neutralize <note>original: correct</note> toxins <note>original: Venom</note>. In doing so, I was merely judging the lion by its claw <note>original: *Ex ungue Leonem*</note>, measuring *Hercules* by his foot, or guessing at the bravery of *Apollo* by his strangling of the serpent while he was still an infant in his cradle. I did not intend it as a full measure of the power <note>original: Vertue</note> of *Alkalies*, but as a trial <note>original: Essay</note> of what might be expected from them if they are guided to their full maturity by an industrious and prudent Artist. If they do so much in their "infancy," what may be expected from them in the future?

For visual <note>original: ocular</note> proof of what I mentioned before, look first at how raw qualities <note>original: Crudity</note> are matured and ripened by *Alkalies*. It cannot be denied that rawness is the cause of decay <note>original: Corruption</note> in any perishable thing. This is so dominant in plants <note>original: Vegetals</note> that if they are moist and compressed, they will begin to heat up within a few hours—a preliminary sign of following rot <note>original: putrefaction</note>. If they are dry and left in the open air, they lose their potency <note>original: virtue</note> in a few years, and some in a few months. If they are moistened, they soon decay, rot, stink, and breed worms, etc. This rawness is only partially removed by simple boiling <note>original: solitary Decoction</note>; for boiled meat, herbs, or seeds will grow sour, decay, and stink if left to stand for a time, although not as quickly as if they were raw or unboiled.

<header>Pyrotechny Asserted</header>

But by means of an *alkali*, this rawness <note>original: crudity</note> is removed. Just as *myrrh* and other aromatic spices were used for the embalming of bodies in *Egypt* to preserve them from the decay <note>original: corruption</note> common to all dead bodies (whether of humans or beasts) for many years—even for many ages—so *alkalies* preserve *plants* <note>original: vegetals</note> from both fermentation and decay.

It is true that substances <note>original: species</note> prepared in this way still undergo a continual internal ripening <note>original: maturative</note> process. Through this, they strive daily toward greater perfection until they reach the state of an essential salt, which concludes this motion. However, this happens without any transformative fermentation or rot-based decay <note>original: putrefactive corruption</note>. Thus, plants prepared in this way are in a daily state of progression toward perfection, yet they retain their original and specific healing powers <note>original: pristine virtues</note>. These powers are only enhanced and refined <note>original: graduated</note>, rather than being corrupted or destroyed, as happens in all other chemical transformations.

Secondly, the separation of gummy, earthy matter <note>original: gummous terrestriety</note> is most evident in this example: dissolve *opium* in clear water or in *alcohol* <note>original: Spirit of Wine</note> (it makes no difference here). Pour it off or filter it perfectly so that it is very clear and transparent. Then, add an equal amount of a strong *solution of tartar* <note>original: Lixivium of Tartar; a solution of potassium carbonate</note>. Immediately, accompanied by a strong smell like urine, you will see a separation—not of a small, trivial amount, but a large quantity of a resin-like curd, similar to what happens when mixing *wine* with warm milk. Let it sit in a simmering <note>original: simpering</note> heat until this thickened mass <note>original: coagulum</note> has gathered together. Then, filter it again, and you will find a resinous (or rather gummy) substance. It will resemble *aloes* in its color and how it breaks; it will be bitter and have a numbing <note>original: stupefying</note> effect. This same process occurs in all other solid botanical extracts <note>original: concretes</note>, such as *wormwood*, *rue*, *blessed thistle* <note>original: Carduus</note>, and others; only

Let the solution and infusion be as saturated with the *medicinal plant* <note>original: Simple</note> as the liquid will allow to dissolve. Can any man desire a clearer proof or demonstration?

*Thirdly*, regarding the toxicity <note>original: Venome</note> of *plants* <note>original: Vegetals</note>, I said enough in the previous chapter: namely, that there is no *plant* so fierce, so poisonous, or so deadly that—if boiled with a suitable amount of an *alkali* added to the liquid—its harmfulness cannot be entirely corrected. Although in some cases a few unpleasant symptoms may remain, these are the kind that time <note>original: Age</note> will eventually and entirely erase and overcome.

And here, I think I see some *novice* <note>original: Tyrocinist; a beginner in a profession</note> triumphing like *Archimedes* crying, "I have found it! I have found it!" <note>original: *Heureka, Heureka*</note>. To this person, I shall briefly address my words with truth and caution.

First, then, I say for the encouragement of all young, conscientious *practitioners* that this simple, seemingly trivial preparation far exceeds the entire confused collection of preparations that adorn the *Galenists’* <note>Followers of the traditional medical system of Galen, which relied on balancing "humors" rather than chemical extraction</note> method, and that by many degrees. It is superior because by this means both the raw qualities <note>original: Crudities</note> are mastered, the gummy residues are separated, and the toxicity <note>original: Venom</note> is extinguished. Thus, substances that were once sticky <note>original: viscous</note> and dangerously poisonous become processed <note>original: digested</note>, salt-like <note>original: saline</note>, and friendly to human nature and to the *Archeus* <note>a Paracelsian term for the "internal chemist" or vital force governing the stomach and body</note> of the stomach. Therefore (until a better method reveals itself), I shall confidently advise this as a safe path for all beginners who seek truth more than mere agreement with any person, whoever they may be.

# Pyrotechny Asserted

But nonetheless, let those who will not knowingly deceive them believe me: this hasty method of preparation does not fully satisfy the practitioner's goal. On the contrary, it falls short in many ways. While ***alkalies*** and ***solid plant matter*** <note>original: Concrete Vegetables</note> mix easily when boiled, they do not quickly unite "radically"—that is, they do not penetrate each other’s core. I shall make this clear to your eyes with a simple demonstration. 

For if ***opium***, or ***aloes***, or any other ***solid substance*** is ***boiled into an extract*** <note>original: Elixated; the process of boiling down to create an elixir</note> using an ***alkali***, and filtered ever so carefully, then placed into a bottle, you will find the sides and bottom of the glass fouled with a sticky gumminess and residue within a few weeks. This is a clear sign that the gummy nature of the plant was not totally overcome or separated by that quick method of ***extraction*** <note>original: Elixation</note>. 

Furthermore, the ***alkali*** retains its original caustic, soapy taste <note>original: lixiviateness; referring to the sharp, salty taste of lye or alkaline salts</note>. It will not lose this until a long time has passed, as the substances work on each other until both finally crystallize into a ***neutral salt***. This salt is very different from the sharp, caustic taste of the original ***alkali***. Until that happens, you should expect some troublesome side effects when using these preparations. These are partly due to the ***alkali***, whose corrosive nature is somewhat harmful to the stomach, and partly due to the ***solid matter***, whose ***ultimate life*** <note>original: Vita ultima; the fundamental essence or vital force of a substance in alchemy</note> has not been fully controlled by this simple preparation. It is no wonder, then, if some harmful qualities—the ***thistles*** and ***thorns*** <note>original: Tribuli and Spinæ</note> of ***plant substances***—appear when applied as medicine.

Recognizing these problems and finding that a long period of heating <term>digestion: a process of slow, gentle heating used to mature a chemical mixture</term> was the only remedy, the Chemist <note>original: Art</note> carefully sought a way to shorten this tedious waiting period,

<summary>The author warns that quick chemical extractions often fail to fully bond the alkali with the plant matter, resulting in gummy residues and caustic medicines. and performed it by clever <note>original: ingenuous</note> ways.

For liquid things attain their maturity through a long process of time (as seen in the making and ripening of noble <note>original: Generous</note> wines and strong beer). They also require the activation of an acidic ***ferment***, which causes a working ***ebullition*** <term>ebullition: a boiling or bubbling movement caused by chemical reaction</term>. Once this bubbling ceases, a more secret, still ferment works invisibly and imperceptibly. This process perfectly matures what the active, boiling ferment only began and did only in part. Thus, after a long time, the wines become noble, sparkling, lively, and healing <note>original: Balſamical</note>.

But ***alkalies*** are absolutely opposed to all acidities, and so no such fermentation can be expected in them. For this reason, liquids prepared using alkalies reach their highest potency much more slowly—unless that time is shortened by the chemist's skill <note>original: Art's Industry</note>.

Truly, the mystery of true ***pyrotechny*** <term>pyrotechny: the art of using fire in chemical or alchemical processes</term> is to "redeem time"—and man has no more precious jewel than time. To perform this is not easy for a conceited <term>putationer</term> <note>original: Putationer; one who relies on mere suppositions or guesses (from Latin *putare*)</note> or a perverse <term>sciolist</term> <note>original: Sciolist; a person with only a superficial, amateurish knowledge of a subject</note>. 

Consider, then, Nature in her daily operations. Observe how, through the cycles of heat and cold, night and day, moisture and dryness, she brings the hardest iron and steel, the long-lasting brass, and the durable marble to a spontaneous breakdown <note>original: Corruption</note>. She does this by means of the air and the "fire of nature," which is the power of fermentation. Consider how ferments are suited to their own specific environments, and where a closed or an open digestion <note>digestion: the process of softening or breaking down a substance through gentle heat</note> is required. A true "Son of Art" <note>a traditional term for a dedicated student of alchemy</note> must know the proper use of both ***air*** and ***fire***, ***dryness*** and ***moisture***, ***cold*** and ***heat***. This is true ***spagiry*** <term>spagiry: the alchemical art of separating and recombining substances to create medicines</term>; other curiosities are merely idle nonsense <note>original: Rapſodies</note>.

Alkalies, therefore, are to be corrected them—

# Pyrotechny Asserted

However, let those people believe me—for I will not knowingly deceive them—that this premature <note>original: precocious</note> method of preparation does not fully satisfy the chemist's <note>original: Artist's</note> desire. On the contrary, it falls short in many ways. For *alkalis* and *solid plant matter* <note>original: Concrete Vegetables</note>, though they mix easily when boiled down <note>original: in decoction</note>, do not unite fundamentally <note>original: radically</note> very quickly. That is to say, they do not penetrate into one another's core <note>original: Center or Profundity</note>. I will make this clear to the eyes with a simple demonstration:

If you extract an elixir <note>original: Elixated</note> from *opium*, *aloes*, or any other solid substance using an *alkali*, filter it as carefully as possible, and put it in a bottle, within a few weeks you will find the sides and bottom of the glass fouled with a *viscous gumminess* and *sediment* <note>original: Residence</note>. This is a very clear sign that the gummy quality was not totally overcome or separated by that quick method of extraction. Furthermore, the *alkali* retains its original caustic, lye-like taste <note>original: lixiviateness</note>. It will not lose this until a long time has passed and they have reacted with one another. Eventually, both will crystallize into a neutral salt <note>original: Neuter Salt</note>, which is very different from the sharp, caustic taste of the original *alkali*. Until that happens, you should expect some troublesome symptoms to accompany these preparations. 

These symptoms are caused partly by the *alkali*, whose corrosive, caustic nature is somewhat harmful to the stomach, and partly by the solid substance. Because its *ultimate life* <note>original: Vita ultima; the essential or innermost life force of a substance</note> was not fully overcome by this superficial <note>original: trivial</note> preparation, it is no wonder that some offensive qualities—which are the *thorns* <note>original: Tribuli; literally caltrops or thorns</note> and *spines* <note>original: Spinæ</note> of plant materials—appear when they are used as medicine.

Recognizing these inconveniences, and finding that a long, slow heating <note>original: tedious digestion</note> was the only remedy for them, the chemical Art studiously sought a way to shorten this tiresome waiting period,

# and Illustrated.

...and performed it through skillful <note>original: ingenuous</note> methods.

For liquid substances reach their maturity over a long period of time—as seen in the production and ripening of fine <note>original: Generous</note> wines and strong beer. They also require the stimulation of an acid ferment, which causes an active bubbling <note>original: Ebullition</note>. Once that stops, a more hidden and quiet ferment continues to work invisibly. This process perfectly ripens what the initial boiling ferment only started and only partially completed. Thus, after a long time, the wines become rich, sparkling, lively, and healing <note>original: Balsamical</note>.

But *alkalis* <note>original: Alcalies</note> are completely incompatible with all acids. Therefore, no such fermentation can be expected from them. Because of this, liquids prepared using alkalis take a much longer, more tedious time to reach their full strength <note>original: energy</note>, unless that time is shortened by the skill and industry of the chemist <note>original: Art's Industry</note>.

Truly, this is the secret <note>original: Mystery</note> of true *Pyrotechny* <note>term: The art of using fire in chemistry or alchemy</note>: to save <note>original: redeem</note> time—and humanity has no jewel more precious than time. Accomplishing this is not easy for a conceited, shallow thinker <note>original: Putationer</note> or a stubborn pseudo-intellectual <note>original: Sciolist</note>. Consider Nature’s daily operations: how through the cycles of heat and cold, night and day, moisture and dryness, she causes the hardest iron and steel, the enduring brass, and the durable marble to decay naturally. She does this through the air and the "fire of nature," which is the power of fermentation <note>original: fermental vertue</note>. Consider how specific ferments belong in specific places, and when a sealed versus an open digestion <note>term: A process of slow heating used to soften or decompose substances</note> is required. A true "Son of Art" <note>note: A dedicated practitioner of alchemy or chemistry</note> must know the proper use of both *air* and *fire*, *dryness* and *moisture*, and *cold* and *heat*. This is true *Spagiry* <note>term: The alchemical process of separating and then recombining the components of a substance</note>; all other oddities are merely worthless fragments <note>original: idle Rapsodies</note>.

Alkalis, therefore, must be refined <note>original: corrected</note> themselves,

# Pyrotechny Asserted

...themselves, so that they may improve <note>original: correct</note> other simple plant medicines <note>original: Vegetable Simples</note>, and be raised up <note>original: exalted</note> in their own natures. In this way, they can draw all other extracts <note>original: Tinctures</note> up to their own level of excellence.

In their simple, stable <note>original: fixed</note> state, they have a corrosive <note>original: caustick</note>, fiery, and lye-like <note>original: lixiviate; refers to the salts extracted from wood ashes</note> quality. This quality must be removed, and the essential healing power <note>original: seminal balsamick vertue</note>—which they lack—must be added. Furthermore, their solid physical form <note>original: fixed corporiety</note> must be overcome so that they can become volatile <note>term: Capable of evaporating or turning into vapor; in alchemy, this often meant making a substance more spiritually active</note>.

It should be noted here that there is a great difference between *alkalis* <note>original: Alcalies</note> that are naturally volatile and those that have been made volatile <note>original: volatized</note>. It is just like the difference between something that *can* be distilled or refined <note>original: sublimable</note> and something that already *has* been distilled or refined. The first has the capacity to be changed; the second has already undergone the transformation.

*Alkalis* that have been distilled or turned into a *Spirit* <note>term: A highly concentrated, refined essence produced by distillation</note> have been raised to the highest level of excellence intended for them by nature. It is this *Spirit* that the famous chemist *Helmont* <note>original: Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644), a pioneer of chemical medicine</note> referred to when he said, *“Whatever this cannot achieve, nothing in the world can achieve.”*

This *Spirit* can be reached through various methods, some resulting in more strength and effectiveness than others. If you wish to be a master of noble medicines <note>original: Medicaments</note>, you should study how to obtain this *Spirit* above all else, except perhaps for the "Great Solvent" <note>term: Likely the Alkahest, a legendary universal solvent sought by alchemists to break substances down into their primary elements</note>.

Now, *alkalis* are made volatile in two ways: through *alcoholization* <note>original: Alcoolization; the process of grinding a substance into a fine powder or saturating it with spirits</note> and through the making of an elixir <note>original: Elixeration</note>. Please forgive me, kind reader, if I use these technical terms. Without them, no art can be discussed, and they cannot be translated into plain *English* without a long and tedious explanation <note>original: Periphrasis</note>.

*Alcoholization* is a process of feeding, soaking <note>original: imbibing</note>, or circulating a stable <note>original: fixt</note> *alkali* with a volatile *Spirit* until

...until both are made into one, a neutral product arising between them that is distinct from each parent substance.

And under this heading, there are three distinct kinds of <term>Spirits</term>: <term>Acetous</term> <note>original: vinegar-based</note>, <term>Urinous</term> <note>original: derived from urine</note>, and <term>Vinous</term> <note>original: wine-based</note>. There are as many species of these as there are varieties of <term>essential</term> and <term>distilled oils</term>.

I shall only provide a brief essay on these here, reserving a fuller treatment of them for a more convenient place.

Of all the mentioned methods of <term>operation</term>, the one performed with a <term>vinegar-based alcohol</term> <note>original: acetous Alcool</note> is the easiest. This is because there is such a contrast <note>original: contrariety</note> between an <term>alkali</term> and an <term>acid spirit</term> that they do not meet without a tumultuous <term>boiling</term> <note>original: ebullition; a vigorous bubbling or fizzing reaction caused by the release of gas</note>. This boiling gradually stops, and when adding more <term>spirit</term> causes no further <term>boiling</term>, it is a sign that the <term>alkali</term> is saturated <note>original: satiated</note>.

By means of these <term>spirits</term>, the fixed <term>alkali</term> loses its fiery corrosiveness and is made volatile. An expert <term>distiller</term> will find this process completely finished through repeated <term>cohobation</term> <note>term: The process of repeatedly distilling a liquid from the same solid residue to concentrate or "fix" its properties</note>, which might better be called <term>imbibition</term> <note>term: The act of a solid substance soaking up or absorbing a liquid</note>. For even though that <term>alkali</term> would take in no more of the <term>spirit</term> (as all <term>boiling</term> ceased upon mixing), if it is instead mingled with burnt <term>clay</term> and distilled in the manner of <term>spirit of salt</term> <note>original: hydrochloric acid</note> or <term>nitre</term> <note>original: potassium nitrate or saltpeter</note> until all that can be distilled is drawn off from the <term>dead head</term> <note>original: Caput mortuum; the useless residue left in the flask after the active ingredients have been distilled away</note>, then you may soak it again with a new <term>alcohol</term>. This will allow it to take in new <term>spirits</term>; do this until it is a second time...

varied, and then distill it with a very strong fire. Finally, repeat this operation until all the salt is brought over with the spirit; this will then be a most noble *Alkalized Spirit* <note>original: Spirit Alcalizate; a substance where a solid alkali has been transformed into a liquid, volatile state</note>. This may be done with *Spirit of Vitriol* <note>original: sulfuric acid</note>, *Spirit of Nitre* <note>original: nitric acid</note>, *Spirit of Salt* <note>original: hydrochloric acid</note>, *Spirit of Vinegar*, etc.; or, in short, with any acid *spirit*. But concerning the many excellent preparations that may be made using *alkalis*, it is not necessary for them to be actually distilled, as that is a more tedious work. It is sufficient that they are only made volatile—that is, soaked in a *spirit*—until a neutral *salt* is produced between the spirit and the *alkali*. After the *tasteless phlegm* <note>original: insipid Flegm; the watery, inactive portion of a substance</note> is discarded, join this with the corrected *tincture* of any *vegetable* substance and digest both together until they crystallize in the form of a *Tinctured Salt*, which possesses the *essential composition* <note>original: Crasis; the unique constitutional temperament or mixture of components</note> of the *original substance* <note>original: Concrete</note>.

Furthermore, the *alkali* itself, when mixed with acid *spirits* such as *vitriol*, *salt*, *nitre*, *sulfur*, or the like, yields noble cleansing <note>original: abstersive</note> and diuretic medicines. One such medicine—commonly known by the name *Tartarum Vitriolatum* <note>original: potassium sulfate, used as a laxative and diuretic</note>—is a pleasant, sweet medicine if it is made correctly. To make it truly, take pure *Salt of Tartar* <note>original: potassium carbonate</note> burned by itself <note>original: calcined per se</note> in a potter's furnace, and pour on good *Spirit of Vitriol* for as long as it continues to bubble, then dry it. In a dose of 10, 15, or 20 grains repeated each morning, it cleanses the *stomach* and powerfully dissolves obstructions in the *mesenteric vessels* <note>original: Mesaraicks; the veins or vessels of the mesentery, once thought to be central to digestion and health</note>. It is also profitably given as a cleansing agent in *fevers*, *agues* <note>original: periodic fevers, such as malaria</note>, *jaundice*, *scurvy* <note>original: Scorbute</note>, and *worms* in children, as well as against cold *undigested matter* <note>original: Crudities</note> in the stomach that causes indigestion and many similar cases.

With *nitric acid* <note>original: Spirit of Nitre</note> a similar salt is obtained, but one that melts more easily, having a cool, salty taste. It is an excellent *cleansing agent* <note>original: abstersive</note> for burning and putrid *fevers*, *kidney stones* <note>original: Gravel</note>, *painful urination* <note>original: Heat of Urine</note>, and acidity in the *stomach* (commonly called *heartburn*). It also treats heat in the *back* and *kidneys*, and heat in the *stomach* that causes hot and foul breath, along with many other ailments associated with the first and second stages of digestion <note>original: first and second digestions; an ancient medical theory where the stomach performs the 'first' digestion and the liver the 'second'</note>.

And so, with *sulfuric acid* <note>original: Spirit of Sulphur</note>, *hydrochloric acid* <note>original: Spirit of Salt</note>, and others, very noble *preparations* may be made. However, they are much more effective if the purified *tinctures* of noble *plants* <note>original: Vegetals</note> (their harmful properties being first corrected) are united with this *salt* in a proper proportion, with the *salt* being dissolved in a suitable *liquid*. Digest the *salt* and *tincture* together until both become very clear. At the first mixing they will be muddy and will drop a light *sediment* <note>original: Fæcula; the dregs or starch-like matter that settles from a liquid</note>, but eventually, they will become very clear. Pour off this clear, infused *liquid* and, using a slow fire, draw away the moisture until the "sign" appears <note>likely referring to a surface film indicating the solution is ready to crystallize</note>. In a cool place, it will *crystallize* into a pure *salt* infused with the true tincture of the *plant*, retaining its taste, smell, and medicinal power.

Thus, from *Hellebore* (black or white), *Opium*, or any other *herb* <note>original: Simple; a single medicinal herb used on its own</note> that will yield a *tincture* in *alcohol* <note>original: Spirit of Wine</note>, a *salt* may be made. This is then called by the name of the *plant* with which it is joined, such as *Salt of Hellebore*, *of Opium*, *of Jalap* <note>original: Zalap; a Mexican plant used as a powerful purgative</note>, etc. These *salts*, besides the *cleansing* power of the base salt, have a specific added purpose according to the nature of the *original substance* <note>original: Concrete</note>. By using these, a diligent and industrious *physician* may, with God’s blessing, cure many deplorable diseases.

But this way of making *salts* is inferior to other *preparations*, which I shall discuss in order.

Therefore, these acids must be changed by the stomach into its own nature—and how difficult that is, let the philosophers judge. 

For the acidity of the stomach to *neutralize* <note>original: satiate</note> the *alkaline nature* <note>original: lixiviateneß</note> of an *alkali* is no hard matter, provided the *alkali* does not overpower it with too great a quantity. The opposition between alkalinity and all acids is so visible to the eye that the superfluous acidity of the stomach may neutralize a suitable dose of an *alkali* (which it receives) without trouble or harm to itself. This can be done daily with very good success where the acidity of the stomach is too abundant. The *alkali* thus neutralized becomes mild and, by tempering itself with the remainder of the acidic *chyle* <note>the milky fluid of digested food</note>, may pass to the second digestion <note>in early medicine, the "second digestion" occurred in the liver to create blood</note>, where it takes on a *salty nature* <note>original: saline habit</note>. For one acid to act upon another acid is as improbable as for one alkali to work upon another. To imagine that the stomach’s natural acidity could endure an unneutralized mineral acid is to believe that Nature lacks discretion. This is a warning to those who tamper too much with acidic—especially corrosive—**spirits**. They must be sure to administer them only at a time when there is *waste to be cleansed from the primary vessels* <note>original Latin: "Fæces abstergendæ in primis vasibus"</note>, such as around the mouth of the stomach, the root of the tongue, the throat, etc. Furthermore, they should give them in an appropriate dose and not continue their use for too long, unless the dose is very small and carefully managed.

This is why the wise and noble *Helmont* <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644), a foundational chemist and physician</note>, in his treatise concerning the ***Tree of Life***, tells a memorable story of a man he advised to use the true, clear, and pure ***spirit of sulfur*** <note>original: Spirit of Sulphur; now known as sulfuric acid</note>. (He does not mean that tinted, low-quality, adulterated spirit—dishonestly made and wickedly sold to ignorant pharmacists by the rogue chemists who swarm almost everywhere these days.) He advised the man to take two drops of that spirit before or during each meal in his first drink. He warned him not to be so reckless as to exceed that dose, knowing well that such a daily amount was sufficient. Its acidity was enough to cleanse the accidental *residue* <note>original: Sordes; meaning filth or morbid matter</note> sticking to the stomach which hindered digestion. Furthermore, its sulfurous ***gas*** <note>Helmont coined the term "gas" to describe a vital, invisible vapor or spirit within substances</note> kept the food and drink from rotting.

By following this serious advice carefully, the patient lived to a very old age—specifically forty years after the advice was given, despite being fifty-eight years old at the time. Remarkably, in all those forty years, he was never troubled by even the slightest fever, even though he once broke his leg in a fall on the ice. Let the diligent reader consider this exam-

...principle, so as to profit by it. Consider then seriously your intention, and apply your remedies accordingly. If they are not as you would have them, you must by Art <note>Art refers to the chemical or alchemical process of refining and preparing substances</note> make them so, or confess yourself to be no Philosophers <note>original: Philosophers; in this context, the term refers to "natural philosophers" or practitioners of chemical medicine</note>.

All *acidities*, simply as acids, are corrosive and irritating to a greater or lesser degree (as evidenced by how *crab's eyes* <note>original: Crabs-eyes; calcium-rich stones found in the stomachs of crayfish, used as an antacid in early modern medicine</note> react even in the least acidic *white* or *Rhenish wine*). But no acidity in the world outside of the stomach is comparable to the *stomach's acidity*. This is a peerless substance, which varies in every different species of animal and is an inseparable companion of life.

Now, most *acidities* (indeed, all that have no poisonous qualities mixed in) help *digestion*, because they are cleansing <note>original: abstersive</note> and dissolve certain waste products <note>original: fæces; here referring to metabolic residue or "filth" obstructing the organs rather than excrement</note> which affect and afflict (that is, obstruct and hinder) the activity of the primary organs, regarding both the appetite and the digestive faculty.

There are also various kinds of *acidities*. Some are changed spontaneously by being dried, such as the tender leaves of grapes, young grapes, etc. Others are changed by digestion, such as the juice of lemons and oranges. Others change through a light operation on a specific object, such as *white wine* acting on *crab's eyes*. Therefore, experience has taught us to eat *lemons* and drink *white wine* with sugar; when the stomach acts upon these during *digestive processing* <note>original Greek-derived: pepantick digestion; referring to the "ripening" or concocting of food into nutrients</note>, they become cooling, cleansing, and diuretic.

But the acidity of vinegar—being a product of wine that has become overly sour or "fretted"—is more resistant to the stomach, specifically because it has been further removed from wine (which is a refresher of the stomach). Therefore, if it is made into a syrup with sugar, in no large

A certain dose causes *vomiting* in some people. However, for those with strong *constitutions* who eat heavy meats—such as *roast beef*, boiled meats, *souse* <note>original: Souse; pickled meat, typically pigs' feet or ears</note>, and so on—it serves as a wholesome sauce, sometimes sharpened <note>original: acu-ated; from the Latin "acuere," meaning to sharpen or intensify</note> with *mustard seed*. Note here that when large amounts of heavy meat or raw, green herbs are eaten with it, their bulk uses up all the acidity in the process of breaking them down <note>original: fretting</note>. This prepares them for the stomach’s natural digestive process <note>original: Stomachal Ferment</note>. The stomach then digests the acidity along with the food, as it is no longer acidic and thus does not interfere with the stomach’s own digestive acids.

As for *mineral acids* and other products created by the *reverberatory furnace* <note>original: Fire of Reverberation; a furnace that reflects heat back onto the substance being heated, allowing for very high temperatures</note> which are as burning as fire—specifically the *spirits* of *vitriol* <note>sulfuric acid</note>, *sulfur*, *sea salt* <note>hydrochloric acid</note>, *nitre* <note>nitric acid</note>, *rock salt* <note>original: Salt-gem</note>, and so on—those of mineral origin carry a well-deserved suspicion of *arsenical* poison <note>original: malignity</note>. Therefore, they must be used cautiously and with discretion. Otherwise, tainted *sulfur* and *vitriol* mixed with arsenic will not only fail to provide the expected medicinal help, but will also harm the patient, leading to the physician’s just and deserved humiliation <note>original: Confusion</note>.

Regarding the acid *spirits* of *nitre* or *sea salt*, these are the least suspected of dangerous toxicity <note>original: virulency</note>. Anyone wishing to use them honestly and successfully must pay close attention to both the dosage and how often the dose is repeated.

That concludes our discussion on the cleansing <note>original: abstersive</note> nature of *acid spirits*, along with some necessary warnings regarding their use. Now we shall add a bit concerning *alkalis* <note>original: Alcalies</note> and their corrosive, lye-like <note>original: lixiviate</note> nature. By examining both acids and alkalis as they exist on their own, we may learn to create a third, neutral product.
