product, yet sharing the cleansing <note>original: abstersive</note> nature of either parent.

**Alkalis**, we say then, are of a cleansing nature like salts, but harmful to the **stomach** when they are **lixiviate** <note>original: lixiviate; salts extracted from wood ashes using lye</note> or **calcineous** <note>original: calcineous; derived from lime or created through intense heating/calcination</note>. The reason for this is obvious even to an untrained eye: there is a clear contrariety (if you will pardon the expression) between lixiviate and acid qualities. If both are present in a high degree, they resist each other even to the point of an actual burning <note>original: flagration</note>. This is seen when slaking **quicklime** <note>calcium oxide</note> with water, and also in the mixture of strong **Oil of Vitriol** <note>sulfuric acid</note> with a well-calcined **Salt of Tartar** <note>potassium carbonate</note>. 

However, even the smallest amount of perceptible acidity cannot meet with the slightest actual or potential **lixiviate alkali** without an immediate, tumultuous resistance occurring between them. This is apparent in the reaction between any **alkalized salt** and **vinegar**. It is also seen in the reaction between the weakest vinegar or wine and powdered **crabs-eyes** <note>original: Crabs-eyes; small, stony concretions of calcium carbonate found in the stomachs of crayfish, used historically as a common antacid</note>. There is no end to this contest until either the acid or the lixiviate quality (or both) is mortified—that is, saturated and extinguished—unless the two opposite substances meet in such a way that one overpowers the other, forcing it to submit.

Hence it follows that where the ferment of the **stomach** is strong enough—and we have shown more than once that this ferment is an acidity—the use of alkalis must necessarily weaken both the appetite and the digestive faculties while they are being taken. In a very weak **stomach**, this weakening may even cause digestion to stop for a time, which is an effect that is neither commendable nor desirable.

Therefore, it follows that the application of **alkalis** in their proper nature is only excusable when there is a superfluous sourness <note>original: Acor; a Latin term for acidity or sharp sourness</note> in the **stomach**.

Otherwise it must cause an alienation (at the least), if not a total ruin <note>original: pessundation; from the Latin *pessundare*, meaning to ruin or destroy</note> of the appetite and digestion, which are both stimulated and performed by acidity—to which an **alkali** in its nature is fundamentally opposed <note>original: contradistinct</note>.

So then, although we do not deny that both **alkalis** and **acid spirits** are cleansing <note>original: abstersive</note> and commendable medicines <note>original: medicaments</note> in their proper place (applied in appropriate cases with due caution and deliberate judgment, regarding both timing and dosage), yet because there are many cases in which both of them may be improper and do actual harm—the one due to its corrosive acidity, the other due to its harsh, caustic <note>original: lixiviate caustick</note> hostility—we affirm and lay down as undeniable these few positions concerning them:

**First**, that the virtue found in these aforementioned salts or spirits does not consist in their corrosive or caustic qualities; rather, these are only the marks <note>original: impressions</note> of the fire upon the substances, and they may be removed without doing any harm to the spirits or salts.

**Second**, that the operations they perform which are medicinal—specifically for opening obstructions—are performed far more powerfully when they are sweetened <note>original: dulcified; the process of neutralizing or removing the corrosive "sting" of a chemical</note> than while they retain their bitterness or sharp astringency <note>original: ponticity</note>.

**Third**, that since both the **acidities** and the **alkalis** are products of extreme fire, neither of them possesses any vital essence <note>original: seminal crasis; the natural, life-giving constitution or "mixture" of a substance</note>. Instead, the volatile acidity acts as a mineral sourness <note>original: acetousness</note> (attained only by the extreme heat of a reverberating furnace <note>original: reverberation; a furnace where the flame is reflected off the roof onto the material</note>), and the fixed alkaline salt acts as a salt purified by the violent action of fire.

It is barely saturated <note>original: satiated</note> and not distilled; it possesses only the cleansing <note>original: abstersive</note> and medicinal properties of the *alkali* and *spirit of nitre* <note>nitric acid</note>, or *vitriol* <note>sulfuric acid</note>, and so on. This is many degrees less refined <note>original: noble</note> than the other method. Therefore, when applied to *plants* <note>original: Vegetals</note>, the preparation made with oils turned into elixirs or with a pure spirit of wine (which is entirely *volatile sulfur*) is far more refined, effective, and capable of penetrating the body for medicinal use than the mineral-based one. The reason is clear: plant-based spirits have a closer natural relationship to the plants they treat. In contrast, those made from *mineral spirits* are as naturally different from plants as the minerals themselves (from which they were extracted by the operation of the *fire*) are different from the plant kingdom.

But *alkalis*, *essential oils*, and flammable *spirits* are fundamentally related <note>original: radically of kin</note> to one another. Through them, the *alkali* recovers what it lost during the burning process: a life-giving, vital, and essential balm <note>original: Balsom</note>. Consequently, it becomes not only volatile but also capable of fermentation and extremely compatible with <note>original: sociable to</note> our human nature. It serves as an excellent way to prepare and enhance refined plants, especially those that are fragrant, balsamic, and light <note>original: ethereal</note>.

Before I move on, I shall answer two objections which critical <note>original: captious</note> people might raise: one claiming my teachings oppose those of the noble *Van Helmont* <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644), a highly influential Flemish physician and chemist</note>, and the other claiming I am contradicting myself.

Regarding the first, they will object to Helmont's doctrine, which states that *volatile spirits* (such as those of wine, vinegar, etc.) are *fixed* <note>made solid or stable</note> by means of fixed salts; whereas I claim that the salts are made volatile by their *spirits*.

To this I shall answer that both are true. When the *spirit* is applied to the *alkali*, it is robbed of its salty <note>original: saline</note> parts and rejects the rest as a tasteless, watery residue <note>original: flegm; phlegm, the term for the inactive, watery portion of a distilled substance</note>. Thus, the *spirit* (specifically its salty part...

...of it) fixed compared to what it was. Yet it is not so fixed that fire cannot cause it to distill over into a receiving vessel <note>original: Recipient</note>, which a fixed alkali <note>original: Alcaly</note> alone would never do. Thus, the alkali is made more volatile, and the *spirit* is more fixed than before.

And therefore *Helmont* <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644), a pioneering chemist and physician whom the author frequently cites.</note>, speaking of this operation in his treatise concerning the *Duelich* <note>original: *Duelich*, a term Helmont used for bladder stones or the disease of "the stone."</note>, says, *that a spirit acting on a body in a corrosive way is in a manner fixed* <note>original: *Quodammodo fixatur*</note>. It becomes so fixed that it can withstand much greater heat than it could before. Thus, *Spirit of Wine* <note>alcohol or ethanol</note>, which was extremely volatile—evaporating <note>original: to flye</note> with the slightest heat—becomes (in its saline part) so fixed that it will not evaporate except in heat equal to the distillation of *Aqua Fortis* <note>nitric acid</note>. This may (and quite rightly) be called a "fixation."

But besides this, there is a great mystery in these operations, which I will address more conveniently when answering the second objection. I will raise that objection now so that the answer may fully satisfy any earnest <note>original: Ingenuous</note> reader.

The objection comes from those who would catch me in a contradiction <note>original: oppose me to myself</note>. First, they point out that I say the spirit of volatile alkalis is not acidic, but rather the opposite of acidity; yet, in another place, I claim that a volatile spirit of *tartar* <note>potassium bitartrate, often found in wine casks</note> is acidic, just as all spirits extracted by fire are. And second, that in my first treatise, titled *NATURE'S EXPLICATION* <note>George Starkey's 1657 work *Natures Explication and Helmont's Vindication*</note>, I wrote about alkalis turned into elixirs <note>original: Elixerated</note> by *essential oils*, claiming they were the slowest in terms of medicinal power <note>original: Vertue</note> and effectiveness of all preparations, by

...which alkalis are volatilized <note>original: volatized</note>, whereas in this treatise <note>original: Tractate</note> I affirm that alkalis—by elixiration <note>The process of turning a substance into an elixir by combining it with a solvent or oil.</note> with oils, or by reduction to a *Samech* <note>A chemical preparation where a salt, usually salt of tartar, is combined with a spirit until it reaches a neutral, "soapy" state.</note> by rectified spirits—are the most noble regarding vegetable <note>original: Vegetal</note> preparations.

To which objection, in both its parts, I answer as I did to the former: that both are very true. It only requires the careful reader to consider in what respect one or the other may be affirmed.

To answer the last part of the objection first: I say still that *Salt of Tartar* (if elixerated with an *Essential Oil*) becomes a very noble medicine <note>original: Medicament</note>. However, regarding its virtue as an *active dissolving solvent* <note>original: Menstrue</note>, it is the most sluggish of all others, according to Helmont’s <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644), a Dutch chemist and physician.</note> most true observation: "Among salts, I have found those to be more sluggish which followed the lineage of sulfurs" <note>original: *Ex salibus illa languidiora reperi, quæ sequebantur Sulphurum præsapiam.*</note>. Thus, *Spirit of Wine* <note>alcohol</note> is not nearly as strong a dissolving solvent as *Spirit of Vinegar*, especially for metallic <note>original: Metalline</note> bodies; and it is not at all comparable to *Aqua Fortis* <note>nitric acid</note>, *Spirit of Nitre*, *Oil of Vitriol* <note>sulfuric acid</note>, or the like. It is one thing to be a solvent for metallic bodies, and a far different thing to be a noble medicine to volatilize and elevate <note>original: exalt</note> vegetable tinctures. These tinctures require a fermental elevation of their nature much more than they need a corrosive sharpness to open their bodies; each, then, is of use in its own way and kind.

But besides, the question concerns alkalis that are purified <note>original: edulcorated</note> and made volatile, not actually volatilized; and here we must give the prize <note>original: yield the Garland</note> to elixerated salts, especially to those which are elevated into a *Samech*. This is because they have their seminal, balsamic <note>original: Balmick</note> virtue restored to them, of which the others are deprived by the burning of the fire, and not re-

When these meet with vegetable <note>original: Vegetal</note> tinctures, they act as ferments to each other, advancing one another into a true essential balsam <note>original: Balsom; a healing ointment or aromatic medicinal preparation.</note>, which possesses wonderful virtue <note>In this context, "virtue" refers to medicinal power or efficacy.</note>.

Regarding the acidity of some *alkalized spirits* <note>original: Alcalizate Spirits</note> and the *lack of acidity* in others, the difference lies in their preparation and the operations performed upon them. Depending on the methods used, various products appear and are revealed. For the *philosopher* <note>In the 17th century, a "philosopher" often referred to a "natural philosopher" or chemist/alchemist.</note> is an instrumental helper and co-operator with *Nature*, and *Fire* is an instrumental aid to the *philosopher*.

Happy is the *philosopher* who can make his preparations such that a gentle heat causes the *alkalis* to vaporize <note>original: flye; meaning to become volatile or sublime.</note>; he (without exaggeration) may command *Nature’s* choicest secrets. But if he is forced to use extreme heat, that heat will leave a fiery impression on the resulting spirit. Thus, the objections are answered.

However, to satisfy the studious reader, I will add that the *spirit* obtained by using *pontic spirits* <note>original: Pontique Spirits; sharp, sour, or astringent substances.</note> will be acid and astringent. Conversely, those obtained through *essential oils*—which are *vegetable sulfurs*—and *rectified spirits of wine*—which are merely *sulfurs* in disguise (as proven by their flammability)—will not be acid. Therefore, Helmont <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644), a foundational figure in chemistry.</note>, in categorizing the spirits of all salts, concludes they are all acid, *except for alkalized salts and the spirits of essential sulfurs in vegetables, etc.* <note>original: Exceptis Alcalizatis, & Sulphurum essentialium in Vegetabilibus, &c.</note>

Now to proceed to the operations on salts by means of essential oils and alcoholized <note>original: Alcoolizate; highly rectified or pure.</note> spirit of wine, so that I may draw to a conclusion of this discourse, having first fully satisfied the studious reader.

I join the process of <term>elixiration</term> <note>The process of turning a substance into an elixir.</note> with oils together with the making of it into a <term>Samech</term> <note>A term used by Paracelsus and van Helmont to describe a salt, particularly Salt of Tartar, prepared as a medicinal solvent.</note> with spirits, as they are of much the same nature, and the one is convertible into the other by the artist's craft and industry.

For essential oils and flammable spirits <note>original: burning Spirits; i.e., alcohol.</note> are but the same thing in a various disguise, and both of them are hard to be reconciled with fixed salts <note>Salts that do not evaporate when heated.</note>.

Concerning essential oils and salts, [Jan Baptista van] Helmont says expressly and truly, *that if they be joined without any water, within three months' time, by a hidden and secret circulation* <note>original: occulta & secreta circulatione</note>, *they will all be turned into a volatile salt.* And of spirit of wine he says, *that Salt of Tartar by its mere touch turns one half of it into water* (robbing it of its volatile saltiness and fixing that quality upon itself with a kind of fixity). But in both cases, there is something sufficiently obscure and not easily to be understood. For first of all, in the elixiration of oils and <term>alkalis</term> <note>original: Alcalies; basic (non-acidic) chemical compounds.</note>, the <term>weight</term> <note>original: pondus; the specific proportion or ratio of ingredients.</note> is not set down, and the time is tedious (namely, 3 months). If a man should wait that long and fail, he has no comfort but to think that he erred in Helmont's "hidden and secret" digestion <note>In chemistry, digestion is the process of exposing a substance to gentle, continuous heat.</note>, which will prove (like the alchemist's measure of coals) but a cold comfort. So also in the operation on Salt of Tartar with the most highly <term>dephlegmated</term> <note>original: deflegmed; purified by removing all water (phlegm).</note> spirit of wine: it is true that the salt by its mere touch will so transmute it, but how to make them touch is the difficulty. For pure salt in pure spirit of wine sinks to the bottom like sand in clear and common water, nor will they touch each other

—that is, to be mingled one with the other. No wonder, then, if several superficial scholars <note>original: Sciolists; those who claim knowledge they do not truly possess.</note> have complained of the tediousness of this operation, saying that in sixty rectifications <note>The process of refining a liquid by repeated distillation.</note> they could not perfect the *Balsamum Samech* <note>A medicinal balsam made from Salt of Tartar and spirit of wine.</note>.

Whereas if the spirit is already perfectly dehydrated, it does not touch the salt at all; nor will repeated distillations rob it of even one ounce, much less half an ounce, because there is no union—no, not even a mixture. And where that is missing, there can be no action or reaction <note>original: Action nor Passion; philosophical terms for the agent acting and the subject being acted upon.</note>.

And here my tired alchemists <note>original: Cole-burners; a term for those who worked at the furnaces, often implying they lacked theoretical understanding.</note> are in a labyrinth, because they do not know the means of conjunction. This is not any additional substance added to the matter, but only a *method of arrangement* <note>original: modus dispositionis</note>; if any man is ignorant of this, let him remain ignorant.

Fie, foolish pretenders to that in which you are but bunglers! Do you not see the heavenly influences, and how by a medium they visit and make fruitful earthly bodies? For shame, learn to imitate Nature in her most ordinary operations.

*Alkalis* then are to be joined with essential oils, so that both may make a soap <note>original: Sapo</note> together. Then, in time, by a secret fermenting decoction <note>A process of heating or boiling to extract essence.</note>, they will transmute each other into a third, neutral substance made of both, which is a volatile elixir. In the same way, *alkalis* and pure spirits are to be joined together, so that one may seem to eat up the other; then this touch will be like the clutches of a thief whose hands are bonds. Otherwise, there is no mixture, no union, and consequently no operation of one upon the other. (**Note well** <note>original: N. B.; Nota Bene</note>.)

And indeed this, and this only, is the *Gordian*— <note>The "Gordian Knot," a metaphor for an extremely difficult or intricate problem.</note>

This is the knot that puzzles, and forever will puzzle, those doting, foolish alchemists. They do not know the true means to reconcile extremes <note>The author refers to the difficulty of joining "oily" and "watery" substances, which were seen as chemical opposites.</note>, but instead imagine new exotic substances. To Him be all honor, glory, and everlasting blessing.

O foolish **OPERATORS**! You think that by your artificial heats you can draw down celestial influences and introduce ferments (the true parents of all forms), yet you do not know how to use any of your heats to imitate the sun in **Bermuda** <note>Bermuda was famous in the 17th century for its warm climate and citrus fruits.</note> when it produces oranges or lemons. Perform this dissolution with spirit of wine <note>Alcohol.</note>, which will certainly work (do not doubt it) once the oil and the salt are radically and inseparably united.

Pay close attention and have a little more patience, as I will proceed even further with the honesty I have already begun to show.

If they do not rise as easily as you wish, soak them <note>original: "imbibe them"</note> and dry them through a slow digestion as before using a suitable liquid—for example, good *wine* or *spirits*. The mixture feeds on this liquid and grows more volatile and spiritual with each circulation <note>The process of repeatedly distilling and condensing a liquid to purify it.</note> until, at last, it will evaporate <note>original: "sublime"</note> in a gentle sand-bath <note>original: "heat of Sand"</note> in the form of a glorified salt. At that point, the plant kingdom <note>original: "Kingdom of the Vegetables"</note> contains no more noble medicine.

To create *Samech* <note>A medicinal salt, specifically potassium tartrate, highly valued in the school of Paracelsus and Van Helmont.</note>, join pure *spirit of wine* <note>High-proof alcohol or ethanol.</note> and pure *salt of tartar* <note>Potassium carbonate, often derived from the crust left in wine barrels.</note>. Add nothing else except true spiritual love—which is the celestial fire, the secret fire, and the only true trigger for the fermentation. Digest these together, and in a few days most of the spirit will be absorbed into the salt. Carefully and discreetly separate the phlegm <note>The watery, inactive residue left after distillation.</note> and replace the spirit with as much as was lost (you may add four times the weight of the salt in spirit each time). After four or five repetitions <note>original: "Reiterations"</note>—or six, if you prefer—you will have a balsam colored like a pure ruby, with a pleasant taste and smell. This is a noble medicine, called by [Jan Baptista van] *Helmont* the *Balsamus*, or the *Secret of Samech* <note>original: "Arcanum Samech"</note>.

For a mineral advancement of these preparations, freely take this honest instruction: You may read it in the works of Helmont <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644), a famous chemist and physician</note> and find it confirmed here: that a sulfur <note>in alchemy, "sulfur" refers to the combustible, "soul" principle of a substance</note> may be extracted out of iron <note>original: ♂, the symbol for Mars or iron</note>, the "masculine metal" <note>original: *Metallus masculus*</note> (which, parenthetically, I tell you is zinc), and likewise out of lead and tin. But there is a natural sulfur, known by the name of *Sulphur Vive* <note>original Latin: "living sulfur," or native sulfur found in its pure state</note>, which was the <term>divine fire</term> <note>original Greek: <term>Θεῖον πῦρ</term> (theion pyr)</note> to Hippocrates. It is a noble, pure sulfur, entirely flammable without any dregs <note>original: *Fæces*</note>; consequently, it carries little danger of an impure mixture. Such impurities—especially the most malignant, arsenic—are common in ordinary, commercially sold brimstone <note>common sulfur</note>.

Regarding the medicinal excellence found in mineral sulfurs (which are more familiar to us than true metallic sulfurs), any philosopher must acknowledge it. My time and space here will not permit me to philosophically discuss <note>original: "ventilate"</note> it; however, in my next part of *Pyrotechny*—which shall be titled *Its Victory and Triumph*—I shall reveal ten most secret mysteries. 

The first shall concern the mysteries of the microcosm <note>the human body viewed as a miniature reflection of the universe</note>; the second shall reveal the mysteries of alkalis; the third, of sulfurs; the fourth, of mercury <note>original: ☿</note>; the fifth, of mercury <note>the symbol ☿ is repeated in the original text</note>; the sixth, of copper <note>original: ♀, the symbol for Venus or copper</note>; the seventh, of lead <note>original: ♄, Saturn</note> and tin <note>original: ♃, Jupiter</note>; the eighth, of iron <note>original: ♂, Mars</note>; the ninth, of the masculine metal <note>zinc</note>; and the tenth, of gold <note>original: ☉, the Sun</note> and its sister, silver <note>original: ☽, the Moon</note>. [The process] concludes thus, that these two ways will so easily and profitably combine <note>original: "marry"</note> with each other to prepare, advance, and spiritualize noble **vegetable tinctures** <note>extracts of medicinal plants</note> which are of excellent effectiveness. Fourthly, the tree is not much unlike the Cedar and is a wood of long duration if kept dry.

From this, it may be gathered that the oil—besides its **diuretic and healing qualities**—also promises, or at least gives hope (based on probable conjectures), for long life and the improvement of one’s constitution. It brings the body to a youthful <note>original: "virid"; meaning green, fresh, or blooming</note>, flourishing state of strength and vigor, and protects <note>original: "vindicating"</note> it from the cold, declining winter of wasting old age.

Use this process for the **Elixir of Propriety** <note>original: *Elixir Proprietatis*; a famous medicinal compound originally attributed to Paracelsus</note> as a rule for preparing any **tincture**, whether of black or white **hellebore**. But for the mixing of specific ingredients <note>original: "commixture of Species"</note>, follow my formula: 

**Hellebore** is remarkably effective for the **spleen** <note>original: *Splenetical*</note> and the **head** <note>original: *Cephalical*</note>. Prepare it with **wild ginger roots** <note>original: *Asarum-roots*</note> and **jalap** <note>original: *Zalap*; a purgative root</note>, and sometimes with **opium**, which I call the **Cephalic and Splenetic Elixir of Ladanum** <note>original: *Elixir Ladani Cephalicum & Spleneticum*</note>.

For an effective **sweat-inducing medicine** <note>original: *Diaphoretick*</note>, use **snake-root**, **saffron**, and **opium**. For an effective **diuretic** <note>medicine to encourage urination</note>, use **satyrion** <note>a type of orchid; it is from this that Paracelsus’s and Helmont’s *Aroph*—a medicinal salt—is made—note this as an aside</note>, **rhubarb**, and **saffron**. 

Against a **constipated disposition** <note>original: "costive temper"</note>, use **bitter apple**, **aloe**, and **balsam of Peru**. Against a **cough** and **diarrhea** <note>original: *Flux*</note>, use **opium**, **caranna resin**, and **gamboge** <note>original: *Gummi Gutta*; a pigmented tree resin used as a drastic purgative</note>. You may vary these combinations as your reason directs you, preparing them all in the manner of the previously taught **Elixir of Propriety**.

As for the **refining into powder** <note>original: *Alcoolization*; the process of reducing a substance to an impalpable powder or highly rectifying a spirit</note> of **alkalies** with purely distilled **spirit of urine**, and its mixture or combination with a **wine-based spirit** <note>original: *Vinous Spirit*</note> in this operation, I could easily launch into a long discussion. However, I shall reserve that for the triumphant part of my *Pyrotechny* <note>meaning his "art of fire" or chemical works</note> which treats the mysteries of the **human body** <note>original: *Microcosm*</note>.

Only in the next and last chapter I shall briefly discuss the Spirit of Urine <note>An ammonia-based solution distilled from fermented urine, highly valued by early chemists for its reactive properties.</note> reduced to a sweet, volatile salt. This is the substance used to create Helmont’s **Essence of Copper** <note>original: *Ens Veneris*; a medicinal preparation popularized by Jan Baptist van Helmont, typically used to treat rickets and fevers.</note>. I include this so that you (noble reader) may not lack such a noble remedy of such great and excellent use. You may read about this in more detail in Helmont’s treatise titled *Butler* <note>Referring to an account by Van Helmont regarding a mysterious healer named Butler who used "drifs" or medicinal stones.</note>. In my next and final chapter, I shall speak of its use and virtues as much as is necessary for the information and instruction of a studious and diligent young **student** <note>original: *Artist* or *Tyro*</note>.

I had intended to conclude this **Treatise** in this chapter and add a brief closing on the manner of using and applying those remedies whose foundations and methods of preparation I have already sufficiently revealed. However, a dear and intimate friend who had read and reviewed what I had written while it was still in my hands (having lent it to him for that purpose) was pleased, upon his review, to send me a clever and friendly letter. It contained several important **questions** <note>original: "Queries"</note> which he requested me to answer publicly for the sake of our mutual friendship. Judging this a reasonable request—and indeed, effectively a command—I thought I could not appropriately deny or refuse him. Therefore, I decided to omit the brief addition I intended for this section, as those topics will now be covered more largely and fully in the final chapter. I encourage the reader to look there for further satisfaction.

### THE
# Conclusion of this Treatise:

->BEING<-

*An Answer to a Friend’s Letter, containing some important Questions <note>original: "Queries"</note>, etc.*

->SIR,<-

Your kind and very learned letter I received and read carefully; in it, I am mindful of the obligation I owe you for the sincere <note>original: "Cordial"</note> love you expressed toward me. How I have deserved it, or could deserve it, I do not see, and I have no hope of being able to repay it. Yet, gratitude compels me to acknowledge what my lack of ability prevents me from repaying. Setting aside formality <note>original: "waving Ceremony"</note> in this acknowledgement, I come most willingly to answer those questions <note>original: "Queries"</note> contained in your letter. Please pardon me, Sir, if I do not answer them in the same order as you sent them, but rather in an order that best fits the structure <note>original: "Method"</note> of my discourse, which must be a summary explanation <note>original: "Explication"</note> of the whole art of **Pyrotechny** <term>Pyrotechny: Literally "fire-work"; the early modern term for chemistry or alchemy, focusing on the use of fire to transform and refine substances.</term>.

Your questions <note>original: "Questions"</note> then, Sir (as I understand them), are of two types: some are those in which you desire to be informed, and some are those in which, under the disguise of a question, you suggest to me partly what you judge to be for my own good, and partly what you believe either actually is, or probably may be, harmful <note>original: "pre-judicial"</note>

However, performing a **related** <note>original: "Collateral"</note> experiment of the same scope and **reasoning** is such an absolute confirmation of the other that no demonstration (short of actual proof) can be clearer. 

For example, in the second chapter, I speak of the preparation of **zinc** <note>original: "Zink"</note> by means of a volatile alkaline <note>original: "alcalizate"</note> salt, so that its **sulfur** <term>Sulphur: In early modern chemistry, this refers to the "oily" or combustible principle of a substance, believed to contain its color and medicinal properties.</term> may come over **colored** <note>original: "tincted"</note> and remain in the **spirit** <note>original: "Spirit"; a volatile liquid produced by distillation</note> under one form or color, etc. If I have actually tried this (as I indeed have) with **antimony** <note>The author uses the symbol ♁, which represents antimony.</note> and the **star-shaped metallic form of antimony** <term>stellate Regulus of Antimony: A purified form of the metal antimony that exhibits star-like crystal patterns on its surface when cooled.</term>, I may conclude the other process is undeniably true. It will not trouble me to be a little stung, since I shall do the **Commonwealth of Learning** <term>Commonwealth of Learning: The global community of scholars and the collective body of human knowledge.</term> a remarkable piece of service, even if I myself happen to smart a little.

But, *Sir*, you are sufficiently convinced that my present restraint is not on that account. For I am a *physician*, graduated as well as any who practice in town, and I have as much authority on my side for my practice, as much reason, and much more truth and experience on my side for what I plead against the common *Galenical* <term>Galenical Method: A traditional medical system based on the teachings of the Greek physician Galen, which relied on balancing the four "humors." The author, likely a chemical physician, opposed this approach.</term> method.

But my confinement for a time happens through the malice of a remarkable adversary. Considering his crooked disposition, which matches the frame of his body, that which once was said of the Emperor *Galba* in a similar case—**Galba's character is poorly housed** <note>original: "Ingenium Galbæ malè habitat"; a Roman saying regarding the Emperor Galba, who was physically deformed but considered to have a noble mind.</note>—may here be inverted. If there were ever a man whose mind and body did most exactly agree, he certainly is one. I might have been aware of him had I remembered the **adage** <note>original: "Adagy"</note>, **Always beware of those whom Nature has marked** <note>original: "Hos caveas semper, tu quos Natura notavit." This refers to the early modern belief in physiognomy, suggesting that physical deformities were outward signs of a warped character.</note>. 

However, my former familiarity with him gave him the opportunity for his future harassment of me. He prosecuted this with such earnestness, as if he accounted it a sufficient gain merely to be prejudicial to my peace of mind. What moved him to it (except for his own perverse disposition), even a day’s torture would scarcely force me to guess. Perhaps it might be a desire for future fame; if it were, I heartily wish that he may have his desire. If my writings be worth any future esteem, I am content that he live on through them. I do not doubt but that he will gain such a name as *Oporinus* <note>Johannes Oporinus (1507–1568) was a famous printer and once the secretary to the physician Paracelsus, whom he later famously maligned and betrayed.</note> got by his slandering and persecuting *Paracelsus*.

He is now considered a better gambler than a physician, and a better drunkard <note>original: "Carouser"</note> than a Christian. 

For this account, I owe you both a sigh and a tear. I could wish him to be a model not only of talent, but also of piety. 

Indeed, Sir, I must give an honest testimony of him: he is a man of significant abilities, and as for his hatred toward me, the world may judge that as they please. Among these *specifics*, it should be carefully noted that those of the most excellent quality (once produced) are the most tedious and secret to prepare. However, the discovery of one serves as a guide and a stepping stone toward finding others.

Having said this, to fully satisfy your question, allow me to tell you that there is nothing I have written about that I have not prepared and made with God’s help. Yet, I am not always supplied with every kind of these medicines, nor with the most superior types. I will give you satisfactory reasons for this before I leave this subject. As I told you before, in this search, the less superior medicines reveal themselves first to a studious researcher, and then the more powerful ones follow. However, even the less superior ones are powerful enough to treat acute diseases—namely *fevers, dysentery, pleurisy* <note>original: "Plurisies"; an inflammation of the lungs and chest.</note>, and so on. The substances causing these diseases, which lie in or stick to the primary vessels, are dissolved and removed by <term>cleansing</term> <note>original: "abstersive"</note> and <term>dissolving</term> <note>original: "resolutive"</note> remedies. These types of medicines are of the greatest practical use, even if they aren't the most powerful in scope. ...life, and actually destroy many people in both the city and the countryside.

Although many remedies can be and are made for these diseases, the most desirable ones are those that can be produced in large quantities and, once made, can be applied to many different ailments <note>original: "Griefs"</note>.

For this reason, although I know many specific remedies for *fevers*—which I have often made and used—when I find a *medicine* that is no more difficult to prepare but has a much wider range of healing power <note>original: "Vertue"</note>, I set aside the making of others. I content myself with that one medicine as long as it is effective, remembering the proverb <note>original: "Adagy"</note>: *It is pointless to use many things to do what can be done with fewer* <note>original: "Frustra fit per plura quod fieri potest per pauciora"</note>.

Once I understand how to prepare an *excellent medicine* <note>original: "noble Medicine"</note>, my primary focus through daily, repeated experiments is to see how I can improve the preparation. My goal is to make it less tedious and difficult to produce, or to ensure that the final product is more pleasant to take or more effective in its operation.

It is true that I perform many daily experiments on the <term>exaltation</term> <note>original: "Exaltation"; in early chemistry, this refers to raising a substance to a higher state of purity, potency, or "noble" quality.</note> of medications to give them a higher and more noble power. However, I primarily practice with those that I can most conveniently make in large quantities. I believe it is more honorable for a *physician* to be able to cure thousands of acute diseases every year than to focus entirely on preparing the most *noble medications*. Although those elite medicines will cure minor <note>original: "inferiour"</note> diseases, they do not need to be used when a simpler preparation will achieve the cure. Furthermore, a person cannot keep a sufficient supply of those rare medicines for all the suffering and impoverished people <note>original: "Objects of Pity and Charity"</note> who appear every year.

<sig>I</sig>

<summary>The author argues that a physician should prioritize medicines that are effective for many ailments and can be produced in large quantities. He emphasizes medical ethics, suggesting it is better to treat thousands with reliable remedies than to focus on rare, elite medicines that cannot serve the poor.</summary>
<keywords>medical ethics, pharmaceutical preparation, fevers, mass-produced medicine, physician's duty, alchemy, exaltation</keywords>

--- Page 200 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's explanation of his methodology. He moves from discussing why he focuses on broad-use medicines to explaining his personal circumstances—specifically, how his lack of facilities and recent time in confinement have shaped his experimental work.</meta>

<page-num>168</page-num>

### Pyrotechny Asserted

I grant that if a man were provided with the proper facilities <note>original: "Conveniency"</note> to follow both types of preparations, it would not be a mistake for him to stock himself with medications of all degrees. But my misfortune has been that I have always lacked convenient opportunity, being so limited in my facilities that I could only attend to a few experiments at a time. Those have usually been intended for the preparation of remedies that were of the most general use, even if not of the most excellent quality. 

Incidentally <note>original: "ever by the by"</note>, I have continued to pursue further and higher discoveries, several of which I have touched upon in my previous treatises. I usually perform these only in small quantities because tedious operations are always accompanied by risk. Nor is it easy for one who has done something in small quantities to immediately make it in larger proportions, because fire is an **irregular agent** <note>original: "Heteroclit Agent"; in this context, it refers to an element that is unpredictable or does not follow standard rules.</note>, nor is it tied to **geometrical rules of proportion**. <note>The author explains that doubling the ingredients in a chemical process does not always mean simply doubling the heat or time; scaling up chemical reactions was a major challenge in early "pyrotechny" or chemistry.</note>

And, Sir, this has been the true reason for my patient acceptance of ten months of confinement: so that I might **devote myself to experiments** <note>original: "vacare experimentis"</note>. This was rarely permitted to me while I was at liberty. For he who maintains a **medical practice** will find his time so taken up with a multitude of **visits made and received** <note>original: "active and passive Visits"</note> that it is impossible for him to attend to serious and tedious research. To commit the management of such things to a careless servant is so uncertain a course for success that it will soon make an artist <note>The author uses "Artist" to refer to a skilled practitioner of chemistry or alchemy.</note> weary of that method. During this time, then, I have had the opportunity to try very many things, and I have made it my business to reduce those **theoretical contemplations** and **conclusions** (which read—

<meta>catchword: ing</meta>

<summary>The author explains that limited facilities and his medical practice previously hindered his research, but a ten-month period of confinement allowed him to focus on experimental chemistry. He notes the difficulty of scaling up chemical processes due to the unpredictable nature of fire.</summary>

<keywords>chemical scaling, medicinal preparations, confinement, experimental method, pyrotechny, medical practice distractions</keywords>

--- Page 201 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's discussion on the practical limitations of preparing advanced medicines. He transitions from his personal circumstances to offering advice for aspiring "searchers of nature" who lack the specialized equipment or knowledge to create the most advanced chemical remedies.</meta>

<page-num>169</page-num>
<header>and Illustrated.</header>

[continu]ing and side experiments had suggested to me) into practice. However, to prepare any large quantities of the more difficult medicines is a work that I must postpone <note>original: "adjourn"</note> until I have better facilities <note>original: "Conveniency"</note>. I hope God will shortly provide me with these, or else I shall retire to some other place to find that opportunity which is denied to me here.

And so I come in order to your question: namely, what advice would I give someone who intends to devote himself to searching for nature’s mysteries but is not a master of the more **noble preparations**? <note>In alchemy and early chemistry, "noble" preparations were those considered most potent, pure, or transformative, such as universal medicines or "arcana."</note>

My advice and counsel to such a person is the same advice I follow myself, as I do not have the facilities to prepare such medications. I consider it to be the same thing (in terms of the result) to lack the noble remedies because of a lack of knowledge on how to prepare them, or because of a lack of the facilities to prepare them in sufficient quantities.

My counsel, I say, in such a case is the same as that of the noble Helmont <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644) was a pioneering chemist and physician whose work was highly influential in the 17th century.</note>: **"If the filth should stick in the primary passages, one must use cleansing and dissolving remedies, while nature safely manages the rest."** <note>original Latin: "Si sordes primis haeserint latebris, utendum abstersivis, & resolutivis, natura tuto satagente reliquum."</note> There are many of these **cleansing** <note>original: "abstersive"</note> remedies that will not fail a conscientious physician. Of this sort are **alkalis** <note>original: "Alcalies"</note> that have been **refined** <note>original: "alcoolited"; at the time, this meant reducing a substance to an extremely fine powder or a pure essence.</note> with **nitric acid** <note>original: "Spirit of Nitre"</note> or **sulfuric acid** <note>original: "Spirit of Vitriol"</note>, etc., or made into elixirs with oils or a wine-based spirit, which may be tailored to a specific purpose <note>original: "specificated"</note> with any vegetable as the chemist <note>original: "Artist"</note> pleases. These operations are not so tedious or difficult that large quantities of remedies cannot be made for the cure of many thousands every year.

But then he adds, **"If they should stick in a deeper retreat..."** <note>original Latin: "Si penitiori recessu haeserint..."</note>

<meta>catchword: to be taken [sumenda]</meta>

<summary>The author explains that when one lacks the resources to create complex "noble" medicines, they should rely on effective cleansing and dissolving remedies as recommended by the chemist Van Helmont. He argues that these simpler chemical preparations are practical to produce in large quantities and sufficient for many medical needs.</summary>
<keywords>Jan Baptist van Helmont, noble preparations, alkalis, Spirit of Nitre, Vitriol, abstersive remedies, chemical medicine, alchemy, medical facilities</keywords>

--- Page 202 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's practical medical advice for those who cannot create the most complex chemical remedies. He transitions from general cleansing principles to specific mineral and vegetable preparations recommended by Helmont.</meta>

<page-num>170</page-num>
<header>Pyrotechny Asserted</header>

"One should use volatile alkalis, which cleanse everything just like soap, etc." <note>original Latin: "sumenda sunt alcalia volatilia, quæ instar saponis cuncta abstergunt, &c."</note> In such cases, you should volatilize your alkalis and combine them with mineral sulfurs, and you shall achieve your goal.

However, in a physician's practice, hardly one disease in forty will absolutely require those highly refined remedies. Therefore, powerful and effective **cleansing agents** <note>original: "Abstersives"</note> may satisfy the needs of a diligent and careful doctor.

Now, besides the cleansing quality of some remedies, there are those which have a soothing <note>original: "parative"</note> disposition. As if by a charm, these allay the fury of the enraged **Archeus** <term>Archeus: In the medical theory of Jan Baptist van Helmont, the Archeus is the internal "vital spirit" or governing energy of the body that controls health; disease was often seen as the Archeus becoming "angry" or disturbed.</term>, bringing it to rest and quiet, and so erasing the angry "idea" impressed upon it. 

Of this sort, in the mineral family, I admire the **Essence of Copper** <note>original: "Ens of ♀"; the symbol ♀ represents Venus or copper</note>. This is prepared according to Helmont's directions in his treatise titled *Butler*. It is made using the volatile salt of urine, purified from its foul smell <note>original: "Fœtor"</note>. With this, the sweetened **colcothar** <term>colcothar: the crystalline residue or "dead head" left over after the distillation of vitriol</term> of copper sulfate <note>original: "Vitriol of Venus"</note> is sublimated two or three times. Together, they become a glorious, tinted substance—or rather a spirit. In a dose of five or six grains, it cures fevers, malaria <note>original: "Agues"</note>, pleurisy, and similar ailments, giving ease to all the disturbances of the enraged Archeus. Nor is the process of making it so tedious that it cannot be produced in large quantities and in a short amount of time.

In the vegetable family, the true preparation of **opium** is an excellent remedy. If it is prepared with the volatile elixir-like alkali of tartar <margin>*</margin> (especially by its **Samech** <term>Samech: a Paracelsian term for the essential salt of tartar, or potassium carbonate, used as a powerful solvent</term>), it is eminently **diuretic** <term>diuretic: a substance that promotes the production of urine</term> and **diaphoretic** <term>diaphoretic: a substance that induces sweating</term>. It eases all pain in the body and is an approved remedy for more than forty different diseases. By adding other **simples** <term>simples: individual medicinal herbs or minerals used as ingredients</term>, its effects are made even more noble, especially by the addition of myrrh...

<meta>catchword: Aloes</meta>

<summary>The author describes practical chemical remedies, focusing on the Essence of Copper and a specialized preparation of opium. These treatments are intended to calm the "Archeus" (the body's vital spirit) and cleanse the system of disease.</summary>
<keywords>alkalis, Archeus, Essence of Copper, Van Helmont, Colcothar, Opium, Samech, diuretic, diaphoretic, chemical medicine</keywords>

--- Page 203 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's response to a correspondent, promising to make specific chemical medicines available for purchase and detailing his plans for future publications on "Pyrotechny" (chemistry).</meta>

<page-num>171</page-num>
<header>and Illustrated.</header>

Aloes and Saffron, the basis of the *Elixir Proprietatis* <term>Elixir Proprietatis: A famous "Elixir of Propriety" or "Elixir of Life" traditionally made from aloes, saffron, and myrrh, popularized by Paracelsus and Helmont for its digestive and life-extending properties.</term>.

And so, Sir, I have come to your last question: why do I not do as Helmont did? "To put remedies up for sale" <note>original Latin: *Venalia remedia exponere*</note>, as he said of himself. You believe this to be a more convincing argument than writing large volumes of books. I grant you, Sir, that this is a very effective way of convincing the world of the reality of what I argue for in my writing. When a man—following the advice of the one who said, "Speak, so that I may see you" <note>original Latin: *Loquere ut te videam*</note>—produces experimental proof of what he writes, it is a powerful thing.

It is something that I have been planning for a long time. Sir, I intend to produce more generally useful medicines in large quantities, such as **Essence of Copper** <note>original: *Ens Veneris*</note>, **Tartar-based Soothing Medicine** <note>original: *Samech Nepenthes*; "Samech" was an alchemical term often used for the salt of tartar</note>, **Salt of Nepenthes**, and **Tartarized Elixir of Propriety** <note>original: *Samech Elixeris Proprietatis*</note>. I will also produce all sorts of **purified alkalis** <note>original: *Alcoolited Alcalies*</note>, along with **acidic**, **wine-based**, and **urine-based spirits**. 

I will provide brief and full written directions concerning their use, effectively summarizing my next book, *Pyrotechny Triumphing* <term>Pyrotechny: Literally "the art of fire," this was the common term for alchemical chemistry in the 17th century.</term>, and distributing these instructions in single sheets. As even nobler medicines can be made in quantity, I shall do the same with them, which you may confidently expect, God willing, this summer. 

And so, Sir, I hope I have satisfactorily replied to your kind and learned letter. I shall now take my leave of you, committing you to the protection of Him who alone is able to keep both you and me. My prayers are, and shall be, that He would make Truth increasingly glorious and cause Light to appear daily to discover those dark and rotten foundations upon which a pompous but unstable <note>original: "crazy"; in this era, the word meant fragile, full of cracks, or structurally unsound</note> building has been raised for many ages, to

the ruin of many families and lives, through the passive deception of the deluded schools <note>original: "Schools"; here Starkey refers to the traditional academic medical institutions that followed the teachings of Galen and Avicenna, which he believed were outdated and harmful compared to chemical medicine</note>, whom I beseech **GOD** in his own time to convince, so that they may love and embrace the truth, which until now they have hated and persecuted.
